Living For The Knife

by ofriviq

Summary

“You kissed me in Raccoon City. But you haven’t since. Do you ever think about doing it again?” He looks at her headily. Lips parted, cheeks flushed by the copious amounts of alcohol he’s ingested.

“My life doesn’t revolve around you,” she says. Maybe, when it comes down to it, that’s one of the biggest lies she’s ever told.

⟢ ──

A study of Ada Wong and Leon Kennedy, exploring the moments between missions and after goodbyes.

Title from Working For The Knife by Mitski

Chapter 1: PART ONE.

At the end of September 1998, Ada Wong and Leon S. Kennedy meet in Raccoon City

following an outbreak of the t-Virus.

⟡

December, 1998.

The hospital is playing Christmas music, so it must be December.

It’s hard for her to keep track of time when she’s just laying down, listening to the sound of whatever machines are keeping her alive—or just keeping watch. No one’s even told her what’s actually wrong with her, she just knows she hurts all over. And that she isn’t in the U.S. anymore.

She thinks she’s in Austria or Switzerland—probably somewhere near the border between the two. The doctors and nurses who treat her all talk in the same quiet, accented German, though they sometimes dip into French.

These are languages Ada Wong knows, but she’s too muddled to really listen. She thinks they keep her that way on purpose. Whatever this hospital—place—is, there’s probably no record of her being there at all. Really, she should be happy her employer cared enough to treat her. They could have left her for dead in the ruins of NEST.

She would have been fine with dying. Ada Wong doesn’t have much of a life to lose.

But then she thinks of Leon S. Kennedy, that stupid rookie cop who’d been all too easy to sway. Did he make it out? Or was his saviour complex the end of him.

She shouldn’t have kissed him.

That kind of emotional manipulation has always been a step too far, even for her—it had taken a surprising toll on her to pretend with John. But she’d felt strangely compelled to with Leon, as if he’d been manipulating her with his… With his stupid… Well, with himself.

She’s never met anyone like him before. If she has, she can’t remember it. If she has, it was before, and that means it doesn’t matter anymore.

It's thoughts of Leon Kennedy that keeps Ada from losing her mind in that hospital bed.

⟡

February, 1999.

Leon is twenty-one. And a half.

The half might seem arbitrary, but in his favourite foster family, they’d taken half-birthdays very seriously.

Monika said it was because she never knew how long a kid would be staying with her, and she wanted them to experience at least one good birthday if she had the chance. So she made half- birthdays count too.

Leon lived with her for nine months. She was the kindest out of all of them. When he’d arrived, just past lunch on a gloomy Monday morning, she’d immediately washed all his clothes to rid them of that garbage bag smell. Then she’d made him his favourite food and let him watch a grown-up movie with her and her husband, Luke.

Leon had been eleven then, and Monika and Luke were the best thing that had ever happened to him. They talked about adopting him, he knew that. Sometimes he’d hide in the doorway to his bedroom and listen as they mumbled to each other about making him their son.

He wanted it so bad he thought he’d burst.

But then Luke fell off a ladder and broke his back, and Monika couldn’t care for both of them.

Being dropped off at a military base where everyone thinks you’ve gotten some kind of special treatment is sort of like being the fourth kid to arrive at an already overcrowded foster home.

Everyone hates you. Everyone thinks you must have done something—right or wrong is irrelevant. Everyone looks away when you get close. Everyone refuses to let you in on anything.

You’re alone.

You’re alone, and there’s a mountain of grief weighing down on you. Everyone you’ve lost, people you know you’ll never see again. You mourn a past, even if it was kind of crappy. Somehow, the future always seems worse.

⟡

July, 2001.

It’s a scorching hot day when a picture of Leon S. Kennedy lands in her inbox.

She stares at it with a mix of emotions entirely unfamiliar. She’s startled by how relieved she is, first of all. It’s not like he’s been on her mind constantly, but often enough that she’s carried the weight of his possible death on her conscience.

But she’s also worried. It’s terrifying—to worry for a man she met once, three years ago, simply because he looks perhaps a little too different in a sort of grainy photograph.

It’s clearly Leon, though wider and stronger. He’s put on muscle and cut his hair. He’s in army fatigues, of all things, leaning against what looks like a metal shed. There’s a battered book in his hand that she can’t make out the title of.

There’s a line of text above the image.

Took some time. They have him training under Major Krauser for USSTRATCOM. Do you want details? - R.

Does she? She wants to know how Leon ended up working for the government—though already she has theories. But should she really pry like this?

Now that she knows he’s alive, isn’t it time to drop this interest in him and forget? His death is off her conscience, because he’s not dead at all. This photo should be it. The end.

But that mix of feelings… There’s something in there that tells her it isn’t.

⟡

In 2002, before the destruction of Umbrella’s Russian branch, Leon is sent to a small South

American country as part of a secret military detachment, aiming to prevent another

outbreak.

Meanwhile, Ada operates in South America as a liaison for a rival bio-weapons company, attempting to convince the Sacred Snakes cartel to abandon Umbrella as their supplier.

⟡

September, 2002.

Twenty-five is supposed to be the age everything makes sense, right? Isn’t that when your brain finishes developing and everything just falls into place? That’s what Leon thought, at least. But he’s turned twenty-five and then some, and everything is still just… confusing. Heavy. It’s all a mess.

Nightmares were a thing of the past, of his unstable childhood, but now they’re back, and it feels like everyday something new forces itself into the batch. It used to be Raccoon City—Claire and Sherry dying, Marvin dying, the contagion spreading, the U.S. crumbling, Leon unable to do anything about it. But now it’s the training, too. Boots on his neck and hands on his wrists and being pinned down and kicked. Major Krauser screaming in his face. The jungle. The deaths. The screams. The mutations.

Leon can’t remember the last time he had a good night's sleep. Probably circa 1998.

Yesterday, Collins got honourably discharged—he lost a leg in the jungle, but Leon knows he’s happy to simply be alive. They all are, more or less. Krauser, too, even though he’s grumpier than usual. Maybe because he was also discharged.

Shaking his major’s hand, though, and pretending he’s not jealous enough to tremble is hard on Leon.

That will never be him. He will never be discharged from the military. He will die in service of this government that doesn’t care for its people, whether he has four limbs or none. And even though it’s been four years since Raccoon City, he’s not really made peace with that yet.

But he thinks of Sherry. And Manuela. They were just kids and forced to endure terrible horrors because of Umbrella and its crazy affiliates. There’s no world where Leon doesn’t fight tooth and nail to stop that, even if he wishes he could quit every single day.

⟡

May, 2004.

The black kitten nuzzles into the palm of Ada’s gloved hand.

She glances around the alley, scanning for its mother. Unsurprisingly, she sees nothing.

The kitten is small, definitely underfed. Its fur is matted and dirty. It can’t move very well, but it’s so insistent on burrowing into her warm hand. Enough so that Ada pulls her glove off. Fleas or whatever be damned.

When morning comes, the little creature will probably be dead. It deserves some warmth. Some company and some love.

“I wish I could take you home,” she whispers to it, scratching behind a tiny ear. The little cat mreows in response. “But I don’t have a home. If I did, though…”

She feels herself smile at the thought.

“I’d name you… Tuan Tuan. Round and cute.”

The alley is dark around her. Ada’s only in there because she heard a noise and by nature is too paranoid to keep walking. Gun pulled, she’d stalked deeper into the narrow space, stepping over empty boxes and broken trash bags until she’d found the tiniest little kitten in the corner, curled up and terrified. Startled, probably, by the noise it made. Somehow, it sent the lid of a garbage can rolling into a wall.

Ada is sitting against that wall now, with the kitten against her chest.

“Or maybe Xiao Yu,” she mumbles. “After me.”

The kitten digs its claws into the thin fabric of Ada’s sweater, but it’s too weak to do anything except hold on.

“You’re brave, Xiao Yu,” she whispers to the kitten. “You’re smart. You made it this far, huh? You’re a real fighter.”

It peers up at her with dark eyes and meows.

“Yes,” Ada nods. “I mean every word.”

⟡

In 2004, President Graham’s daughter, Ashley, is abducted. With government agencies under

scrutiny due to a suspected mole, Leon is sent alone to investigate.

Meanwhile, after Ada uncovers Wesker’s secret dealings with TRICELL and Jack Krauser

fails to retrieve a Plaga sample in time, she is assigned to do what Krauser couldn’t.

⟡

November, 2004.

“Only twenty-seven, and one of the best in the country?” The hand on Leon’s shoulder is heavy, sticky with sweat and spilled champagne. The man’s breath smells like cigarettes. “Agent Kennedy, it’s an honour to meet you.”

Leon can’t remember his name. Or what his purpose is. He’s government, of course—everyone is. Doesn’t matter. The man is decidedly unlikable no matter what.

“Likewise, sir,” he says, because he doesn’t have a choice.

The party is in his honour, after all. Kindly arranged by the White House to properly thank him for saving Ashley Graham. It can’t be public, of course. No one outside this room knows what happened in Spain, and President Graham wants to keep it that way.

That’s okay. Leon knows this party isn’t for him at all. It’s just another way to bask in the glory of their skill and dominance. Once again, the American government has thwarted a threat of bioterrorism! The terrorists aimed high, but Leon easily, single-handedly, fought through hell for the love of his country and defeated them.

It’s propaganda, whispered from ear to ear. And Leon has a hand in it. He’s the one who said he did it all on his own. He’s the one who assured the president no one but him and Ashley left the island alive. He made no mentions of Ada Wong or Luis Serra in his debriefings or reports. He made sure Ashley knew to do the same.

“You’re an inspiration,” the man says with a laugh that’s more of an exhale.

It sends more of that cigarette smell wafting into Leon’s face. His gut clenches uncomfortably.

“Thank you, sir.”

They’re all so proud of him, but they have no idea who he is. What he’s gone through to get to this point.

They don’t know he killed his former major and that he hates himself for it. They don’t know how much blood he has on his hands. They don’t know he goes to sleep dreaming of a woman they all want to see dead. They don’t know he hates everything they represent.

“And Ms. Graham,” the man guffaws. “Brave. Beautiful.”

“Indeed, sir.” Leon shifts. His hand clenches around his sweating glass. He knows it’s not proper to grab the body, that he should grab the stem. But the army let him keep his hair, he’s sure they can let him get away with bad etiquette. Manners? What’s the difference anyway? They didn’t teach him that.

“A man like you, Kennedy, representing America and our values, even if it’s all very hush hush… A man like that should be a family man.”

“With all due respect, sir, I don’t think a family would appreciate me getting blown to bits in some European village.”

The man blinks at him then laughs again, as if Leon just said something hilarious.

“Oh, Kennedy!” he says, patting him on the shoulder again. “Image, son. You’re a hero now! A good ol’ American hero. There’s an image to consider.”

Leon shakes his head. “Luckily, that’s not my job.”

“You just have to live up to it,” the man winks.

“Excuse me,” Leon says, shifting until the man’s hand falls off his shoulder. “I’m getting something stronger.”

The man lets out another deep belly laugh. “Indeed, son! Celebrate!”

⟡

July, 2005.

Ada crushes the cigarette under her heel when Leon stumbles through the gates of the White House. Security is tight—it’s the Fourth of July after all. She can’t get much closer than she is, but she doubts she needs to be.

It’s like Leon has some kind of sixth sense when it comes to her. It should be creepy, but she finds it strangely… endearing.

So when he brushes off a driver and staggers out through security and across the road to her, she’s not even surprised.

“Lost your invite?” he asks, words only a little slurred.

“Same place you lost your wits, maybe,” she hums.

“What’r’you doing here?”

“The fireworks,” she gestures towards the White House. “Quite a spectacle.”

“They ended hours ago.”

“Had to stop for a smoke.”

“Since when do you smoke?”

She shrugs. “Don’t think it’s a habit I’ll keep up.”

He nods. “That’s good.”

“Should I walk you home?”

“Sure.”

He says nothing the entire walk back to his apartment, seemingly content to shuffle slowly beside Ada. At random points he mutters something under his breath, and it takes a while for her to realise it’s Spanish.

She bundles him into the elevator and presses the button for his floor. Hopefully no one saw them —for multiple reasons. Primarily because they must have looked ridiculous: her dragging a grown man behind her like a toddler.

With the door finally locked behind them, she points at the couch. “Lay down.”

“Am I a dog?” he asks, voice raspy.

But he looks at her with surprisingly clear eyes.

“You’re drunk,” she tells him. Curiously, he suddenly seems quite sober. Funny. Maybe the walk helped. “Aren’t you tired?”

“The fresh air was sobering.” His gaze falls to the floor. “Thank you.”

“I don’t need your thanks,” she says. “Do you want water?”

“Sure.”

Ada hears him sit down on the couch as she fills a glass for him in the kitchen. Leon accepts the water gratefully, and Ada sits on the floor by his coffee table. Not quite on the other side of it, but not right in front of him either. Certainly not on the couch next to him.

“Why are you here tonight?” he asks her then.

The million dollar question.

“I assumed you’d be at the White House. Guess I was bored.”

“You wanted to see me?”

“To cure my boredom.” She shakes her head. “It doesn’t matter. I think you should sleep.”

At once his face shifts into something lewd. “You’re free to join me.”

“I have a hotel room which I’m sure will prove more than adequate.”

Leon slumps into the cushions. “I’m not tired.”

“Leon.”

“Turn on the TV, at least.” He scans the space for a remote and clumsily leans forward to get it, only barely avoiding knocking over his glass. Ada tries to protest, but to no avail. The screen lights up, showing a sit-com re-run. “Just a little.”

“You watch this?” she asks, shifting, unable to pretend to not be interested in what’s on the TV. She so rarely watches anything.

Leon shrugs. “When I can’t sleep I watch something dumb. It helps me.”

“It bores you.”

His eyes narrow a little. “It soothes me.”

“It bores you out of your mind.”

He huffs. “Maybe. But so what? It’s not like I’m going to turn on some movie where a buff guy shoots some people and saves the day. That’s just a normal Tuesday to me.”

“A buff guy, huh?”

“The mirror doesn’t lie.”

Ada rolls her eyes, smiling. Hopefully he’ll forget that in the morning.

“Come on, let’s get you to bed, buff guy.”

Leon parts his lips, as if to speak. Hesitation hangs in the air for a short moment before he says, “Do you ever think about kissing me?”

Ada blinks. “Kissing you?”

“You kissed me in Raccoon City. But you haven’t since. Do you ever think about doing it again?” He looks at her headily. Lips parted, cheeks flushed by the copious amounts of alcohol he’s ingested.

“My life doesn’t revolve around you,” she says. Maybe, when it comes down to it, that’s one of the biggest lies she’s ever told.

“Not what I asked. At all.”

“You’re such an idiot,” Ada says, forcing a breathless laugh. She looks over her shoulder at the TV. “It’s time to sleep.”

“No,” he says. “You’re too interesting.”

She hears him sliding off the couch then inching closer to her on the floor.

“Not really—”

She makes the mistake of looking at him again; suddenly he looks so tired and weary. His bangs are plastered to his forehead in thick globs, and the smile he’s clearly forcing doesn’t reach his eyes. But the earnestness in them still stir a warm feeling in the pit of her stomach. Uncomfortable and intoxicating.

Quickly, she makes to stand up, but Leon is quicker, and he manages to grab her wrist before she can leave. His hand is warm and large on her. She hates how it makes her feel.

“You always leave me,” he says. “Why?”

“Because I have to,” she answers thinly and prays to every god and deity that he didn’t hear the way her breath caught when he grabbed her.

“I’m sorry,” he says, peering up at her.

“Leon—”

“I don’t know why you do what you do, Ada. But it can’t be all, can it? Do we really get nothing but endless missions and endless pain? Do we just break our backs for people who do harm? Forever? You deserve more than that! But—”

“But what?” she asks, struggling to keep up with his train of thought.

“But you’re like a machine! Do you believe in the work you do, or is it all you have?”

She refuses to answer such a stupid question. “Let me go. Go to bed.”

“Ada.” He tugs on her wrist, and she finds herself on her knees, a dull ache shooting through her legs. “Stay.”

“Why?” she demands.

When Leon exhales, she feels it on her face. They are so close—close enough that when she shifts just slightly, her knees brushes his. Close enough to smell whiskey and pine and fireworks. Close enough to stare into his eyes and be swept away by the current that swirls in them.

His tongue darts out to wet his lips before he speaks. “Because I want to kiss you.” That time he certainly must hear it: her breath hitching. He leans in even closer. “I think about Raccoon City all the time. How you kissed me. How I missed you, so bad, even after everything.”

“Leon.”

“You don’t have to,” he says. She looks at his lips. They’re shiny with his saliva, but she’s close enough to see how dry they are beneath it. How bitten and raw they are. “I let you go. You can leave.”

Ada is almost shocked to find that he’s right. There isn’t a hand wrapped around her wrist anymore. She can just walk away.

But when she doesn’t immediately stand, he threads a hand through her hair. The other cups her cheek. His hands are so gentle that she barely feels the weight of them. Like he’s afraid to touch her but desperate to do it all the same. The hand in her hair trembles as it runs through the short dark strands; with restraint, perhaps.

When his thumb comes to rest on her bottom lip, her breath hitches again—like he’d just pulled the pin from a hand grenade. If he moves his finger, she’ll explode and kill them both.

Ada doesn’t think she can remember the last time she felt like this. So… on the verge of something. So wanting. She wants this. Wants Leon. Her body wants him.

They stare at each other. No one speaks.

Then his thumb pushes into the heat of her mouth, and the sound he makes sparks the most dangerous of flames inside her.

She has to move. She has to flee.

Leon makes no effort to stop her when she pushes his hands off her. It both hurts and soothes. He knows it’s stupid, too.

“You need to sleep.” Ada barely recognises her own voice.

Leon sighs and leans back, taking the tense moment with him. She forces herself to feel relieved.

At length, he says, “Tuck me in?”

“I—” She sighs. “Fine.”

“And stay? Just a little? Until I’m asleep?”

She shifts awkwardly. She should say no. She should get him in bed and then run. Perhaps never see him again.

In defiance, she asks him, “Why not get a girlfriend?”

Immediately, his shoulders tense, and his gaze glaze over. “I don’t want a girlfriend.”

“I’m sure Claire Redfield would say yes.”

He glares at her. “Come on, Ada. Play fair.”

“Or Ashley Graham, maybe? Ingrid Hunnigan? You’ve got your pick of the litter, Kennedy.”

“Ada,” he snaps. “Don’t… Don’t do that.”

“What? Don’t think you deserve it?” She knows she’s being mean. Maybe one day Leon will thank her for it. “Your life doesn’t have to be just misery. You’re the government’s golden boy. Get a girlfriend. Knock her up. They’ll thank you for it.”

He bares his teeth at her. “Shut up.” His voice breaks on the words, his shoulders slouching inwards.

Right before her eyes, Leon is collapsing and crumbling like a sandcastle washed away by a wave. He can be so large, in presence and in body. He’s tall, broad shouldered, and years of grueling training had made him strong. People flock to him like sailors to a siren. When he smiles, when he talks, people listen, pulled to him like moths to a flame—but he still seems to think the world moves against him.

He doesn’t know how fucking good he has it.

Nice place in D.C., probably a sizable salary to match. He has freedom. He exists. So what if he has to work for the government to pay off a debt they imposed on him?

Ada hasn’t had a home for ten years.

But her heart aches, she realises. She pities the man before her. She wants to see him smile again.

“I’ll stay,” she says, voice thin. “A little while.”

His gaze slowly meets hers.

“Ada. You really don’t have to.”

She shrugs. “It’s fine. It’s not like I’ve got better places to be.” It is not fine. But Leon… The way he makes her feel… She doesn’t want to part with it quite yet. He had the most curious effect on her, unlike anything she’s ever experienced.

Leon moves with and without confidence at once. He’s certain in his movements, but wary of his gaze. He traverses the apartment like a gentle giant on unsteady legs. She watches him brush his teeth, and he watches her watching him.

A few moments later, she finds herself, strangely enough, beneath the blankets in his bed.

They lay facing each other.

She feels his breath on her face, minty and warm. He’s close, closer than he necessarily has to be, but she says nothing of it. She likes the heat he radiates.

“Can I tell you something?” he asks, voice barely above a whisper.

“Go ahead.”

“I dream about you. All the time. After Raccoon City… I don’t know if it was my brain trying to spare me or something. And I didn’t know you were alive. There’s this one dream I’d have… It kept me going through training.”

Ada’s cheeks heat up, but there’s nowhere to hide. She can only pray his eyes haven’t adjusted to the dark yet. But hers have; she sees him clearly despite the dim light.

“You remember it?” she asks.

“Only because I’ve thought of it almost every day since,” he admits without shame. “Especially since Spain.”

“You really dream about me?” It is, perhaps, not what she should ask. She should ask him to move further away, or to cover up his bare chest. But she’s too curious.

“I do,” he says.

“You’re insane,” she replies, though it lacks animosity.

“I think you’re a little insane too,” he says. “Why else would you be here?”

She can’t argue with that. “Tell me about your dream.”

He blinks. “What?”

“Please.”

He obeys easily enough. “We’re in bed, in the dark. In our home. And you touch me.”

“I touch you?”

She hears his movement before she feels his touch. His hand moves across the starchy fabric before his fingers wrap gently around her wrist, pulling it closer until she feels the warmth of his chest beneath her fingertips.

“You touch me,” he echoes. “And I touch you.”

Light as a feather, his fingers dance over the length of her arm, leaving goosebumps behind. He is braver than he was in Spain, where he only stared at her pleadingly and chose duty over her— again, though, she’s not very hurt by that—but here there’s no interruptions. No mission. He can do whatever he wants, really. She won’t stop him. Because beneath the covers, her body feels like it’s burning up.

“And?” she asks.

“And you keep touching me.”

When did he move closer? Ada hasn’t even noticed, too busy staring at his eyes. His lips. His jaw.

“How?”

“What?” He seems as shocked by her question as she is herself.

“How do I touch you? Do you touch me?”

His arm wraps around her waist pulling her body against his—and she feels all of him. Instinctively, her legs tangle with his, and she uses the leverage to pull herself flush with him, further into his warmth.

Blue eyes flare, and his grip on her tightens. “Of course I do,” he said. “Resisting you is the one thing I’ll never succeed at.”

You have done very well, she almost says.

But she pushes all those thoughts from her mind and digs her fingers into his chest. What is she doing, really? It’s wrong, so wrong. She should stop. She should push him away and flee. But she can’t. She doesn’t want to.

“And?” She wants more. She presses her heated forehead into his neck and exhales. Then inhales. Pine. Fireworks. Whiskey.

“And,” he says airily. “And I fuck you.”

He rolls his hips into hers, pulling an embarrassing noise from her lips.

“Leon.”

“While I was looking for Ashley—” he does it again, earning another choked moan from her, “— all I could think about was you.”

She feels his voice more than she hears it. Something deep and scratchy right by her ear.

“You’re insane, Leon.” She barely recognises her own voice.

“So what?”

She’s unsure how it happens, but suddenly she finds herself on her back with Leon above her, propped up on his forearms. His hips press against hers, and a fire roars in the pit of her stomach. She truly is just dry kindling, caught in his growing flames.

“You fuck me?” she pushes, desperate for more. For any scrap he’ll throw her. Too many years of solitude bubble to the surface, and suddenly she is desperate. A starving animal drinking from oil spills, eating roadkill.

“Yeah, Ada. I do,” Leon says into the skin of her neck. He’s kissing her there, she realises belatedly. His mouth leaves behind a hot, wet trail as he makes his way to the collar of the ratty T- shirt she’d put on to go pick him up.

“Do I like it?” she asks.

She doesn’t know what to do with her hands splayed awkwardly along her body. Tentatively, she touches his arm, feeling the muscle moving beneath his burning skin. God, he’s strong. The other hand disappears into his hair—to keep him in place when he suddenly bites down on the skin beneath her collarbone.

“Don’t ask stupid questions,” Leon answers, his words punctuated with another roll of his hips.

“Leon,” she says, half moaning his name.

“Can I make you come?”

Ada gasps at his words, a small little noise that she’s never heard herself make before.

“Leon—”

“Yes. Please.” She isn’t sure she ever has. She can’t remember. She ventures no, because she’s never spared a single thought for her body before. For its needs beyond food and water and sleep. November, 2005.

Leon is only twenty-eight, but he feels ancient. “Forty, at the youngest,” Leon says.

Except maybe Claire.

Not that… Not that loving an internationally wanted mercenary is the same as being a lesbian but…

In 2006, approximately one year after the collapse of WP Corporation, a bomb explodes at a

Before the death of Oswell E. Spencer, Ada crosses paths with Albert Wesker one final time.

August, 2006.

Smart used to mean a very different thing. It used to come whispered against her temple, paired with kisses and little treats. Now it’s almost always said in such contempt.

The man Ada has tied up at gunpoint in the centre of the room snarls at her, spit flying.

You think you’re smart, don’t you?

She fires a shot at the floor between his legs to shut him up. It doesn’t work.

So clever, he continues. His voice is gravelly, roughened by the kick he received to his windpipe earlier.

Ada wishes she wasn’t smart. If she was regular, maybe she’d be dead. No, she’d definitely be dead. Her brains are the only reason she’s alive, in more ways than one.

“Just give me the information I want,” she tells him flatly. “We’ll get you home safe and sound.”

“Dead, more like,” the man says.

“Doesn’t it sound peaceful?” she hums.

The man shakes his head, disappointed. “If you were actually smart, you’d let me go.”

She rolls her eyes. “Uhuh.”

“I can pay you. I can protect you.”

“The only one who’s any good at doing that is me.”

“I have connections. Power! I swear it! I know the president!”

Her arms steadies. She points the gun straight between his eyes. “You know a Leon S. Kennedy?”

The man nods. “Met him by the grace of Graham himself, back in ‘04.”

Ada asks before she can stop herself. “What is Kennedy working on?”

The man frowns. “He’s in… In Pittsburgh! Helping the FBI.”

Two days ago, she was assigned this mission. She knew then already that this man had to die, whether or not he gave the information her employer thought he had.

She lowers her arm, even reholsters the gun at her hip. “Can I tell you a story?” she asks, watching confusion settle into the lines in his face.

“About… About Agent Kennedy?”

Shaking her head, she says, “No. A story about me. One I’ll never be able to tell him. Even though I think I’d really like to.”

The man nods carefully.

“I am smart. I was always smart. If I’d grown up here in America, I’m sure they would have put me on the local news. But I grew up in rural China. My mother died giving birth to me, and my brother died in an accident when I was five. All I had was my father, but he was already old. Couldn’t work much after I turned… eight? It gets murky.” Ada turns her back to him, fidgeting with the tools in her bag, splayed open on a fold-up table. “My father took out a loan from an American loanshark.”

The man shifts in his chair. She listens as he struggles, clearly trying to escape his bonds. The man, whoever he is, certainly isn’t very clever at all.

Shortly after the Pittsburgh Incident, the White House is placed on alert after an unidentified

April, 2008.

The ice is melting slowly, diluting the colour of the expensive whiskey in his glass. Leon isn’t sure how long he’s been sitting there, just staring at it. But the bar is still loud around him, so it can’t be too late. The place is packed, the people on the dance floor are pressed together like sardines.

Leon doesn’t really know why he’s there. It’s never been his scene—this party stuff. Maybe it just feels a little less sad to drink surrounded by people, even though no one talks to him.

Some try. Both men and women.

They slide into his booth with liquor soft smiles and fluttering eyelashes, talking about how sad and lonely he looks. Doesn’t he want a friend? Someone to speak to, just for the night?

He tells all of them no. Calls a cab for the drunkest ones, makes sure they get away safely, that their friends know where they’ve gone. But never more than that. He never talks to anyone. Never gets anyone’s name or number.

But what that man told him—back in 2004 at that stupid party—about being a family man… It’s stuck with him. Oddly enough. Leon doesn’t want a family. He doesn’t want liabilities and responsibilities. He’s happy for the ones at work who do. He coos at baby pictures and smiles at milestones, though he knows that life isn’t for him.

Leon hasn’t seen that man in a while. He wonders for a moment what happened to him. Who he was.

It doesn’t matter.

He picks the glass up, swirling the amber liquid around. The sound of the remaining ice cubes bumping into each other is drowned by the booming music.

The whiskey tastes bitter going down, but he’s used to it. Maybe more than he’d prefer. But it’s alright. It’s not like he’s an alcoholic or anything.

He’s just about to stand when someone slides into his booth. A woman, he’s guessing by the shape of her body beneath a large coat, but she’s looking down enough that he can’t see her face.

Sighing, he places his palms flat on the sticky table. “I was just leaving,” he says.

The woman looks up.

Smiles a little.

It’s been almost three years since he saw Ada.

Almost three years since he took her to bed, ate her out, and then woke up to find her gone. He hadn’t even been hurt about it back then. But he hadn’t known how long he’d have to wait to see her again.

She leaves through the door. Leon doesn’t even try to kiss her. He doesn’t try anything. Just unlocks it and steps aside as she leaves.

When she emerges onto the street, the air is colder and more bitter than it was just minutes ago.

June, 2009.

There is little Leon hates more than these ridiculous meetings. The White House calls them once a year and just goes through the most boring PowerPoint presentation ever made. Photographs, often blurry and/or black and white, on a white background, paired with text in Calibri.

They’re going through a list of internationally wanted criminals and terrorists. Can’t they do that with a little flair?

Only the rookies are listening, actively taking notes. Leon is in the back, sketching out another gun on his notepad. Something big and powerful enough that the recoil could pull a shoulder out of its socket.

“Next up. Real name, unknown. Alias, Ada Wong, amongst others.”

He looks up.

Smiles, almost.

The woman at the front of the room drones on, listing Ada’s crimes, her affiliates—everything they know about her. It’s probably more than Leon does, but he tunes it out and studies the picture of her instead.

It looks recent. Her hair is longer than it was at Christmas, and her arm is in a sling across her chest. But the quality is horrible. It’s clearly just from a security camera somewhere—somewhere they missed out on grabbing her, taking her in.

That does make Leon smile.

If she’s wanted, she’s alive. If she’s wanted, she’s out there. Free.

“…and one of her earliest suspected crimes, killing known mob affiliate Nathan Wesninski.”

The person on Leon’s left shifts not-so-subtly, glancing at him.

They all know Wesninski killed Leon’s family.

Leon, however, did not know Ada killed Wesninski.

Maybe that should fill him with some kind of dread, a fear of his past haunting him. Déjà vu? What should you feel when you find out the woman you love killed the man who orphaned you?

Leon, at least, smiles wider. It’s like something in his chest eases, a knot of something horrible unraveling and slithering back into its hole. Wesninski is dead. Ada killed him.

On his right, Ingrid Hunnigan clicks her pen and laughs softly.

“You gonna send her a gift basket?” she asks in a low voice.

“Maybe,” Leon whispers back, still smiling. “You got her address?”

“Your friend isn’t doing too good, you know.”

“Who?” she asks.

“Kennedy,” Robert says. His hands shake when they lower the coffee cup back into its saucer. “He’s probably dipsomaniacal.”

Ada rolls her eyes. “Just say alcoholic like a normal person.”

She drums her nails against the iron-wrought table. “Why do you say that?”

“You pay me to watch him. I watch him.” He leans back. “He drinks a lot. At bars. At home. Maybe not a worrying amount, but enough to worry.”

She brushes off his contradictory statement. Robert is… a character, for sure. An original. Unique. But, God, is he good at watching.

“I’m sure he’s fine,” she says airily. “He’s a big boy.”

Ada gives him a flat look.

Leon has been depressed for years. He’s been drinking too much for years, too. It’s probably worrying that Robert finds it significant enough to mention it to her now, since she’s technically only paying him to tell her if Leon is alive or dead.

But Leon has friends. Co-workers at least. And a therapist, supplied kindly by the same government that fucked him up enough to need one.

Sometimes Ada wishes she could do therapy. Just to try it. She’s not used to feeling her emotions, much less talking about them.

“He will be fine,” she says, even though Robert doesn’t need assurance, nor does he care about Leon.

“If you say so,” Robert says.

She nods. “I do.”

January, 2011.

Leon is thirty-three and a half.

Is that old?

He feels old.

Everyone around him, however, loves reminding him how young he is. How accomplished. How lucky and inspiring.

Standing next to Adam Benford, shaking hand after hand after fucking hand, Leon’s not sure what he feels or is. A puppet built to smile and shake hands and pose in pictures.

The DSO is his idea. He’s happy enough to let Benford take the credit, but damnit, shouldn’t coming up with the thing give him some leverage? Why can’t he hide away in some dinky corner and drink from the open bar?

At least he has vacation coming up.

A hand large enough that Leon knows it belongs to Chris Redfield lands on his shoulder.

“Borrow you for a moment?” Chris says. He looks towards the president, right next to Leon. “Sir?” There’s still a long (unofficial, messy) line of men in suits waiting to shake Leon’s hand; he feels like he’s a fucking pop star or something. “Work,” Chris adds.

Immediately, Benford nods. “Go right ahead, gentlemen.”

Chris drags him off to the side, far enough away that people sort of naturally begin to avoid them. Frowning, Leon follows, unsure what could be important enough that it has to be dealt with in the middle of the party for his agency.

“What, Redfield?” he asks.

Chris throws a look over his shoulder, back towards Benford and the group of disappointed suits. “Just thought you needed some saving, is all,” he says.

Leon snorts. “My knight in shining armour.”

“Hey, just trying to help, man.” There’s a little smile on Chris’ lips. “You looked a little… out of it over there.”

“It’s a lot,” Leon says, hoping it doesn’t make him sound like too much of a wuss. “When they took me in after Raccoon City, I’m not sure I’d ever end up… here.”

Chris’ eyes fall to the floor—like it’s just as hard on him to talk about what happened as it is to Leon. Then again, it’s not like Chris was on a sunny beach while Leon was swimming through the sewers. He dealt with his own shit. But Leon stayed with Claire when Chris couldn’t. Claire was only there for Chris.

It’s a sore topic.

“Sorry,” Leon says quietly.

“No, no.” Chris shakes his head. “I should—I don’t know. I just wanted to give you a moment’s peace, that’s all.”

“Thank you.” He clears his throat, a little awkward.

“You should be proud of yourself, you know. The DSO, everything you did at STRATCOM. In Raccoon City.”

These sensitive, empathetic fucking Redfield siblings…

Leon waves over a waiter and snags a glass of champagne off his tray. He swallows it all in one go, the bubbles cutting like knives as it goes down.

“I should have stayed home,” he says roughly, staring into the bottom of his glass. “They told me not to come in, but I did anyway.”

“You saved Claire and Sherry.”

I met Ada.

“Claire didn’t need me. Neither did Sherry.”

“Well, the past is the past, Leon. There’s nothing to do about it now.”

“No,” he agrees. “But I should have stayed home.”

⟡

In February 2011, the U.S. begins investigating the use of bio-weapons by rebel forces in the Eastern Slav Republic's ongoing civil war. Leon is ordered out of his vacation to investigate.

⟡

May, 2011.

It’s a funny thing—not existing.

People romanticize it, probably because of stupid action movies. They think disappearing means freedom, no obligations, no expectations. You have no coworkers, and no boss breathing down your neck. No family to disappoint.

No life.

Ada has spent most of hers convincing herself that it was preferable to be a vague concept. Not a real person. But anonymity isn’t freedom.

It’s erosion.

The “safehouse” apartment in Belgrade is small and painfully generic; the walls are beige. The furniture is beige. So is the tapwater, dirtied by old pipes.

She’s been there twelve days. It’s the longest she’s gone without moving, doing something, probably since she was eighteen. So she should be happy, right? Thankful for this opportunity to rest and recuperate or whatever, but instead she feels like she’s rotting.

Ada lies sprawled across the couch with the television on mute, one leg hanging over the armrest, eyes fixed on nothing. The room smells faintly of cigarette smoke from the tenant before her. She doesn’t smoke, but the scent clings stubbornly to the curtains anyway.

The silence is fucking unbearable.

For once she wishes there was noise. She’s always found safety in silence—it means there’s no one there. It gives her space to move. Now, though, it just means there’s nothing left to distract her.

Which is dangerous. Because when Ada has nothing to do, she thinks. And lately every thought circles back to him.

Leon Kennedy with wet hair and exhausted eyes standing in the rain in Eastern Slav Republic. Leon Kennedy stubbornly trying to save people who don’t want to be saved. Leon Kennedy looking at her like she’s still capable of redemption even after everything.

Ada groans softly and pinches skin on her arm hard enough to hurt. It’s her newest strategy; every time she catches herself thinking about him, she redirects the thought.

Pain as conditioning. Like training a dog.

It should be easy. It worked on her before. But maybe it’s one of those things that stop being effective when you’re used to pushing forward with a bullet wound. Because so far, her conditioning isn’t working for shit.

She still remembers the exact cadence of his voice from when she saw him a few months ago. The rough exhaustion in it.

Ada rolls onto her side and stares at the blinking digital clock on the microwave.

02:14.

She slept three hours yesterday. Maybe four the day before. Her body is tired, but her brain won’t shut off, restless beneath her skin like something alive and clawing.

For the first time since she was eighteen, she has nowhere to be. Nothing lined up.

The world thinks Ada Wong is dead. Ada Wong never even lived. She’s not real.

She sits up abruptly and reaches for the laptop on the coffee table before she can stop herself. The screen glows pale blue against the dark apartment. For a moment she just stares at her reflection in it.

She looks older lately. Worn down.

Before she can reconsider it, she types his name into a secure government database she absolutely should not still have access to.

KENNEDY, LEON SCOTT

His file appears instantly.

Status: ACTIVE.

Of course. Ada clicks her tongue against her teeth and scrolls.

Mission reports, clearance updates, psychological evaluations with entire paragraphs blacked out. One recent hospital visit after the Eastern Slav Republic.

Her stomach twists uncomfortably as she reads, her brows pinching together.

Injuries sustained during service.

Expected return to active duty: immediate.

Leon could lose a limb, and the government would hand him a handgun and a protein bar before pushing him back into the field.

Her fingers hover over the touchpad. If they tremble, it’s only a trick of light.

There’s a photograph attached to the file taken only a few months ago, some kind of official ID picture—probably for the formation of the brand spanking new Division of Security Operations. Leon is facing forward beneath harsh fluorescent lighting, expression carefully neutral.

He looks terrible. There’s too much tension around his mouth, exhaustion dragging at his eyes.

Ada slams the laptop shut hard enough to make the table rattle.

She should not pity him. It’s not right. He does enough of it on his own, and he doesn’t even know how good he has it; the thought keeps coming back to her.

How is he not better at dealing with it yet? It’s been over a decade since Raccoon City, he’s been an active agent for years. How come he’s deteriorating like this? His government should teach him how to shut up and take it, how to keep going even when things are hard. Ada learned to when she was fifteen, after all.

Maybe older dogs are harder to train. Especially if they’re stubborn the way Leon is. Once an idea is planted in his head, it’s like it’s impossible to get it of there.

Like what happened at the RPD.

Like Ada.

It’s strange comfort, knowing that he’s thinking about her, too. Wherever he is, whatever he’s doing, she’s almost certain his thoughts are about her.

⟡

April, 2012.

The sleeping pills are not to be mixed with the alcohol. He knows that.

But… Well.

Mistakes happen.

He hasn’t been sleeping well lately. Things just keep stacking up. It’s like every day it gets harder to parse through the mess in his head. Lately, his nightmares have started haunting him by day, too. It has him walking on eggshells around the office, terrified of what lurks around the corner.

It’s not real. None of it is real, but it sends his heart into his throat. It makes him nauseous. Sometimes, when he looks into the mirror, he swears the blood vessels in his eyes and the veins in his arms are black—infected by the Plaga. Sometimes Ashley’s standing behind him, knife to her throat. Sometimes it’s Krauser. Or Luis. Or Ada.

So, yeah. He accidentally swallowed his sleeping pills with whiskey. Again. So what. Nothing too bad has happened yet. And he’s not on call for once. He can afford to slip up.

In his hand he has the bear keychain Ada gave him. It’s eight years ago now, but it doesn’t feel like that’s true. Leon can count the amount of times he’s seen her since on two hands, he doesn’t even need to use a single toe. Hell, he barely needs the second hand. It doesn’t feel like that’s true, either.

He tosses the keychain onto the floor by his feet and takes another swig from his bottle.

Sherry turns twenty-six soon. The thought kind of freaks him out. She’s an adult now. But when he looks at her… He still sees that girl Claire saved in Raccoon City. She’s young. Too young. She’s older than Leon was when they sent him into that jungle with Krauser. She’s only a year younger than he was in Spain.

It’s a self-pitying, gross thought, but it’s branded into him by now.

How could they do that to me?

How could my own country put me through this?

It comes served with the guilt. The blame.

Why have I never fought harder to get out?

Why do I just roll over?

Why do I fight for a government I don’t believe in?

He looks for the answer at the bottom of the bottle. As usual he doesn’t find it.

At least he sleeps.

⟡

October, 2012.

Ada brushes his bangs out of his face. They’re sweaty. All of him is sweaty. There’s vomit on his shirt.

“Ada,” he says on an exhale. He tries to smile. “I’d—Mm. It’s not long time no see. Saw you yesterday.”

She hasn’t seen him since her mission in the Eastern Slav Republic. Over a year ago. But he only shakes his head when she tries to tell him that.

“N’no, no,” he slurs. “Yesterday.”

When she looks at him, she feels… disgust. Pity. This isn’t Leon S. Kennedy. She’s not sure who it is but… But he makes her chest tight. He makes it hard to breathe. He brings tears to her eyes.

God. When was the last time she cried?

“Leon,” she says. “What happened?”

He motions vaguely towards the bottle under the coffee table. “Just… You know.”

She sighs. “You do this a lot?”

He shakes his head. Of course, she has no idea if that’s true. But Robert hasn’t mentioned anything lately. Either he thinks Ada doesn’t care, or it’s not actually as bad as it looks.

“Let’s get you to bed.”

Somehow, she manages to get him off the floor. He’s so much bigger than her, even though she thinks he’s slimmed down a little—at least since Spain. But he was younger then. Maybe it’s just that.

They stagger through the apartment, bumping into walls and doorframes. It’s okay, though. Leon has no pictures on his walls that can fall at the impact. No weird decorative tables cluttered with things that get in the way.

He collapses face down into the mattress with a groan, but she’s quick to get him onto his side.

Bleary blue eyes peer at her through thick lashes. “You cut your hair again.”

Ada nods, too busy arranging him under the covers to talk. She doesn’t bother trying to get him out of his clothes. He’s probably slept in much worse places, in much worse circumstances.

“Looks nice,” he continues.

“Thanks,” she says with a small huff.

“Stay?”

For some reason, she nods.

There’s a chair in the corner of the room that Ada sits down on. Leon watches her, smiling. “I put that there for you, you know.”

“Creepy,” she says.

“You’re the one watching me sleep.”

“I don’t do that.”

He shakes his head slightly. “Y’do it all the time.”

She sighs. “Get some sleep, okay?”

It doesn’t take him long. But she can’t make herself leave. Over and over again, she tells herself just one more minute. One more minute. Then suddenly the sun is rising, and her whole body hurts from spending the night in a dining chair.

She’s gone before Leon wakes up, though. It makes her ache in all the same places it always does.

⟡

In December of 2012, Ada’s doppelgänger is ordered to capture Jake Muller, who is deemed

likely to be a source of C-Virus antibodies.

Right at the divide between June and July, 2013, as Ada discovers she is being framed for global C-Virus attacks and Leon is hunting Simmons after the president’s death, their paths

cross.

Despite the accusations surrounding “Ada Wong,” Leon continues to trust her.

✦ . ⁺ . ✦ . ⁺ . ✦

Chapter 2: PART TWO

Chapter Summary

He stopped the pills. Lost Ada. Picked up alcohol. Earned a few more scars and wounds and nightmares.

He’s not himself.

So maybe it’s for the best.

October, 2013.

Leon is older than his dad ever was.

Should he be proud of that? Maybe. His dad was a criminal. He could have turned Leon into one, too.

He didn’t, though. Because he died when he was thirty-five.

Well, Leon is thirty-six now.

And he misses his father. And his mother. His sister, too, even though he never really knew her. She was so young.

So maybe it’s more so the idea of them he misses. People to love, people who love him back. People to go to when things are hard. People who can help him.

Leon is thirty-six, and he’s old enough and sad enough to admit that he’s lonely. That he wants to love someone properly. Wants a warm body in his bed and kisses in the shower and two empty cups in the sink.

But he knows he can’t date, for a list of reasons longer than the Declaration of Independence.

Ada Wong is half, if not more, of the reasons.

He couldn’t date someone else when he’s so fucking in love with Ada that it’s ruining his life. Ruining everything. It’s been fifteen years since he met her, and maybe he should let her go, but he can’t. There’s only one person Leon’s ever told about her, and Claire would never outright say that Ada isn’t good for him, that he does enough wallowing and hiding away without her in the mix.

He can’t possibly tell Claire she’s wrong, that she just doesn’t understand. Despite what Leon has told her, Claire thinks Ada manipulated him. That she’s bad news, a villain he needs to stay away from.

But so fucking what? Leon works for the U.S. government. How fucking perfect are they? How many wars have they started? How many other governments have they toppled and economies collapsed? Benford started a fucking ‘war on terror’, and Leon still smiled in photos and shook his hand like the good government dog he is.

It’s just two sides of the same coin, and Leon knows that Ada is good. He can feel it in his bones. She’s saved his life, she helped him save Ashley’s. Hell, Ada Wong has saved the world.

But it also wouldn’t matter if she hadn’t, because Leon knows that Ada could shoot him point blank and he’d thank her for it. She could spit on him and hit him and torture him, and he’d just beg for more. A hit, after all, is still a touch.

If Ada is hitting him, she’s there.

He knows it’s awful. He knows he’s fucked up beyond belief, beyond relief. It just doesn’t matter.

There isn’t a lot that matters anymore.

Leon Kennedy is thirty-six, and he has nothing. No one.

He thinks of ending it more often than not, and then he thinks of everyone who’s dead because of him, and why should he be allowed to escape that? No. He has to sit with it. His hands, his face, his entire self is drenched in the blood of innocents, and he cannot escape that. He’s not allowed to.

But now he can’t stop thinking. Not even when he’s asleep. Nightmares haunt him, keeping him awake and restless and out of his mind. He trembles with exhaustion, stumbles over his own feet, walks into people without noticing.

The pills help, though. They blur the edge of every thought, and they help him sleep. Fitful and light, but it’s more than he’s had in years.

Now he spends most evenings half-conscious on the couch, TV flickering soundlessly across the apartment while he drifts somewhere ugly and numb in-between awake and asleep.

Sometimes he has good dreams. Of Ada.

Not really good, maybe, because even in dreams, she appears like smoke and fire, like blood blooming through water, like mountains and lakes and falling. But they’re still better than the other dreams—than rotting flesh and gunfire and people screaming his name while he fails them over and over again.

The dreams of Ada are quieter, in a sense. Sometimes he dreams that she kisses him. Sometimes she sits beside him in silence. Sometimes she lets him hold her long enough that, upon waking, the emptiness beside him feels cruel—intentional and evil and too real.

Dreaming of Ada leaves something sour lodged beneath his ribs all day long. A horrible kind of… longing. He wants her. Always.

He starts chasing the feeling after a while without meaning to, sinking deeper into the chemical fog each night hoping maybe his brain will conjure her again. And eventually it does often enough that her presence stops startling him entirely. She belongs to that hazy in-between place now, alongside the TV static and half-finished glasses of whiskey and exhaustion.

Leon is thirty-six, and he’s on his back on the bathroom floor; it’s heated—and he’s cold.

The tile presses pleasantly against his spine, but his body feels distant in a way he thinks should probably scare him more than it does.

He can’t… quite remember how he ended up there. There was the couch… Maybe he needed to pee. Did he? He can’t tell. Can’t really feel his body.

The light overhead is too bright. Or maybe too soft.

The edges of everything bleed together strangely, the white ceiling rippling faintly like waves every time he blinks.

He thinks of the ocean. Of Ada at his side and water misting his face, pearling in her hair.

It’s not even been that long since he saw her.

His heartbeat feels slow.

He thinks of being kissed in the middle of death and decay.

The constant noise in his head finally starts unraveling, threads snapping one by one until everything grows softer. The guilt dulls. The memories blur. Faces lose their names; names lose their faces.

But his chest still aches. A deep, rotten feeling beneath his ribs like bruising spreading slowly inward.

He wonders sometimes if loving Ada has actually altered his brain chemistry. If she’s dug herself so deeply into him that his body now treats wanting her like survival itself.

Sustenance. Water. Oxygen. Ada.

Withdrawal. Maybe he’s not missing her. Maybe the way he’s feeling is just withdrawal.

The thought almost makes him laugh.

Instead, he exhales shakily through his nose. The room feels too warm now, all of a sudden. His skin too tight. He thinks he might be sweating. Or dying.

He wishes he was and instantly regrets it.

His fingers twitch against the floor.

He remembers Ada’s hands with humiliating clarity. The shape of them. The precise weight of her touch against his jaw when she kissed him in Racoon City. The exact circumference of her wrist when he grabbed it in Spain. The smell of her. The sound of her voice.

God. He’s pathetic.

Maybe everyone else is right. Maybe Ada ruined him.

Leon turns his head slightly. The movement sends a slow wave of dizziness through him, warm and nauseating. The bathtub looms nearby, porcelain gleaming under the light.

His eyes drift shut again.

For a second, he can almost feel someone beside him on the floor. A knee against his thigh. Cool fingers against his wrist.

“Leon,” the person says, and he isn’t shocked at all.

Conjuring up visions of her is his favourite pastime recently.

“Ada,” he slurs.

“What have you taken?” she asks, sighing—as if she’s a little annoyed.

“Dunno,” he replies. “Missed you.”

“Right,” she says. “Sit up, big guy.”

He sits. Immediately the dizziness comes back, and he has to wait several seconds before he can slowly open his eyes.

Ada tilts her head at him, lips pursed. “Didn’t I tell you to stop this?”

She did, actually. Leon is surprised he remembers that well enough to make his imagination tell him again.

“How else…?” he starts but can’t really find all the words. His mouth is dry, his lips cracked. “Y’know… You’re here.”

Ada looks at him for a long moment after that. Unreadable in the way she has always been—there are three different versions of her layered on top of each other, and Leon has spent fifteen years trying to figure out which one is real.

Maybe all of them. Maybe none. He wants all of them, anyway, so it doesn’t matter.

Leon stares at her.

He’s gotten better at dreaming her now—better at the details. Sometimes she even speaks in full conversations instead of fragmented little pieces. Once, after mixing whiskey with sleeping pills, his brain conjured her so vividly he’d reached for her hand and cried when it closed on empty air. But sometimes she’s solid when he touches her. Leon knows he’s really fucked up, then. When she reaches for him first or kisses his temples or brushes his hair out of his face.

“You look awful,” Ada says.

Leon huffs out a laugh. “That’s mean.” Then, quieter to himself, “Why do I always make you mean?”

“You’re on the floor.”

“It’s heated.”

“That’s supposed to be justification?” Her voice sounds so real. Sometimes it does.

His stomach twists faintly. The drugs are making everything swim together in slow motion, but Ada remains strangely sharp at the centre of it all. He can see the faint smudge beneath her eyeliner, a tiny crease between her perfect brows, and a glint of irritation in her eyes.

“You’re not real,” he tells her.

Ada blinks. “Excuse me?”

“I dream you up.” He gestures at her with a trembling hand. He’s still flat on his back. “So why are you mean?”

She sighs again, quieter this time, and crouches properly in front of him. The movement is smooth and controlled, balanced on the balls of her feet with catlike grace. Leon watches her hands automatically.

One reaches toward his face, and he flinches before he can stop himself.

Ada’s fingers pause against his jaw. There’s pressure.

Oh.

He’s Fucked. Up.

She’s never felt this real… Shit, he can even smell her. Did he take too many? He’d swallowed them down with a new whiskey—maybe they didn’t go well together.

“Weird,” he mutters.

“What?”

“Feels real.”

Ada’s thumb brushes briefly beneath one of his eyes.

“You’re getting worse,” she says quietly.

Leon smiles a little—crooked and tired. “Maybe.”

“It’s not funny, Leon.”

“No,” he agrees. “Not really.”

Silence settles between them. But it’s not uncomfortable. Ada has never been uncomfortable to Leon, not even when she’s pointing guns at him or stitching him up in hotel rooms or disappearing for months or lying directly to his face. Being near her always feels strangely easy.

“You look good tonight,” he says at length.

Ada rolls her eyes softly. “You’re high.”

“Mhm.” He scoots backwards until he can slump against the bathtub. “‘S why you’re here. It’s easier to… see you like this. But tonight… I can even smell you. ‘S like you’re here. Maybe I did too much, huh, Ada?”

God, his brain is cruel. It remembers her too well.

His eyes drift half-shut.

“I know I did. Because I know you’re not here,” he continues in a murmur, words sticking together.

“Why not?”

“Because…” Leon frowns faintly, trying to gather the thought before it slips away again. “I don’t know! But you leave. You always leave. Why would you… Why come if you just wanna leave?”

“You think I want to leave?” she asks quietly.

Leon’s sluggish brain catches on the question slowly, like a hook dragging through water.

“You always do.”

“I always have to.”

“There’s a difference?”

“Yes.”

The room tilts slightly again. Leon closes his eyes for a moment until the dizziness settles into something manageable. When he opens them, Ada is still there. Elbows resting loosely against her knees now, posture relaxed in a way he rarely sees from her.

Usually she looks poised to bolt at any given second.

“You died,” Leon says suddenly.

The words slip out before he can stop them.

Ada stills.

In another state of mind, Leon might have regretted bringing it up. But the drugs have dissolved most of the barriers between thought and speech. Everything inside him leaks out, slow and messy.

“Chris told me,” he continues. “You died. I thought you died.”

Ada’s gaze drops briefly to the floor between them. “That wasn’t me.”

“I know that now.”

“But for a while,” she fills in, “you thought it was.”

“Yeah.” Leon frowns. “I did.”

He remembers it too clearly. The horror of hearing Chris’ words. Thinking that was how it ended. The pain, like a thousand needles pricking every inch of his body, digging beneath his skin, piercing his veins, and seeping the blood out of him.

He’d grieved her like something vital had been ripped out of him. A lung. His heart.

All he could think about was that bar, about walking out on her like a petulant child.

Ada watches him for another long moment before speaking again. “All that happened with Simmons…” she says slowly, “changed things for me too.”

Leon’s brow furrows faintly. “What?”

“In Lanshiang.” Her voice grows quieter. “You wouldn’t leave me behind.”

He stares at her blankly for a second before the memory surfaces sluggishly through the chemical haze.

“You died in Racoon City, too. I dropped you.”

“I didn’t die.”

“I dropped you.” He chokes on the words. “I let you go.”

Ada ignores that. “I’ve spent years pushing you away,” she says instead. “Or keeping you at arm’s length, at least.” Her lips twitch faintly. “But after Lanshiang… I saw how much….” The real Ada would never fumble her words like this. “I knew you love me. And I knew that I… felt the same. So in China, when I saw how different you were, and after what I saw a year ago… I don’t want to stray as far away anymore. I can’t.”

The room feels very still.

Leon stares at her and wonders, again, if he took too many, drank too much. How fucked up is he, really, if his subconscious is cruel enough to invent this conversation?

He swallows thickly.

“You’re not real,” he says again, weaker this time.

Ada’s expression softens into something almost fond. “I suppose this would be easier if I wasn’t. For you, at least.”

“I wish…” he trails off. Frowns. “Yeah.”

“What?”

“I was just thinking about you. Before.”

“Oh?”

He nods and instantly regrets it when the whole world moves with it. He winces, eyes squeezed shut again. When he opens them, she’s suddenly closer, kneeling right by his side.

“I was thinking about you… hitting me.”

“Why?”

“Because I want you to touch me.”

She sighs and reaches for him again. This time he remembers not to flinch.

“You don’t want it soft?” she asks, her palm a little chilly against his cheek.

It feels so real.

“Real Ada wouldn’t do that.”

A lie. That night, so many years ago now, when he took her to his bed, she was soft. Her hands were soft, and her lips were soft. Real Ada has never hit him. Real Ada loves him.

The Ada on his bathroom floor slaps him.

The sound cracks through the room sharp enough to make Leon’s head ring.

Not hard. Not really. Leon’s seen her kill people. If she wanted to dislocate his jaw, she could. It’s like a little love tap, really, and Ada’s hand lingers briefly against his face afterward, fingers curled slightly from the impact.

There’s a faint sting blooming across his cheek now, warm and immediate and hard for his fogged- out brain to smooth into a dream.

Leon stares at her.

Slowly, very slowly, Ada raises one eyebrow. “Did that feel imaginary to you?”

He opens his mouth, feels the skin on his lips crack.

But nothing comes out.

Because…

No.

No, that felt real.

Too real.

The drugs are still dragging everything through sticky syrup, but suddenly there’s a horrible sharpness cutting through the haze: the ache in his jaw; the heat of the bathroom floor beneath him.

His stomach twists violently.

“Oh,” he says faintly.

Ada watches him carefully now. Some of the irritation has disappeared from her face, replaced by something more cautious, almost worried.

“Oh?” she echoes.

“You’re actually here,” he says.

“I’m starting to understand why you’re a field agent and not big on intelligence work.”

“Ada.”

“Yes, Leon.”

“You’re here.”

She sighs softly again, but there’s relief threaded through it now too, hidden poorly beneath the exasperation. “Yes.”

Leon just stares at her. And suddenly—horribly—everything he’s said comes rushing back.

“Oh my God,” he whispers.

Ada’s expression shifts. “Leon.”

“No, no, wait.” He drags a hand over his face hard enough to hurt. “Jesus Christ. You’re real?”

“Well. Yes.”

“You heard all that?”

“Yes. I’m right here. You talk loudly.”

“All of it?”

“Yes, Leon.”

His entire body floods with humiliation so intense it almost burns through the drugs completely. He makes a strangled sound and drops his head back against the bathtub with a dull thunk.

“Kill me.”

Ada folds her arms. “No. It’s not like it’s the worst you’ve ever told me.”

“I told you I hallucinate you.”

“You did.”

“I told you I think about you hitting me.”

“That one was… enlightening. But you do tend to say a lot of stupid things.”

Leon groans and covers his eyes with both hands. The motion makes him sway slightly where he sits.

“Ada, I’m… I’m serious. Shoot me.”

“No.” Her voice is soft.

Leon peeks at her through his fingers.

Ada is still kneeling in front of him, one hand resting lightly against his knee now to steady him.

“You’re staying?” he asks quietly, terrified to death of the answer.

“For now,” she says.

Maybe those words should hollow him out. For now. Temporary. Borrowed time. That’s always what she gives him. But he does not complain. He has no right to. He’d give up everything for a second with her.

Even though it makes him dizzy enough that bile floods his mouth, Leon clumsily stands. He spits into the bathtub and coughs. Then he looks at Ada, still on the floor.

She’s craning her neck to look at him, expression flat.

“D’you want dinner?” Leon asks, only a little slurred.

“You’re telling me there’s food in this apartment?” she asks.

He laughs, short and rough. “No.” He gestures towards the bathroom door. “We order.”

“Romantic,” she hums. Her eyes flit to him. “Do you want it to be?”

She rises, smoother than he ever could, and for once she seems to be in flat shoes. If Leon had been cognisant enough to realise that sooner, perhaps he would have understood how real she is. Ada in his dreams always wears heels, always appearing with that click clack sound. Her heels on asphalt and cobblestone and metal.

Leon’s eyes narrow. “Guess.”

She turns. “Good thing I brought groceries.”

He follows her out of the bathroom on wobbly legs, pulled along by her invisible leash. Lo and behold, there are several bags on the kitchen counter.

“Why?” he asks stupidly.

“Because you don’t eat nearly enough, Leon. Especially not with your drinking.”

“You know about that.” It’s not a question.

She leans against the counter and looks at him. “Yes.”

“You…” He isn’t sure what to say, if what he wants to say is… allowed. He decides it is. If she slaps him again, he’ll only be glad anyway. “Care.”

“Yes.”

The speed at which she admits it makes him smile—dopey and awkward, and there’s probably gross stuff stuck in his teeth, and he just threw up a little in the bathroom. But she cares. It feels even better than the slap.

Once, years ago, she implied that she loves him. Is that still true for her, the way it is for him?

The drugs are still there, though it’s simmering now. Easier to manage. Or it’s just her force, somehow cutting through the haze.

“I can’t really cook,” he says, not wanting to linger in the softness. He slides onto a barstool as she starts unpacking the bags.

“Good thing I can.”

Ada sighs. “You know we can’t.”

“Maybe,” he admits. “But also not really.”

Her lips purse. “I wish we could,” she says after a few moments of silence.

Leon wonders if she’s being genuine or if she’s lying to him. Even worse, is she only telling him because she knows he’ll forget it all when he wakes.

“Jus’—Get a new job,” he says.

“I wish I could.” She gives him a look he can’t decipher. “Why do I have to leave my job, anyway?”

“I’m the government.” Leon frowns. “I… I can’t.”

“There’s always a choice,” she says.

The chicken ends up in a bowl. She adds some seasoning, and then the bowl is gone. It’s replaced by a delicious smell a few moments later, but Leon can’t see what.

He can’t see anything, actually.

He blinks frantically, but it’s all black.

Panic sets in, sharp like ice, but it melts just as quickly when there’s a hand on his shoulder.

Leon lifts his head and looks at Ada.

He’s not sure when he face-planted into the counter, but his face is cool from the countertops.

She frowns at him. “You okay?”

“You—you wouldn’t be here if I was,” Leon says, only a tiny bit scathing.

But he knows he has no right to be angry. Ada is correct, as she often is. There is always a choice, and he puts too much ‘blame’ for their… sporadic relationship on her. He could leave the DSO, work with her instead. He could seek her out, climb through her windows, and make her food. He could find her in a dark moment and bring her out of it.

He doesn’t.

He chooses to sit at home instead, nursing diluted whiskey and dreaming about her. Waiting.

Is he stupid?

Yes, he decides. He must be.

Ada just sighs. “Go lay down. This’ll be done soon. I’ll bring it out to you.”

“No,” he tells her, shaking his head—which makes him dizzy again. He waits for the world to stop spinning again before he continues. “Wanna be here. Look at you.”

“You’re like a toddler,” she replies, though it doesn’t sound unkind. “Sure. Stay.”

She cooks, and he watches, pretending it’s a normal night. That they always do this. His hazy imagination allows it easily, settling warmth in his chest. It’s as if his brain is taunting him, purposely setting him up for how horrible he’ll feel in the morning when he wakes up and she’s gone. Will this even feel real, then? He knows his dread well enough to picture it, to taste it on his tongue without feeling it.

Come morning, he’ll probably wake up and think it was all a dream. It will crush him, soul and spirit, but he’ll get up, because there’s no other choice.

Then he’ll see the dirty plates. Two of them. The cutlery. A pair of pairs. Glasses in the sink. And he’ll remember, and it’ll crush him again.

But he’ll go to work, because there’s no other choice.
