December, 2013.

Ada Wong isn’t real.

She’s not sure if Leon knows that. Maybe he does and forgets. She doesn’t exist, though.

Ada Wong is a fake name for a tool that has a simple purpose and function. She’s not a person with wants and needs and dreams. But now it’s been so long that the syllables of her real name feel wrong. She doesn’t exist either.

Ada Wong could be real. She wants to be. With Leon she is.

Somehow he breathes life into her. Not in the sense that she’s necessarily happier or more excited around him. He simply makes her a person. Someone who’s allowed to be more than one thing.

It’s terrifying, and it’s horrible. It’s distracting. Ada knows better than to indulge him. She can’t stop herself.

It’s Christmas. She’s never celebrated, not in any life. But he is. With those two other orphans—the Redfields. It’s sweet, and she’s certainly not jealous. From what she gathers, holidays are only messy. All the gifts and the planning… It’s stressful, clearly, judging by how busy and loud every street and store is come December.

Ada Wong likes the quiet, and she likes the snow—despite how annoying that can be, too.

She watches how her boots sink into the thin layer that’s fallen and settled. Black on white. It’s satisfying, and so is the crunch of the powdery snow beneath her sole.

If she looked up and to her left, she’d be staring at Claire Redfield’s apartment building. So she doesn’t.

That’s not why she’s in the city. She’s told herself for days that that’s not why she’s in the city. She just has to keep moving, can’t stay too long anywhere these days. That’s why she’s there.

She passes the building. Not directly in front of it, but across the desolate street. Far enough that she could still pretend this is coincidence. That she’s only in the city because it’s D.C., and not because it’s his.

Around and above her, the windows glow amber against the darkening evening, soft and domestic. The sidewalks and streets are almost completely empty—the bad weather keeps even those who don’t celebrate Christmas inside. Snow catches in the dark strands of her hair as she walks, melting slowly against the collar of her coat.

She slows slightly, throws a glance over her shoulder to the unit she knows belongs to Claire Redfield, and feels pathetic.

What is she doing? Why is she there?

Ada lowers her head further and keeps walking.

Then a door slams open behind so hard the sound echoes down the street.

“—Ada!”

She stops instantly. Her entire body locks before she can help it, training overriding thought. Her hand nearly drops toward the gun beneath her coat before she recognizes the voice.

Her heart pounds against her ribcage, and she’s terrified for the first time in a decade.

Leon.

For one second, humiliation crashes through her so intensely she almost keeps walking.

He’s seen her.

This isn’t a mission. This isn’t like Raccoon City or Spain or China or wherever. It’s not like when she visits him, either. Then she knows. She can prepare.

Ada closes her eyes briefly.

Then she turns.

Leon is halfway down the apartment steps already, breath visible in sharp white clouds through the cold. He’s wearing jeans, a dark sweater, and absolutely no coat whatsoever. Snow dusts his blond hair and catches along his shoulders.

And slippers. Indoor slippers that look soggy before he reaches the sidewalk.

Ada stares at him and is a little surprised to see him steady enough to run like that. He looks better, she thinks, but it’s so dark. She can’t really see. But just the fact that he’s upright is enough to settle something constantly worried inside her.

Leon will make it through this year, too.

If they both try hard enough, maybe he’ll even get to turn forty. Maybe Ada Wong will turn forty, too.

Xiao Yu, however, is just as old as Leon.

She likes that.

He’s in the middle of the street now, blue eyes wide, but neither of them say anything.

Finally, a little breathless, Leon says, “You’re a much prettier stalker than your usual guy.”

Ada arches one brow automatically. “I have no idea what you’re talking about.”

His mouth twitches into a grin. He looks twenty-one and eager, looking at her like that.

“I’m serious,” he says, walking even closer through the snow. “He wears weird sweaters. Draws an awful lot of attention.”

“I’m not stalking you, Leon.”

“No, I know. You pay someone else to do it.”

The words aren’t very light or teasing, but the way he says them is.

“You’re going to freeze to death,” she says, eyeing the slippers.

Leon glances down. “I almost took Claire’s. But half my foot was hanging out.”

He reaches her then, close enough now that Ada can see the pink flush spreading across his cheeks from the cold. His hair is slightly too long. There’s a faint crease between his brows.

“I’m sure someone with your brilliant survival instincts knows how to put on a pair of boots.”

“I get Helena to do the laces for me when we’re heading into the field,” he says.

She almost smiles at that.

Almost.

“Aren’t you supposed to be inside celebrating Christmas?” she asks.

“I was.”

“Was?”

His expression shifts strangely then. Softer. Nervous, almost. “I saw you. I knew it was you.” He laughs, just as nervously as he looks. “Unsure what I did to make the universe or God or whatever smile on me but… I looked out the window, and there you were. Which is perfect, ‘cause I have something for you.”

Ada blinks. Her heart stops beating.

“What?”

“A gift.”

The word lands awkwardly in her head, foreign enough that for a second, she genuinely doesn’t understand what he means. Nobody gives Ada Wong gifts.

Payments, information, weapons, forged passports—yes.

But gifts?

When was the last time somebody gave her something without expecting blood in return? She honestly cannot remember it.

Well. It would probably be the orgasms he gave her in 2005.

Leon seems to notice her confusion, because his smile turns smaller around the edges.

“It’s not a big deal,” he says quickly. “I just saw it and thought of you.”

That just makes it worse.

Ada watches, silent, as Leon reaches into his pocket with slightly clumsy fingers, numbed red from cold. Then he presses something small into her gloved palm.

A necklace; thin silver chain; delicate enough that Ada could probably snap it accidentally if she pulled too hard.

At the centre hangs a butterfly pendant.

She stares at it.

“It’s stupid,” he says into the silence, suddenly sounding uncertain. “Claire said women like jewellery, which feels sexist, honestly, but she threatened me when I tried to buy you a knife instead, so—”

“A knife?” she asks, voice thicker than she’d like.

She can’t tear her eyes off the necklace.

“She said you probably already had enough. That it’d be nice to give you something that has nothing to do with your job. Something for you.”

Ada looks up at him finally. Snow has started gathering in his hair now in earnest. He’s shivering slightly, though he seems too focused on her reaction to notice.

Something inside Ada hurts—worse than falling, worse than gunshot wounds and virus infections. It’s a quiet, devastating kind of hurt that wants to eat her up from the inside.

“I don’t have anything for you,” she says quietly.

He shrugs immediately. “It’s not like I expected to give this to you today. I just… carry it around.”

Ada looks down at the necklace once more.

Then at him.

At the cold-reddened ears and stupid slippers and hopeful expression he’s trying very hard to hide.

Ada makes a decision. Perhaps—no, definitely—the worst one she’s ever made. She steps forward, grabs the front of his sweater, and kisses him.

Leon makes a startled sound against her mouth, but he melts into it instantly. Of course he does— he wants her so badly, she knows it.

Knows that he’d do too much for her. All she has to do is ask.

His hands hover uncertainly for only half a second before one settles carefully against her waist. Ada kisses him harder.

She’s starving, and she allows her body to feel it.

This is dangerous. She shouldn’t want this, she’s tried so hard to rinse it out of herself. Leon makes her feel too alive and too human and too capable of being loved.

She licks into his mouth and swallows the sounds he makes, small whimpers too reminiscent of what he sounds like when he’s wounded, of what he sounded like between her legs. It fills her with a desperation, a need, to protect him. To hold him closer and get him through it safely.

But it’s just a kiss. It’s just Christmas.

He tastes like mulled wine and a lot of food, and he kisses like it’s been years since the last time. To be fair, it probably has been.

No, it definitely has been.

He’s hard—she can feel it. At some point, he pulled her closer, pressed his hips to hers, and now she can feel how hard he is.

She pulls back, fear and heat battling in every single part of her.

“Ada,” he says softly.

She releases his sweater immediately. Steps back.

“Merry Christmas,” she says, already backing further and further away.

Leon doesn’t even try to follow her.

“See you,” he says, fingers touching his lips. All of him is trembling, and she suspects it’s not just from the cold.

“Take care,” she tells him—then she disappears again.

In 2014, about a year after the Lanshiang bioterror incident, Leon leads a team of DSO agents to Bethesda, Maryland. Their mission is to apprehend and interrogate Senator Eyre.

June, 2014.

Leon doesn’t think he’ll see thirty-seven.

He stopped the pills. Ada told him take care, and he wants to take care. He knows he’s making a big deal out of nothing, really. That she didn’t mean get sober. She was just saying goodbye. Still, it makes him flush them all down the toilet.

He can’t stop the alcohol, though. He tells himself that’s okay. Alcohol isn’t as bad as pills. Right?

He tells himself a lot of things and believes none of it. Like, I did what I could. It’s not my fault. Nothing could have changed the outcome.

A bomb goes off in Washington, and he’s the only one who survives. That’s not his fault.

He hasn’t seen Ada since December. That hurts even more than losing his entire squad, and he knows that’s wrong. God, he knows. But since he stopped the pills, he hasn’t even seen her— hallucinated her.

It’s fucked to hell that he misses it, but who could blame him?

Well. Lots of people. But it’s not like he’s telling Chris Redfield that he used to get high to hallucinate that a “bioterrorist” came into his bed to sleep next to him. Chris judges him enough for the alcohol. For his “self pity” and “wallowing”.

The Redfield siblings are better people than Leon could ever be, so they judge him with well meaning, and they fail to understand.

Leon’s birthday is two months away, but he’s not sure he’ll make it. And for the first time ever, he’s not sure he actually wants to. His usual motivations can barely get him up anymore. He’s angrier than usual.

Weaker and slower and more violent.

He stopped the pills. Lost Ada. Picked up alcohol. Earned a few more scars and wounds and nightmares.

He’s not himself.

So maybe it’s for the best.

Together with Chris Redfield, Leon heads to New York City to save Dr. Rebecca Chambers

August, 2014.

The first thing Ada notices is the blood. It paints the concrete in thick dark streaks beneath the emergency lights, almost black in the rain. The body lies twisted near the loading dock wall, one arm bent wrong beneath him.

The man is dead.

Ada crouches silently atop the adjacent rooftop and watches Leon stand over him. But something feels wrong. Not the kill itself—the man had been armed, an old Umbrella affiliate the government’s been hunting for years. She understands why Leon killed him; she doesn’t blame him.

What’s wrong is the stillness afterward.

Leon doesn’t move.

Rain soaks through his shirt in seconds, plastering fabric to skin. His gun hangs loose at his side. Smoke still curls faintly from the barrel.

And he just stares. Looks, at least. She can’t see too much from where she’s perched.

Ada’s stomach tightens. She’s unsettled enough that she drops soundlessly from the rooftop before she can reconsider it.

Her boots hit the wet pavement softly, but loud enough that Leon’s gun snaps toward her instantly.

He’s too fast. The movement is so sharp and instinctive that Ada freezes for one half-second, genuinely unsure if he recognizes her.

But then his eyes focus.

“Ada.”

He doesn’t lower the gun. He doesn’t even smile. Rain drips from his hair into his eyes. There’s a cut splitting one eyebrow open, blood diluted pink against his temple by water.

Ada lowers her gaze briefly toward the corpse. Then back to Leon.

“Come to finish what he couldn’t?” he asks.

She studies him carefully. “You’re injured.”

“I’ve been worse.”

Up close, he looks terrible. Thinner than he was at Christmas; sharper around the jaw. The exhaustion beneath his eyes has deepened into something ugly and permanent. He smells like alcohol, even through the rain.

“How long have you been bleeding?” she asks. “Maybe I don’t even need to shoot you.”

He’s not her mission. She’s not supposed to be there at all.

Leon glances vaguely toward his side like he forgot about it. There’s a dark stain spreading beneath his tactical vest.

Finally, he drops his gun.

Ada lets out a breath she’s embarrassed she was holding in. He shouldn’t scare her. He doesn’t.

“Couple hours,” he replies.

“Leon.”

“What?” His voice sharpens unexpectedly. “Mission’s done. I’m going home now. Unless you’re here to make that difficult.”

Ada steps closer.

“Let me see it.”

“No.” His voice is flat, his eyes distant.

Ada blinks once. “What?”

“I said no.”

Rainwater runs down Leon’s face as he finally looks directly at her. His eyes are bloodshot— exhaustion and liquor making a dangerous cocktail in his bloodstream.

“I think you should stop this, Ada,” he says.

The words hit harder than they should; Ada feels them physically.

“Leon—”

“No, seriously.” He laughs once, low and humourless. “This is impressive timing, actually. As usual. You got some kinda tracker on me? Wait until I need you before showing up.”

Ada’s jaw tightens. “Don’t be ridiculous. I was working.”

“Funny. So was I.”

“You’re drunk,” she snaps.

She’s unsure when she became angry.

Leon smiles at that, but it’s a smile she hasn’t seen before. Not one of his stupid smirks or one of the awkward boyish ones. It’s toothy and tight and wrong.

“Little bit.”

“You’re working.”

“So?”

“What happened to taking care, hm?”

Leon looks away from her toward the body instead. “I stopped the pills. I thought that would get me all better. You know what I figured out, though? There are a lotta ways to kill yourself slowly.” Ada can’t breathe properly for a second. The blood loss is making him loose-lipped. Meaner than usual. Honest in ways he normally hides behind jokes.

“You don’t mean that.”

“Don’t I?”

“No.” But the certainty in her voice weakens slightly.

Leon notices. His laugh this time is quieter. Sadder.

“There she is,” he murmurs. “Thought maybe you stopped caring.”

Ada stares at him.

Then at the blood soaking through his side.

Then back at him again.

And suddenly she’s furious. She’s beyond angry. Her blood boils, burning the inside of her skin. She wants to claw at it and rip it all off. She’s so angry she hurts.

But not at Leon.

At herself.

Because she did this.

Every appearance. Every disappearance. Every moment she let him believe maybe someday while still keeping one foot out the door.

Ada spent years telling herself distance protected him, but now she’s looking at what almost sixteen years of stolen moments and lofty promises created.

A broken man.

It’s not just her, she knows that. Leon has seen and done more than people could ever imagine. He’s lost too much, too many. She teases him for working for the government, but he didn’t choose that. He probably hasn’t made a choice since Racoon City. Since he was twenty-one. But he’s—God, he’s thirty-seven now.

They’re thirty-seven. They’re aged, in the way that a screwdriver gets a little rusty and the rubber handle starts flaking. A good tool, still useful, even if bits have been chipped off.

Leon sways, taking half a step backwards. Ada moves forward instantly and catches his arm before he can steady himself properly. For one brief second, he goes rigid beneath her touch, but then he melts. It’s not on purpose—he’s too closed off for that.

It’s just pure exhausted instinct. She knows it well enough to see it.

Some part of him has been waiting months for someone to hold him upright.

Leon’s head dips slightly toward hers, rain dripping from his lashes.

“You came,” he says quietly.

Ada grips his arm tighter. “Yes.”

His eyes close briefly. “You always do eventually.”

“Are you dying?” she asks him, eyes on his side again.

“Maybe,” he replies. “But so what?”

She shakes him. “Shut up.”

“I—” His voice cracks. “I don’t want to anymore, Ada. Can’t it just stop?”

“It can’t,” she snaps at him. “You know it fucking can’t.”

“But I want it to.”

Ada stares at him. He looks exhausted enough to collapse where he stands, all the anger and violence gone, sluicing off him with the rain. He’s trembling, shivering, with cold and pain and intoxication. He just killed someone, he’s dying, and it doesn’t seem that he cares a lot.

Her brain tries to tell her that he’s being dramatic and self pitying. He’s drunk. He’s manipulating her.

But her heart knows him. It knows that Leon Kennedy is tired—not sleepy, not overworked.

Soul-deep tired. Bone-deep tired. It has settled in his cells, a virus in its own way. Leon probably doesn’t remember what life was like before this. He only knows the ache, the work, the blood, the nightmares, the exhaustion.

And that kind of tired, that kind of life, makes death sound so very appealing.

She knows.

She knows because that’s been her. Some days, it still is.

Ada’s grip tightens painfully around his arm. “No,” she says again, but the word comes out quieter now. Less sharp.

He’s slurring his words now, growing more and more unsteady as the adrenaline that was, no doubt, keeping him going burns off.

“You’re bleeding through your vest,” she says, and the words taste like glass.

She doesn’t want to lose him. Not like this.

“Yeah.”

“You could die.”

Another shrug. He doesn’t care.

She has watched Leon throw himself into danger for years. Watched him bleed and burn and crawl through hell over and over again. But there was always something driving him forward before. Duty. Anger. Guilt. Hope.

Now there’s just momentum. And when momentum stops—

Ada cannot let herself finish that thought.

“Why not?”

Because the idea of a world without you in it makes me feel insane.

Because every city I pass through already feels haunted enough.

Because you look at me like I was human, and now I don’t know how to become less than that again.

It’s been hell, leaving every time.

Because I love you.

“What?” His voice is slurred around the edges. “M’serious.”

“And drunk.”

“Little disappointing. Can I say that?”

She adjusts his arm more securely around her shoulders and starts guiding him toward the alley exit where her car is parked.

Leon stumbles once, breath catching.

“Easy,” she says automatically, her grip tightening.

“Fair.”

The drive back to the hotel is quiet.

Leon leans his head against the window, still shivering. Water drips off him in a steady stream, soaking the leather along with his blood. The city lights smear across the glass in blurred streaks of gold and white every time the car passes beneath a streetlamp.

“Leon.”

A low hum.

“Don’t fall asleep,” she says.

“M’not,” he mumbles without opening his eyes.

“You are.”

“Just resting my eyes.”

Ada grips the steering wheel tighter. “That’s my line.”

“You’ve got a bunch of good ones.”

He winces but nods a little, and his eyes flutter open. “Getting shot is a pain in the ass.”

He huffs, blinking at her. “A real little comedian.”

“Uhuh.”

The hotel parking garage is mostly empty this late. Good.

“A bomb went off, bringing the ceiling down on him. Multiple skeletal fractures, crushed lung. I don’t even remember. The list was… too long.”

She bites her lip. Nods.

Chris continues. “The doctors are optimistic, though.”

“How?” she asks, huffing wetly. “He looks awful.”

“He’s strong,” he says, and it sounds like he’s trying to convince both of them. “He’s been through too much to die like this, right? In a coma. That’s not Leon.”

She nods again. But she doesn’t know Chris Redfield. Leon might trust him, but to her, his opinion is worth little. She is, however, surprised he hasn’t arrested her; he might not be dumb enough to shoot, but is he dumb enough to let her go? Maybe he’s just waiting for the right moment. Maybe he’s already called backup.

Whatever.

Ada would love to see someone try to make her leave.

Chris clears his throat. “I don’t know… the extent of what is going on between you two,” he begins. “But I’m sure he appreciates you being here. You should talk to him. One of the nurses told me it can help.”

She looks at him. “Chris.”

It settles in her stomach with certainty she doesn’t think she’s ever felt before. It feels more like finding something that’s always been there more than discovering something new.

“Yeah?”

“I need your help with something.”

No matter what happens, there’s no world where she regrets this. Even if it ends with her dead somewhere, at least she tried. Leon will know she tried.

“We should be focusing on Leon,” he replies, a little distant.

“He clearly doesn’t care if your name is clear or not.”

Ada shakes her head. “Maybe not. But I do.”

“I don’t get it. What do you want? What are you saying?”

“I’m saying take me in. Arrest me—whatever it is you do. I’ll tell you everything I know about Umbrella, about your boy Wesker, about Simmons. Everything. But in return I want a life. An identity.” She points towards Leon. “I want him.”

December, 2016.

Recovery sucks fucking cock.

Leon is almost forty.

He’s almost forty, and a ceiling fell on him.

He thought he knew pain, but, Jesus Christ, was he wrong. It hurts to breathe, to move, to shit. He can’t do anything.

For the first time in his entire life, Leon spends time on his couch; he even gets a new one after discovering the one he had was actually extremely uncomfortable to spend more than twenty consecutive minutes on. His new couch is big and soft and could probably fit eight people. It looks a little out of place in his living room, but that doesn’t matter. What matters is that he has a couch that alleviates his pain instead of aggravating it.

He watches TV. He watches movies. He used to love movies, but he honestly can’t remember the last one he saw.

Sherry comes by, and Ingrid and Helena. They bring gifts from the others at the office—food, baked goods, knitted socks, puzzles, and other crafts he could do. Someone gets him a book with workouts you can do sitting down. Leon chucks that one in the bin and starts a puzzle on the coffee table while starting another Lord of the Rings marathon.

The food and the baked goods are his favourites. He’s never been a good cook, and now he can barely make it to the kitchen, much less stand up for long periods of time.

And his new sedentary, pastry-filled lifestyle… Well, it starts to show.

Leon was always a skinny kid, then he put on muscle in the academy, then even more so in the military. Keeping said muscle required a strict diet that Leon followed somewhat religiously, mostly because it was just easy to eat what someone told him to. But now he’s been home for months, having his fill of muffins and cakes, and slowly but surely, it’s softened him.

A thin but noticeable layer of soft fat hugs his thighs and his midsection, making his washboard abs nothing more than a memory. Few of his pants still fit properly.

Leon loves it.

He’s not sure why.

But he loves it.

If Sherry, Helena, or Ingrid notice or care, they don’t say anything. Leon kind of wants someone to say something, though.

Ada would notice. But he hasn’t heard from her in a year. Which would normally be fine— something he could live with, at least—but he almost died. He spent five weeks in a coma, months in the hospital.

Why hasn’t Ada visited him?

She’s alive. The girls have assured him as much, but if they know more, they refuse to share it.

But why hasn’t she come to see him?

It’s hard not to worry. Is she alive, but injured or locked up somewhere? Was the proposal too much and she’s decided she doesn’t want anything to do with him? Did she disappear like she said she wanted to?

Leon hasn’t seen Chris Redfield in months either.

Is there a link there? Though why would that even be?

God, as long as Chris hasn’t taken it upon himself to bring Ada in. Leon could see it, though. Chris globetrotting in Ada’s faint footsteps, eyes on his prize. But why Ada? It just doesn’t make sense.

He ponders that, again, halfway through a 3000-piece puzzle with The Martian playing on the TV. But his phone vibrates, pulling him out of his thoughts.

He almost laughs when he sees Chris’ name on the caller ID.

Pausing the movie, he answers the call. “What a surprise.”

The first thing Chris does is clear his throat. “Uh. Hello.”

“Someone got you at gunpoint?” Leon asks.

“Metaphorically, yeah,” he mutters. “Listen. Don’t kill me.”

“Not the best way to open a conversation, I don’t think.”

Chris sighs. “Ada Wong. I’ve had her in custody since July.”

Leon stands up, too fast and too slow. He still hurts when he moves, especially so suddenly, but he’s already on his way out the door. It might be ten p.m. and snowing, but hell if he’s waiting another second to see her.

Blood is pumping through his body faster than it’s had to in months. He’s almost dizzy from rising so fast, and his breathing picks up.

“What do you mean?” he snaps, collecting his keys and shoving his feet into a pair of boots that don’t need lacing.

“It’s a long story. And not my fault. Write that down, Leon. Put that shit on a Post-it and stick it to your windshield. Stare at it when you drive over. It was not my idea. So don’t kill me.”

“Whose bright fucking idea was it, then?” he asks. He forgoes a jacket and heads towards the elevators to take him down to the garage.

“Ada’s,” he says. “Again. Long story.”

“Why tell me now?”

“We’re letting her go. Well… Sort of. Long story. Just get over here. I know you’re already on your way.”

“You bet I am.”

Leon hangs up without another word.

The weather is bad, and he drives way too fast and way too carelessly. At least traffic is sparse, and despite snow and poor visibility, he makes it to the BSAA’s D.C. facility in record time. He doesn’t even bother parking, just pulls up to the main entrance and gets out and blames it on the fact that he can’t walk very long distances before the pain amps up.

The building is largely dark, but Chris is waiting for him in the lobby.

If he notices Leon’s changed body, he doesn’t comment on it. Perhaps because he’s too busy looking like a kid who got caught with a hand in the candy bowl. Chris Redfield is by no means a small man, but in that moment, he probably wishes he was.

He’s leaning on the empty reception desk, trying to school his expression into something casual, but Leon sees through it.

“Good evening,” he says. “Did you stop at a single red on the way over?”

“Been better. Been worse.”

He nods, a little stilted. “I know what you mean. Well. Follow me, then.”

His body hurts, and his heart is beating so fast he worries he’ll die before he makes it to Ada.

He can’t even begin to fathom what she’s doing in BSAA custody or how long she’s been here. In D.C., his fucking city. Has Chris known all this time? Is that why he’s been so good at avoiding him? Leon had chalked it up to pity, that Chris had just felt bad because of Leon’s injuries and subsequent months-long recovery away from work and the field.

But this?

It’s beyond anything Leon could have imagined.

Finally, Chris stops outside a door. He holds up a keycard but doesn’t move to actually use it. Instead, he looks at Leon.

“There are cameras in there,” he says.

“Reunions,” he says. “Emotions run high.”

“Ada, too?”

“She’s being released tomorrow.”

“Tell me when; I’ll pick her up.”

Sheepishly, Chris shakes his head. “I’m afraid it’s not that kind of release.”

Leon’s eyes narrow. He’s torn between bursting through the unlocked door and getting answers.

“Right,” he says on an exhale. “Sure.”

He opens the door.

Steps through it.

Shuts it behind him.

There’s not a lot of time to reflect on that, though, because Ada stands up from the edge of the bed and stares at him.

“Leon,” she says, her eyes fixed on his body. Her lips part a little.

He’s stuck by the door, rooted to the spot.

Going months, even years, without seeing Ada is not new. But he almost died. He thought he’d never see her again.

Now, she’s right in front of him.

“How have you… been?” she asks, not moving closer.

“Why are you here?” Leon asks before he can really think through what his first words should be.

“Long story.”

“I have nowhere to be.”

She sighs, the way she always does, and finally approaches him. “Leon.”

“I’m not that rookie cop anymore, Ada. You can tell me things.”

She takes his hand in hers, and, honestly, she might as well have kissed him. It feels so good to touch her again.

He stumbles a little, needing to be closer to her, and she catches him easily. Her arms wrap around him, pulling him in tight.

“I will tell you,” she says, softer, lips almost right by his ear when he tucks his face into her neck. “You just have to trust me.”

He nods. “I do trust you,” he says, voice a little shaky. He might not be that rookie cop anymore, but he sort of feels like it. He feels unsteady, unprepared. He feels inexperienced and young, in mind if not in body. But just like in Raccoon City, Ada has his back.

“Good,” she hums. “You wanna sit down?”

They sit down on the small couch in the corner, a little closer than necessary. Leon wastes no time tucking into Ada’s side, face pressing back into the crook of her neck, inhaling her scent.

“I missed you,” he says. Later, he’ll be embarrassed by this humiliating need. “You didn’t come see me.”

“I did,” Ada says. “But you were still in a coma.”

“And Chris arrested you?”

“I went willingly.”

He pulls back and looks at her. “Why would you do that?”

“Just trust me,” she says again. “I had a plan.”

“I could have needed you.”

Her eyes flick down his body. “You look fine to me.”

He winces a little. Ada hasn’t said anything, but she’s looking now—openly. Her dark eyes stare shamelessly at his thicker thighs and softer stomach.

“I got a new couch,” Leon says, as if that’s the reason for his new weight.

“It suits you.”

“What?”

“It looks good on you,” she hums. “Healing looks good on you.”

“It’s not healing; it’s chocolate muffins and carrot cake,” Leon huffs. “I’ve just been home a lot and —“

Her hand clasps the back of his neck, and she pulls him into a kiss—deeper than he expected. Deeper than anything she’s initiated in the past.

When she pulls back, he’s panting a little, staring at her with wide eyes.

“It suits you,” she repeats. “You look good, Leon.”

He blinks, a little stunned. “If you say so.”

“I do.”

That reminds him: “Marry me.”

She smiles a little. “No.”

“Is it because you’re in here? I’ll get you out.”

“I’m exactly where I want to be, okay?”

He sighs. “I don’t understand. Why would Chris arrest you?”

“I told him to,” Ada says simply. “And I told him not to tell you.”

“But… why?”

Ada’s actions have never been particularly confusing to him. Even when she “betrayed” him in Raccoon City, it wasn’t very hard to figure out why or what her motives could have been.

This, though, is confusing.

“It was time to stop hiding,” she says. “I had wanted to for a long time.”

Leon isn’t often selfish. At least he doesn’t think so. He struggles to believe people care for him, he always has. Margaret says it’s because his parents died and he was in the foster system for so long, but what does she know, really?

But with Ada he feels like he can be at least a little selfish. For him, she’s broken probably every rule she’s ever set up for herself. She’s saved his life, betrayed clients and buyers for him; that’s an exaggeration, and he knows it, but he’s staring right at her, and all he can think about is how she slipped out of his hand in Raccoon City. How she found him in the alley, how she pulled him out of it when he needed it the most.

She hasn’t said it, not in so many words, but Leon knows that she loves him.

So, for once, Leon is selfish, and he says, “You did it for me.”

“For me,” Ada insists, but Leon can see the truth in her eyes. He knows her, too.

“You got yourself arrested. For me.”

She rolls her eyes, but it all looks so fake now. A show she’s putting on to trick him and keep him at arm’s length.

It isn’t working.

“Don’t be…” she trails off, fumbling, frustrated. “No, Leon.”

She’s angry, but probably more at herself for failing to keep her plan hidden; because when she looks at him over the rim of her glass, gulping down water, there’s a softness to her eyes. “When did you get smart?” she asks, putting the glass back down with a thud.

“I’m a grown man, Ada.”

“But you couldn’t always… do that.”

“Read between your words?”

“Sure.”

He shrugs. “I know you.”

Ada nods a little. “You do.”

“And I love you.”

Their eyes meet. A small smile tugs on Ada’s lips.

“You do.”

“Do you? Love me?”

“Yes, Leon. I do.”

He glances around the room, a warm feeling settling in his stomach. A kind of content he can never feel with anyone else.

“Do you need me to break you out of here?” he asks, because he has to at least ask.

She huffs a laugh. “No. If I didn’t want to be here, I wouldn’t.”

“Chris said you were being released, but not that kind of release.”

“They’re moving me to the BSAA HQ in London. They’re done with me here.”

“And what is this all leading up to?”

Ada shrugs. “I’m not sure. A trial, maybe? They’ve been interviewing and interrogating me for months. But, God, these lawyers are slow.”

“Oh?”

She hums. “When I told Chris to keep my… situation… under wraps, I thought I’d have to be locked up for three months. Turns out the BSAA’s timeline is more like three years. Or more.”

There’s still something she’s not saying, but Leon can’t really pinpoint what that could be. Maybe what the BSAA wants from her, or what her end goal is. Whatever she’s hiding, he doesn’t want to push anymore.

“Will I be able to visit you?”

“In London?”

“Wherever you are.” He stands up with a grunt. “I think I owe it to you after all the times you’ve visited me.”

“Oh, you owe it to me?” she asks. When she approaches him again, there’s a slight smirk on her lips.

“Obviously I want to, too.”

“I will look forward to it.” Her hands settle on his cheeks. “And don’t you dare lose that weight again.”

He frowns, throwing a glance down his own body. “What?”

“You’re healthy, Leon. You’re resting and taking care of yourself. I like that.”

“You… like it?”

“I like it.”

01. KENNEDY, LEON S., 2017: The Visits.

Years ago, Leon wept to Margaret Linton, questioning how do I know?

How do I know if I love her? If it’s like you say—that my childhood has made me so desperate for connection and that Ada was a very bright light at the end of a very dark tunnel—then how do I know that my feelings aren’t just a byproduct of that?

She’d surprised him by asking why it matters. If he loves Ada, does it matter why?

I guess it doesn’t.

And that had been that. Sort of.

Still, he’s surprised when Ada brings it up. She’s not exactly the… open type, and the room they’re in feels too clinical for such an important question, nothing like the one in D.C. from two weeks prior. It’s also only his first visit. His first time seeing her in London, in the bright, bare interrogation room they’ve been granted for the next hour.

An hour a month—guaranteed—is both more and less than they’ve ever had. Leon isn’t sure what to feel about it.

Ada catches the tennis ball when it rebounds. “I want you to think about this before you answer.”

She sends the ball flying towards the opposite wall. It bounces sharply off it, back towards Leon, sitting next to her—close enough that their thighs are pressed together. He catches the ball but doesn’t throw it again.

“Okay,” he says.

“This,” she says, voice thin. “As juvenile as this sounds. Do you think what we feel is… real? Or is it just us clinging to something good that happened in the midst of something bad?” She pauses, and Leon can sense her looking at him. He keeps his eyes on the tennis ball in his hand. “In Raccoon City, we were both way out of our depth.”

“Yes,” Leon agrees. He doesn’t need to think about it. “But I missed you after you fell. Mourned you, you know? I’ve talked to Maggie about this. Extensively.”

Ada looks away. “What did she say?” she asks.

He sighs. “That I love you. Truly. Maybe it started out as some kind of trauma infatuation, but it grew into something real. But she also said it doesn’t matter how it started. It matters what we do with it.”

“Right.”

“Look, Ada.” He lets the ball go and places a tentative hand on her leg. “I let the world believe I killed the president. I was ready to betray everything for you. I still am. I think that’s love.”

“Or lunacy,” she replies.

Their eyes meet.

“Is it me you’re worried about, or yourself?” he asks. “Your feelings, I mean.”

“I don’t worry,” she replies loftily. “Just like you don’t change, Leon.”

It’s only a month later he finds out why she brought it up.

Because Ada shouldn’t be in custody. She should be free to do whatever she wants. Maybe she would be, if the government hadn’t gotten involved.

“It’s not.”

She hums. “You’d look really pretty with some mascara.”

“Oddly enough, Claire has told me the exact same thing.”

Ada unfurls, shifting until she’s straddling his thighs. Her hands come up to cup his face, thumbs tracing the thin skin beneath his eyes. “I’d put mascara on you then watch you cry it off because I’m fucking you so good.”

Oh.

Leon blinks.

She smiles. “A shame we can’t do anything in here, right?”

It is. Leon’s never been a very sexual being, and he isn’t now either. Sure, he might have fucked his way through at least seventy-five percent of the female recruits in the academy, but he’d been young then. Young and starving for a kind hand. He’s forty soon, and all he wants is Ada.

When he can’t have her, he makes do with his hand.

It’s like a strange routine. Every time after his visit is over, he trudges back to his hotel with a broken heart and jerks off like his life depends on it. He’s not sure it’s very smart, and he knows it’s pathetic. But who’s to judge him?

It’s after their March meeting, with his cock in his hand and his teeth biting down on his fist, that he remembers that Ada was a virgin before him.

In April, he asks her about it.

She stares at him like she wants him dead, cheeks so red they’re almost on par with the dress she’d worn in Spain.

“Who do you suggest I should have slept with?” she bites out.

“No one,” Leon says. They’re on the floor again, but on opposite sides of the room, tossing the tennis ball back and forth. When Ada throws it, he swears she aims for his head, but he catches it and arches an eyebrow. “Just curious.”

“Curiousity kills.”

“Cats, yes. I’m not a cat.”

“Just a government dog.”

“Play nice,” he hums, bouncing the ball back to her.

She catches it. “Yes, I was a virgin. No, I didn’t save myself for you.”

“I didn’t think you did.”

“You think I do everything for you.”

Leon shrugs. “You killed Nathan Wesninski, who killed my family.”

“That wasn’t for you either. I didn’t even know you existed.”

“I know. My point is that you seem to have a habit of doing things that accidentally benefit me.”

“It benefitted you that I was a virgin?” she asks, raising an eyebrow.

“Well, that makes it sound kinda awful.”

“I agree.”

“I just mean that I was glad it was me. That your first time was with someone who loves you. Who, I mean, hopefully, made it good.”

When she throws the ball next time, it’s a lot more gentle. Leon’s palm doesn’t even hurt when he catches it.

“So presumptuous,” Ada says, but her voice is soft enough that he knows she’s saying it was good, you made it good.

Leon looks at the ball in his hand for a moment. “You’ve saved my life plenty of times,” he says lightly. “You’re a good person, Ada.”

She makes a soft noise. “That’s your opinion. A very biased one.”

“Let me testify for you.”

“No.”

“Marry me, then.”

She barks a laugh. “Are those my options?”

“Yes.” He meets her eyes and bounces the ball back.

Without breaking eye contact, she catches it. “I choose neither.”

“Did they set a date?”

“June. End of.”

“Right.”

“I don’t want you there. At all.”

“I don’t think that’s your choice. Not completely.”

And it’s not.

When Leon returns to D.C. the next day, he’s barely stepped off the plane before he’s summoned to the White House. He’s not surprised—it’s a call that’s been a long time coming. President Richards had made it clear since his election that he isn’t Leon’s biggest fan. Leon’s sure that if he could, the president would fire him. But how do you fire the co-founder of the DSO? How do you fire someone who can’t quit?

It’s not surprising when Richards tells him he will be testifying on behalf of the government, against Ada, either.

“It’s important to tell the truth,” Richards says. He’s an older man, plump with leisure and drenched in the smell of expensive tobacco. “I think we can agree on that, Agent Kennedy.”

Leon nods. It’s far from his first time in the Oval Office, but it’s not a room he ever thinks he’ll feel comfortable in. Especially not while standing up; Richard never told him to sit, however.

“We can, sir.”

Richards hums. “You did what you had to in Raccoon City, working with her. But it’s time she faces the consequences. It’s time this evil gets what it deserves.”

He bites back what he wants to say, clenches his fists to keep them from pummelling Richards’ face. “I thought you said it’s important to tell the truth,” he bites out at length.

“And it is.” The president’s eyes narrow. “You will testify at her trial. You will tell the court how she manipulated you in Raccoon City. You will tell them about her work with Neo-Umbrella. You will tell them how she framed you as my predecessors’ murderer. That is the truth, Agent Kennedy.”

“I think truth must be something subjective, sir.”

“Damnit, man!” Richards’ fist slams into the table. “It’s bad enough you go see her once a month. Bad enough she handed herself over in your hospital room. It doesn’t paint a good picture, Kennedy. Not at all! How many state secrets has she wrung from you, huh? Do you understand that that is the question people will have when they learn of this? There is one truth, my truth. That truth is best for you, for America, for everyone.”

“Not for her.”

“She doesn’t matter. She is a terrorist, Kennedy. When we win this case, she will be locked up for life. And so help me God, we will win it. Your honest testimony will help us.”

Leon stares at the president and wonders if the man really believes Leon is so easily bought. Especially when it comes to Ada.

“Alright, sir.” He just wants to leave. Go home and sleep away jet lag. There’s no point in arguing, anyway. It’s the fucking president.

Richards points a chubby finger at Leon. “If you betray me, if you betray your country, things can get very ugly for you, Agent Kennedy.”

“I’m sure,” Leon says. “I think it can get ugly for you, too, Mr. President.”

“Are you…” He at least has the decency to look stunned. “Threatening me?”

“Of course not, sir,” Leon says easily. “Why? Were you threatening me?”

“Get out,” Richards snaps. “Go make yourself useful.”

When Leon goes to London a month later, after thirty days of vaguely mean-spirited emails from the president, he stops the little blonde they have working Ada’s case before she can let them into the restricted section of the HQ.

“I want to testify. For her,” he tells her. Sophie, her name is. She’s young, and Ada thinks the only reason she’s on the case is because she’s stupid enough to think they’ll win.

Sophie lets out a deep breath. “Thank you.”

Leon smiles a little at her reaction. “Ada doesn’t want me to.”

“She doesn’t have to know until you’re up there.”

He nods. “Okay.”

“You will help her. A lot. If the little she’s told us in interviews is true, then… Yeah. Thank you, Agent Kennedy.”

Ada, unsurprisingly, does not want to talk about the trial. Rather, she wants to talk about his hair.

“You were blond in Spain,” she says, showing him a picture of himself from 2004.

Leon winces a little at the sight, taking the photo in hand. “I was.”

She purses her lips. “You’re not a blond.”

“Sorry,” he huffs. “I am.”

“Do you want a mirror? There’s a huge one right behind you.”

“I am blond. In the summer.”

“It wasn’t summer when we met in Spain.”

He sighs, and humiliatingly he can feel his cheeks flush. “I didn’t dye it, if that’s what you think.”

“I didn’t say that,” Ada hums. “Did the sun bleach it?”

“Krauser had us outside more often than not. He had us tanning, too, so that spending long hours in the sun wouldn’t hurt us as bad. And I was blond as a kid, guess it came back.”

“It was cute,” she says, eyes darting between the picture in his hand and his face. “You were cute.”

“Cute?” Leon narrows his eyes at her. “I was not cute.”

“In Raccoon City you were.” And she grins when she says it. “Downright adorable. I mean, why did you think I kissed you?”

“Because I saved your life,” he says, leaning in and stealing a kiss—because he can do that now.

Ada catches his jaw in her hand, holding him in place when he tries to break away.

“Thank you, Leon,” she mumbles against his lips. “I mean that. For everything.”

02. WONG, ADA., 2017: The Interviews.

“Am I boring you, Ms. Wong?”

Ada clicks her pen and meets Sophie’s eyes.

“No,” Ada says. “Not at all.”

Sophie’s glasses slide down her nose a little, and the look she gives Ada is nothing short of scathing. “At least try to appear like you’re paying attention.”

Next to Sophie, Nari shifts, looking a little uncomfortable.

Together, Sophie and Nari make up Ada’s legal counsel. They’re both younger than her and were definitely assigned to this case as some form of punishment. Or this is their idiotic attempt to ‘get ahead’. Oh, we won United States v. Wong! Look at us! They can keep dreaming—this case is unwinnable, but it seems only Ada knows that.

She sighs. “I just don’t understand how many times I have to tell you that Leon S. Kennedy can’t be allowed anywhere near this.”

Nari clears her throat. “That’s not really your decision. He would be a fantastic character witness for you, if what you’ve told us is true. The fact that he’s going to start visiting you every month is good, yes, but… if we could get him on the stand, vouching for you…”

“No,” Ada snaps. “Absolutely not.”

Sophie makes a frustrated noise. “Why not? Oh my God, give me one reason!”

“He doesn’t even know why I’m here. He thinks I’m doing it for him,” Ada says, rolling her eyes.

“Aren’t you?” Nari asks.

“No,” she snaps. “I’m doing it for myself. So that I can be a real person again.”

Sophie opens her binder and looks between her papers. Ada already knows what she’s about to do, and she groans, feeling a little like a petulant child.

With an arched eyebrow, Sophie reads from a small note she’s dug out in a flat voice, “I’m saying take me in. Arrest me—whatever it is you do. I’ll tell you everything I know about Umbrella, about your boy Wesker, about Simmons. Everything. But in return I want a life. An identity. I want him.”

How had Chris even memorised her words well enough to put them in her file?

Nari hums. “This doesn’t make you look bad, you know that right? It makes you look sympathetic.”

Sophie nods so fast her glasses almost slide off completely.

“We are not involving Leon.” Ada crosses her arms.

Across from her, Sophie and Nari exchange a look. Ada hates them both, but she also sort of loves them. If she had daughters, she’d want them to be just like her two upstart, unafraid lawyers. They’re smart, and they’re crazy enough to actually try to fight for her.

“Listen,” Nari begins. “The U.S. government wants to prosecute the shit out of you, Ada. They want you to take the fall for Neo-Umbrella, not to mention the Wesninski and Raccoon City stuff, and the rest of their ridiculously long list. If you lose and go back into their custody…”

“Who knows what they’ll do,” Sophie says, picking up where Nari faltered. “You need Leon.”

“Leon works for them,” Ada says. “He co-founded the DSO. You expect him to show up as a character witness for the defence in the case United States v. Wong?”

“Yes,” Sophie says.

“If what you’ve told us about your past with him is true, then he will say yes,” Nari continues. “Didn’t he ask you to marry him?”

“Twice,” Sophie answers for Ada.

Nari nods. “If you weren’t literally stateless, I’d say marry him now. Then he can’t be made to testify against you.”

Ada looks between them.

There’s a knot in her stomach that refuses to loosen, no matter what happens in her case. If anything, it only tightens every day that passes.

When she told Chris to bring her in, she hadn’t expected this. She’d expected a couple of months in custody, sharing her knowledge, and then that’d be it. The BSAA and the UN would thank her by sponsoring her path to citizenship. Maybe that’s what would have happened, if the U.S. government hadn’t found out the BSAA had her.

Of course, they couldn’t possibly let ‘terrorist Ada Wong’ get off so easily. Of course, they had to make a thing out of it.

A trial.

At least the BSAA has her back. Why? Ada isn’t sure.

“His first visit is coming up in a couple of days,” Sophie says. “Ask him then.”

Ada does not ask him. She relishes instead in seeing him looking healthy. He’s always been attractive, but it’s getting ridiculous now. He’s big all over, and for some reason, his voice is deeper and more rough. Ada wants to hear him whine in her ear while he fucks the daylights out of her, which feels strange for a multitude of reasons. She’s forty, first of all. And she’s never had a need for sex in her life, not even in the immediate aftermath of their rendezvous in 2015.

She thinks maybe it’s her body trying to deflect. Everything around her is stressful, awful, so why not plead for something nice? But in the end, it only makes everything worse, because it’s not like Leon can fuck her on the floor of the dinky interrogation room they’ve been given.

Apart from seeing Leon once a month, there’s not a lot to look forward to. Every day is just talking, talking, talking. To Sophie and Nari, mostly, and their group of four terrified interns or assistants— Ada’s not sure what they are, just they they’re even younger.

Chris comes to see her whenever he’s in London, which isn’t often. But at least he apologises for China—finally. He even has the decency to look earnest about it.

“Did Leon put you up to this?” she asks him.

They’re not in the same room she sees Leon in. No, when Chris visits, he gets to come to her room, which is nice enough and at least has windows. Perhaps the BSAA doesn’t think there’s a risk of… fornication with Chris.

Chris scratches the back of his neck. “No,” he says.

Ada arches an eyebrow. “How two horrible liars like you and him made it so far up the ranks of anti-terrorist organisations is a question for the ages.”

“We value honesty and truth,” he says.

“Remind me again how many times the BSAA has been infiltrated by a mole. And how many attacks the U.S. government has lied about and covered up.”

He leaves after that, and Ada is alone again. She’s alone most of the time, which isn’t a change, really. What’s different is that she has nothing she’s waiting for, no mission right at hand.

All she can do is sit and wait for the next time someone wants her to talk.

Nameless BSAA bureaucrats ask her about her past, and she tells them, because she has to. But there’s so much of it she’s forgotten. Like the name of the village she grew up in. The name of her childhood best friend, her teachers, her family. Ada realises, in telling it, that the story of her past is almost as made up as her present is. It’s so streamlined and void of details, so focused on being taken by Wesninski that she’s let everything that happened before wither away.

When Leon shows up in February, Ada is furious. With him, with Nari and Sophie, with the BSAA and the government, and everything and everyone. It feels like a mistake—all of it. Every choice that has brought them closer feels like the biggest mistake she’s ever made, even if she knows that’s not true.

But it’s loving Leon Kennedy that brought her here. For good and for bad.

She tries to bring back the feeling she had in Leon’s hospital room—the feeling that no matter what, there’s no way she could regret handing herself in because she’s doing it for him. It gets harder and harder every goddamn time.

They keep prodding. Asking questions they’ve asked her already. Why did she kill Wesninski? Why was she with him in the first place?

What happened in Raccoon City? Why was she there, who was she working for?

Why was she in Spain?

They ask her about Albert Wesker and Simmons and Carla and Leon.

And every question eats and eats and eats her alive.

Leon’s visits are the only good thing she has, but an hour a month isn’t enough. It’s a mitten provided to shield her from a snowstorm, and she’s freezing to death while everyone watches her from the safety of a warm, cozy cabin.

“Why can’t I see more of him?” Ada asks her two lawyers the day before Leon’s March visit.

“You’re not really supposed to be allowed to see him at all,” Sophie says, mustering as much compassion as she can, which isn’t that much. Not that compassion is what Ada needs. “The BSAA is only allowing it because of your compliance last year.”

She traces a line in the wooden table with a fingernail. “I want to see more of him.”

“That’s not possible,” Nari says.

And, apparently, it’s not. Not even Chris seems to be able to do anything about it.

So Ada spends her free time planning an attack on the HQ. One she’ll never perform, of course, but still. It feels like a good mental workout. And stress relief. It almost brings a smile to her face, imagining the whole building going boom. This place where all the nightmares that have haunted her since she was taken from her home were resurrected, played back in ultra-HD.

They offer her a therapist, but Ada declines. It would just mean more talking. Besides, she’s not ready for that yet—she’s not as brave as Leon is.

In March, he proposes again, and Ada feels sick when she tells him no—again. It isn’t even a very serious proposal, yet she’s nauseous with grief when she rejects him. The ring is in her room, one of very, very few possession she has these days. Well, she’s never had a lot. Not that matters, at least. Now everything she has suddenly matters, because it’s all from Leon.

The ring in its velvety box, the necklace—taken out of storage just so she can keep it on her bedside table—and a CD she stole from his apartment ages ago. She can’t even play it; it just sits next to the necklace, and she stares at it every night before she falls asleep.

She sleeps poorly. Probably hasn’t gotten a full eight hours since July. There’s too much to think about.

Why is she the only one who can see that this case is screwed? The BSAA is backed by the UN, sure. But what is the UN against the American government, really? Not a lot, considering how many votes have been completely fucked over by the use of a veto.

Nari and Sophie persevere; they keep fighting to improve her odds, to build a case in her favour— one that could get her off every single hook. Killing Wesninski was self defence, and everything she’s done after was a result of how he treated her, what he trained her for; she never manipulated Leon, she fell in love with him; Albert Wesker forced her to do the work she did for him; Neo- Umbrella wasn’t her, it was Carla.

It’s a case built on equal parts truths and lies, and only Ada seems to be able to see how unstable it is.

“It isn’t,” Nari assures her a few days after Leon’s visit. “We can win this. It’s not like we have to convince the government you’re innocent. It’s a jury, Ada. Made up of regular people.”

They’re in a huge conference room, just Ada, Sophie, Nari and their four little helpers. The long table is covered in files and stacks of papers.

“And what you’ve gone through is enough to make anyone feel sick to their stomach,” Sophie adds from the end of the table. “They will be sympathetic. Would be even more so if Le—“

“No.” Ada levels Sophie with a sharp look.

“He’ll be there anyway,” she says. “As a witness for the prosecution.”

She—somehow—hadn’t considered that before. But of course it makes sense. He was in Raccoon City with her, and in Spain, even if his reports mysteriously forgot to mention her presence there.

“He can’t speak for me,” Ada says anyway.

“You’re worried about him,” Nari says. “I get that. But he’s already risked his career for you—in China? He’s risking it now, too, by just coming here every month.”

“Well, he’s an idiot.” Ada crosses her arms over her chest. “He always has been, and he’s incapable of changing.”

Sophie and Nari exchange a look—one that’s become very common between them—then Sophie sighs and tosses a file in Ada’s direction.

“Look at his file at least? Maybe there’s something in there you can tell us more about. Something we can ask him about when he’s on the stand.”

The file is thick and full of things that Ada doesn’t want to know. She skims every fourth page, grateful that they’re so heavily redacted it’s questionable why they’re in there at all. The photos, however, are somewhat interesting. She spends too long looking at a picture that was taken seemingly right in the aftermath of Raccoon City.

A rookie cop shouldn’t have had to go through all of that. Especially not what she put him through. But would he have survived without her? There’s no real use in wondering, but she can’t help it.

And if a picture of him taken right before Spain disappears from the folder and falls down the loose sleeve of her sweater, no one will ever be any the wiser. Probably.

They set her court date for end of June. Good. She’s so sick of the BSAA HQ she needs to get out. Even if it’s just onto a plane and straight into a courtroom in D.C. Sophie and Nari remain optimistic. Leon, too.

She wishes she could shake all of them and make them see how futile it is.
