03.01. The Trial; Prosecution.

The following excerpts are taken from the official court transcripts and proceedings in the matter of United States v. Wong, Case No. 1:17-CVR398, United States District Court. The transcripts have been reproduced in part and presented as entered into the official record.

Excerpt from Trial Proceedings Witness: Dr. Rebecca Chambers Examining Attorney: Oscar Jenkins, Assistant United States Attorney Examination: Direct Examination by the Prosecution A: Ms. Wong has been in close proximity to several strains and variants of Umbrella-made viruses. It is a common misconception that the t-Virus creates zombies and that that is its goal. But the purpose of the t-Virus is to create the so-called ‘Tyrants’, the ultimate bioweapon for military use, according to Umbrella. The zombie-like state is caused by the lack of a certain gene. It’s a whole thing and maybe too complex for today, but… What I mean to say is that it’s not entirely unlikely that Ms. Wong’s proximity to these viruses have caused her to… change. Q: Change? A: Yes. Mentally, if not physically. Not in a way that would be noticeable, perhaps. Rather, just a case of heightened aggression, reduced compassion. I haven’t examined Ms. Wong; I’m not that kind of doctor, really. Q: But you know biochemistry. You know bioweapons. A: I do. Q: And? A: Like I said, it’s very possible Ms. Wong has been altered by her work. Q: To behave more like a bioweapon? That’s what you mean? SOPHIE HUNTER: Objection. Leading the witness. THE COURT: Overruled. Please answer, Dr. Chambers. A: Colder? More ruthless? Yes. That’s what I mean.

········

Ashley Graham never expected she’d be here. She’s been in a lot of strange places, but on the stand about to testify against Ada Wong has to be one of the strangest—which is saying a lot considering what happened in 2004.

When her father was president, she joined him on a state visit to Australia where a koala gave her chlamydia. This is weirder.

“Ms. Graham,” the lawyer before her begins. His name is Oscar Jenkins, and he has a smug little smile Ashley finds incredibly annoying. His voice is smooth, though, and he’s definitely attractive. A smart choice for a case like this. “I am so sorry to have to call you like this, to make you rehash the traumatic events that occurred in the Valdelobos mountains.”

“It’s okay,” she says.

Her eyes drift to Ada Wong—older now, obviously. Ashley knows little of her, other than what Leon told her back then. Sometimes, still, she dreams of what Leon told her on their way back to the mainland. The tone of his voice, how adamant he’d been, comes to her as clearly as it did on the day.

Do not tell anyone about her.

Jenkins clears his throat. “Was Ms. Wong present in the region at the same time as you were?”

Ashley looks down. “Yes.”

“Do you know why she was there?”

“If I understood correctly, it was to, uh, steal a sample of the virus the cultists were using.”

Jenkins nods, solemn. He paces a few steps. “Ada Wong was there in the employ of Albert Wesker, working to steal Las Plagas. Which she ultimately succeeded with, correct?”

“I think so, yes,” Ashley mumbles.

“Now, interestingly enough, there are no mentions of Ms. Wong’s presence in any follow-up reports, but we have established she was there and why. Did you have interactions with her, Ms. Graham?”

“I did not.” At least that’s the truth.

“But Agent Kennedy did. Did he tell you about them?”

She’s glad Leon isn’t in the room. That he’s waiting behind the door she just emerged from. If he was there, listening, Ashley isn’t sure she’d be able to continue.

“He told me she came at him with a gun. That she used him multiple times in her pursuit of the Plaga.”

“And how do you think Agent Kennedy’s mission to save you would have gone if Ms. Wong hadn’t been present? Would he have succeeded faster, with no… distractions?”

“Objection,” one of Ada’s lawyers says, rolling her eyes. “Leading the witness. Again.”

“Sustained,” the judge says.

“Apologies, Your Honor,” Jenkins says, hand on his chest. “Let me rephrase. How would the mission have gone without Ms. Wong’s presence?”

Ashley looks at Ada again. She has her head down, and her hands are fidgeting with something beneath the table.

When she speaks, she’s at last able to put some power into her words. These ones, she actually believes. “If I understood Agent Kennedy correctly, we would have died without her help. Both of us. Or, well, I guess… He would have died. I would have been shipped back to my father, and their plan would have succeeded. Ada Wong saved Leon’s life, and she saved America, too.”

Immediately, the courtroom bursts into murmurs.

Ada looks up, and their eyes meet across the room. There’s a slight furrow between her brows that Ashley can’t read.

Jenkins’ lips part, his eyes narrow into a subtle glare.

“Order,” the judge says, slamming the gavel. “Order, please.”

After that, Jenkins has no further questions for her.

········

Excerpt from Trial Proceedings Witness: Robert Edding Examining Attorney: Oscar Jenkins, Assistant United States Attorney Examination: Direct Examination by the Prosecution Q: Mr. Edding, is it correct that Ms. Wong has been paying you to stalk Leon Kennedy for 16 years? A: I think ‘stalking’ is a phrase that goes best unused. Q: What would you call it? A: Ada--Ms. Wong, that is—hired me to look after Leon Kennedy. Q: What did that entail? A: Well. I made sure he stayed out of trouble. Told her if he failed to do so. Q: You were arrested several times for this; is that correct? A: Never because of Leon, even if he knew. Q: But you were arrested? A: Six times, yes. I never served time. Can’t serve time if the victim don’t see themselves as a victim. See, I think Leon Kennedy liked having me around. Q: Stay on subject, please, Mr. Edding. A: I think that was very relevant. You hear that, jury? Leon Kennedy wanted to be--pardon my language--stalked.

········

Chris Redfield has better things to do than this. He’s been in countless interrogations, countless of times. Usually, he is the one asking the questions. The one demanding answers from people who have ruined entire cities in the name of progress. He has looked monsters in the eye and seen no remorse staring back.

But Ada Wong is not a monster.

And if he had a choice, Chris wouldn’t be here to testify that she is.

He wasn’t that surprised when she handed herself in. Someone like her would have known Leon would be under guard, especially after what happened to him. Still, she risked breaking into his room with a fake identity and concern as her only disguise.

She knew the risks and came anyway.

“Captain Redfield,” Oscar Jenkins says. His expression is practiced sympathy, the kind politicians wear during speeches. “Thank you for being here today.”

Chris gives a short nod. “Of course.”

“Let’s begin with your history with the defendant. You first became aware of Ada Wong through your work with the BSAA, correct?”

“Correct.”

“And would you consider her a criminal?”

The question is almost laughably simple. “Yes.”

Jenkins smiles. “Direct. I appreciate that.” When Chris says nothing, he continues. “Ms. Wong has been involved with the trafficking and theft of bioweapons. She has worked with known terrorists, including Albert Wesker. She has repeatedly obstructed international anti-bioterror efforts. Is that accurate?”

“Yes.”

“And in June of 2016, you personally took Ms. Wong into custody after she unlawfully entered Agent Leon Kennedy’s hospital room.”

“I did.”

“She infiltrated a secure government facility.”

“Yes.”

“She violated a federal agent’s privacy while he was in a vulnerable state.”

“Yes.”

Jenkins nods slowly, as though he has already won.

There’s a pause, a small one. But when Jenkins opens his mouth to speak again, Chris interrupts.

“She handed herself over. I think the sight of Agent Kennedy hit a nerve.”

Jenkins’ expression changes. The courtroom goes quiet enough that when the jury members begin shifting uncomfortably in their chairs, the noise is deafening.

“Nevertheless, she was trespassing,” Jenkins says.

“Yes.”

“She was breaking the law.”

“Yes.”

“And she was there without Agent Kennedy’s knowledge or consent.”

Chris looks toward the defense table. Ada isn’t looking at him. She has spent most of the trial avoiding everyone’s eyes, like she has already accepted whatever verdict is coming.

“Probably not,” he says.

Jenkins levels him with a look Chris doesn’t want to interpret. “Probably?”

“He knew she’d come eventually.”

A quiet murmur spreads through the courtroom.

“Captain, are you suggesting Agent Kennedy expected Ms. Wong to break into his hospital room?”

Chris shifts, crossing one leg over the other. The suit he’s wearing is bordering on too small, the seams taut against his body. “I’m saying I’ve known Leon Kennedy for a very long time.”

“And?”

“Ada Wong has a habit of showing up for him.”

A few people laugh, but the judge immediately bangs the gavel, calling for order again.

Jenkins’ jaw tightens. “Captain Redfield, would you say Ada Wong is dangerous?”

“Yes.”

“Would you trust her?”

“No.” The answer comes immediately, and Jenkins looks relieved.

“So you agree she poses a threat.”

“No.”

The relief disappears in a second.

Chris sighs. “I said I don’t trust her. I don’t trust her because she doesn’t tell anyone what she’s doing. She disappears for years. She works alone. She lies. And yet, every time she’s had the opportunity to unleash a bioweapon on the world, she hasn’t.” The room is silent once more. “Every time she’s had the opportunity to walk away and let everyone else die, she hasn’t.”

“So what exactly are you saying, Captain?”

Chris thinks of Raccoon City. Of Wesker. Of the people who created monsters because they believed they could control them.

Then he thinks of Ada Wong, standing in a hospital hallway with her hands out, finally deciding she was done running—out of love for Leon Kennedy, Chris’ friend.

“I’m saying I’ve spent my whole life fighting monsters.” His eyes drift briefly to Ada. “She isn’t one of them.”

Oscar Jenkins does not ask another question.

········

Excerpt from Trial Proceedings Witness: Dr. Alicent Tower Examining Attorney: Oscar Jenkins, Assistant United States Attorney Examination: Direct Examination by the Prosecution Q: Dr. Tower, could you please state your qualifications for the record. A: I hold a doctorate in international security studies with a specialisation in biological warfare and counter-bioterrorism. For the past seventeen years, I have advised the United States Department of Defense and several international task forces on the proliferation of Bio Organic Weapons. BOWs for short. Q: In simple terms, what has been the greatest challenge in the global fight against bioterrorism? A: Availability. A weapon can only be used if someone possesses it. Following the collapse of Umbrella Corporation, there was a brief period during which the international community believed we had contained the greatest threat humanity had ever created. Q: And were they correct? A: No. Because the science survived. Viruses, parasites, genetic research, manufacturing processes —all of it entered the black market. Governments, insurgent groups, extremist organisations, and private military companies suddenly had access to technology capable of killing hundreds of thousands. Q: And where does Ada Wong fit into this? A: Ms. Wong represents one of the greatest failures of international counter-bioterror strategy. SOPHIE HUNTER: Objection. Argumentative. THE COURT: Overruled. The witness may explain. A: For nearly two decades, Ms. Wong operated as a highly skilled independent contractor specialising in the acquisition of biological assets. She infiltrated outbreaks, research facilities, and active conflict zones, often before government forces could secure them.

Q: What was the consequence of that? A: Every time a sample disappears, every time a laboratory is compromised, every time an organisation acquires a strain it should never have possessed, the clock resets. Q: What do you mean by that? A: Counter-bioterrorism is not only about killing terrorists. It is about removing their ability to continue fighting. You destroy their weapons, you eliminate their research, you cut off supply chains. Q: And Ms. Wong prevented that? A: Repeatedly. For example, in 2004, she infiltrated the Valdelobos region and obtained a dominant Las Plagas specimen that would later become the foundation for further black-market research. Q: Would it be fair to say that her actions prolonged the bioterror conflict? A: In my professional opinion, yes. Q: By how much? A: It is impossible to calculate precisely. Years, perhaps decades. Q: So when people describe Ada Wong as someone who saves lives— SOPHIE HUNTER: Objection. THE COURT: Overruled. Q: You may answer. A: I do not deny that she has, on occasion, prevented catastrophes. But the issue is not whether she has saved lives. Q: Then what is the issue, in your opinion, Doctor? A: The issue is that she has also ensured the next catastrophe had the opportunity to exist.

········

Excerpt from Trial Proceedings Witness: Mary Wesninski Examining Attorney: Oscar Jenkins, Assistant United States Attorney Examination: Direct Examination by the Prosecution Q: Mrs. Wesninski, for the record could you state your name and occupation. A: Mary Anne Wesninski. I am a former social worker; retired. Q: Are you related to Mr. Nathan Wesninski? A: I was his wife. Q: Thank you. Ms. Wesninski, do you know the defendant, Ada Wong? A: Yes, I know her. I knew her from age 10 to 18. Q: In what capacity? A: My husband and I took her into our care. Q: Could you explain what that means, that you took her into your care? A: Yes. She was brought to us from circumstances that… were not appropriate for a child. She had no stable home. No consistent schooling. No legal guardianship that could be verified at the time. Q: How did this come to happen? A: Nathan had connections overseas, in China. We were told of a girl in desperate need of a family. So my husband arranged for her relocation to the United States. We ensured she was housed, educated, trained. She lived under our supervision for years. Q: Would it be fair to say she was raised by you and your husband? A: Yes. In every meaningful sense.

Q: And during that time, how would you describe her behavior? A: Quiet. Intelligent. Observant. She learned quickly. Q: Some might characterize your arrangement as… ethically questionable. How would you respond to that? A: I guess I would not argue with that word, sir. Q: But you still believe it was the right thing to do? A: Absolutely. As a social worker, I have seen my fair share of children suffering. I did not want to add to that, Mr. Jenkins. Q: Did Ada Wong ever express gratitude for what you and your husband provided? A: Never. She was ungrateful from day one. She listened, yes. Learned very fast. But she never realised that we had saved her life. Q: This never changed? A: Not in a meaningful way. When she grew older, she became more distant. But she was always angry and resentful. Q: Distant in what way? A: She stopped speaking to us unless necessary. She… did things. Things that did not belong in a household. She grew violent, you see. I’ve never seen so much wrath and hatred in a girl her age. Q: Did you attempt to intervene? A: Nathan did. More than once. Q: And what was the result? A: She left. Q: Permanently? A: Yes. Q: Mrs. Wesninski, are you aware that the defendant later killed Nathan Wesninski? A: I am aware of that allegation. Q: Do you believe it to be true? A: Yes. Absolutely. One hundred thousand percent, Mr. Jenkins. Q: How do you reconcile that with your testimony that you provided her safety and stability? A: We gave her shelter when she had none, and she killed my husband for it. Q: Do you believe she regrets it? A: No. Q: Why not? A: Because she survived. And that’s all she cares about. Q: No further questions.

········

Ada promised herself she wouldn’t look at him when it was his turn. She’s tried so hard to not look at anyone. Not the jury, who carries her life in their underqualifed hands. Not the prosecution’s star lawyer, in his fitted striped suit and gel slicked hair. Not anyone on the witness stand.

But of course it’s different when Oscar Jenkins calls Agent Leon Kennedy onto the stand.

Of course Ada has to look at him.

She can’t keep her eyes off him.

He appears in a suit—brand new, she guesses, because it fits him like a glove. It’s easy to forget in the midst of rubble and blood and death that Leon Kennedy cleans up nice. That there’s a whole other part of his life that has consisted of fancy parties with presidents.

Beneath the table, Nari puts a hand on her knee. “It’s okay,” she whispers. “Whatever he says. You know where his heart is.”

Ada looks at her, horrified beyond comprehension. “What?”

Nari’s eyes are soft. “The case always looks done for after the prosecution is done. We’ve talked about this. They’ll twist his words. But you know he loves you.”

Ada blinks. She can feel her heart rate pick up, her hands getting clammy. Her gaze tears away from Nari but only returns to Leon, which isn’t much better. It’s worse, if anything. Because he’s looking back at her, and there’s a soft smile on his lips.

She watches him put a hand on the bible and swear to tell nothing but the truth.

Oscar Jenkins follows Leon’s gaze towards Ada, and his jaw tenses. He probably hasn’t had the day he expected, but neither has Ada.

“Agent Kennedy,” he begins. “Why don’t you state your qualifications for the record.”

Leon nods, shifting into a more comfortable position on the stand. “Leon Kennedy. DSO special agent. Division of Security Operations, that is. Been there since it was founded, ‘cause I was one of the co-founders. But I suppose I’ve served in federal anti-bioterror operations since 1998. USSTRATCOM, for example.”

Jenkins nods. “And your experience with B.O.W.s?”

“Extensive.”

“Could you elaborate?”

“I was a rookie police officer in Raccoon City during the t-Virus outbreak in September of 1998. Since then, I have participated in multiple domestic and international operations.”

“And over the course of those operations, have you become familiar with the defendant, Ada Wong?”

“Yes. I’d say so.”

She swallows so thickly she thinks the whole room must hear it.

Apparently, it’s one thing to think one is ready to hear a thing and another to actually hear it. But she hopes Leon lies through his teeth. She hopes he says Ada has never been anything but a threat. She hopes he speaks about how she manipulated him in ‘98 and ditched him in ‘04. She hopes he leaves out every moment in between, every word spoken when really there should have been an arrest, a fight, an escape.

“Can you tell us more about your relationship to Ms. Wong? Where did you first meet her? When have your paths crossed?”

“I met Ada in Raccoon City. In ‘98. She told me she was an FBI agent looking for the t-Virus sample.”

Even though Leon is about to continue, Jenkins cuts in. “She lied to you?”

“She did,” Leon says.

“Is it correct she… kissed you?”

“It is.”

“Did she use you, manipulate you?”

“Yes.”

Ada presses her fingers to her lips. Nari squeezes her leg again.

“Ada, you okay?” she whispers.

But Ada isn’t crying.

She’s trying to hide a smile.

Maybe she’s been wrong about him. Maybe he can change. Maybe he can look out for himself for once. Maybe his selfishnes can manifest in a way that actually benefits him.

“But you’ve met her more than once. Sorry, please continue.”

“I met her again in an… official… capacity in 2004. When I was sent to Spain to rescue Ashley Graham.”

“Did Ms. Wong use the same tactics there? Emotional manipulation, that is.”

Leon tilts his head side to side. “Perhaps.”

“Perhaps?”

“Ada likes to wear this persona. That she’s untouchable and cold. Uncaring. And that’s how she behaved in Spain, I suppose. She made it very clear that her mission was her priority, and she often used me to get what she wanted.”

She wishes she could kiss him. Smush his face in her hands and thank him.

“So, Ms. Wong used you, manipulated you, multiple times. Is that correct?”

“Yes.”

“What is he doing?” Sophie mutters under her breath. She’s drumming a pen frantically against the table. “Why isn’t he saying anything?”

“Quiet,” Nari hushes. “We knew this could happen.”

“Did we?”

Ada shushes them. “Let me listen.”

“We’ll get to the events in Lanshiang in a moment, but I wanted to just quickly ask how you felt learning that Ms. Wong had broken into your hospital room. That she’d violated your privacy in that manner…” Jenkins trails off, gaze falling to the floor in faux sympathy.

But Leon laughs. “Relieved,” he says. “Not at first maybe. But I hadn’t heard from her in six months. I was beginning to worry. So when Chris Redfield called and said she’d been in BSAA custody… I was angry at first, but it quickly became relief. I was relieved she was alive.”

The jury—the whole courtroom—starts murmuring again, like a group of children.

It all fades away, though, because Ada can feel it all slipping.

All of it.

Jenkins’s doesn’t even know what to say. His jaw works, his hands fidget at his hands before he clasps them before himself. “Relieved?” he asks thinly.

Leon nods. “I love her, you know? I was worried she’d died. I wouldn’t have… I don’t think I would have been able to take that. So. Yes, I was relieved. Third time’s the charm, I guess, but I’ll say it again, if you’d like?”

The murmurs grows, becomes full on conversation. Full on outrage.

What does he mean? What does he mean he loves her?

The question moves around the room like wildfire, and Ada sits in the midst of it, letting the flames eat her alive.

What a complete fucking idiot he is.

Why must they both burn? Why would he throw himself onto the pyre just because she was already tied to it?

Anger, complete and all encompassing, burns beneath her skin, boiling her blood. Boiling away common sense.

“Leon,” she says, loud enough that he hears her over the commotion the judge is trying fruitlessly to stop. Their eyes meet across the room, and he smiles like he just did something fantastic. “Don’t.”

He tilts his head. “I swore to tell the truth.”

The judge bangs the gavel again, as if it’s any use. “You can’t talk to the witness, Ms. Wong! Agent Kennedy!”

“Apologies, Your Honour,” Leon says, but there’s not a lick of shame in his body.

When they sentence Ada to death at the end of all of this, will he throw himself into it, too? A‘buy one, get two’ deal? How will he deal with it when she’s dead and he burned his career for nothing? It gets the judge minutes to get the courtroom to settle. By then, Nari and Sophie are the only happy people in the room.

Well. Perhaps Leon, too.

“Agent Kennedy,” Jenkins resumes. Ada suspects he’s almost as angry as she is. “Ms. Wong, this woman you… This woman you claim to love… She has worked for and with terrorists. She has endangered countless lives, including your own. The lives of your fellow agents and fellow Americans. She has lied to you, manipulated you.”

“Yes,” Leon says. “I’m not arguing against that. Jenkins, was it?”

“Correct. Agent Ken—”

Leon cuts him off. Even throws a hand up that makes Sophie snort. “Jenkins. Listen. Ada Wong used, seduced, and bruised me. Countless times. She has broken into my apartment, she had me stalked for years. And I love her! I would marry her if I could—”

“Objection!” someone from the prosecution cries, a woman whose name Ada has forgotten. She’s so angry she’s standing up, hands on the table like she’ll start throwing punches if she doesn’t keep her hands flat on the surface. “Your Honour, the witness is compromised. We—We want his testimony stricken from the record!”

Sophie immediately stands up, already prepared for when the judge calls both the defense and prosection to the bench. Ada watches them go, then looks at Leon again.

He looks entirely too satisfied. She wishes she could go up to him and slap him the way she did in his bathroom that one time. This is just another form of lunacy.

She shakes her head, and his smile wavers, just a little.

The courtroom is still an unruly mess, but the judge isn’t even trying to calm it down. Perhaps she’s even glad for it—a cover for her discussion with the lawyers. Or argument, more like. Sophie and the woman from the prosecution look like they’re one word from a fist fight.

Several minutes pass before the lawyers break off, trickling back to their tables.

“What a fucking prick,” Sophie mutters as they take their seats again.

“Who?” Nari asks, blatantly staring at the prosecution.

“All of them,” Sophie replies.

“What happened over there?” Ada asks.

“Leon’s testimony won’t be stricken. But I think they’re shifting gears.”

“To what?”

Nari sighs. “Does he love you, or did your manipulations work.”

“He shouldn’t be here,” Ada says. “I knew it. I told you.”

“This isn’t us.”

“But he shouldn’t be here.”

When the court finally settles and Leon’s smug smile has died into a flat line, it isn’t Jenkins that rises for the prosecution, but that woman again. She crosses the floor in heels taller than even Ada would consider and stops before Leon.

“Agent Kennedy, my name is Beatrice Kinsley.”

“Uhuh,” Leon replies.

“You say you love the defendant. Ada Wong.”

“I do.”

“And you’ve already admitted to her manipulating your feelings. You said it started back in 1998, when you first met.”

His brows draw together for just a moment. “Correct.”

Does he understand now? Does he finally understand why he should have kept his stupid mouth shut? Why nothing good will come from him trying save her from this?

“Has Ada Wong used your feelings for her against you?”

“I—” This time when he looks at her, it’s the same expression he wore in Spain when she left him behind. When he let her get away, Plaga sample in hand. “Yes. But my feelings for her—”

Kinsley holds up a finger. “Objection,” she says primly. “Irrelevant.”

“Sustained,” the judge says with a sigh. “Agent Kennedy, you must limit yourself to answering the questions. This cannot possibly be your first time in court.”

Leon doesn’t look at anyone when he says, “Yes, Your Honour.”

“Go on, Mrs. Kinsley.”

Kinsley smiles, and Ada wants to fucking strangle her. “So. Agent Kennedy. You implied you have met Ms. Wong in a less official capacity. You said she’s broken into your apartment. Can you elaborate?

“She… She’s kept tabs on me. When I’ve needed her, she’s been there.”

“But only when you need her?”

“We’re busy people.”

“You asked her to marry you.”

“Yes.”

“What did Ms. Wong answer?”

Leon’s lips part. He blinks. “She said no.”

But I wanted to say yes! Ada wants to scream. I’m here because I wanted to marry him. I turned myself in to become a person for him!

He looks helpless. All alone. Ada hates and loves and pities him all at once. He’s digging his own grave. He’s on his back, slowly but surely burying himself alive while sobbing about how he’s about to die. All she can do is sit and watch and hate every second of it.

“Has Ms. Wong ever told you she returns your feelings, Agent Kennedy?”

“Yes,” Leon says. He believes it, too. Ada can see it in his posture—how he straightens. Relaxes, almost.

They both know the truth, though. Those three little words… She’s never said them back.

“When did you first tell Ada Wong you love her?”

Ada isn’t sure she remembers, but she isn’t very surprised when Leon speaks after barely a second of thinking. “End of 2008. I think.”

“Oh?” Kinsley says. “Could you paint me a picture? How did it come to be?”

“It was around Christmas. Maybe on Christmas Eve? I was… drunk. I was drunk a lot of the time back then. Ada, she… She broke in. And I felt lonely, so I told her I love her.”

“Did she say it back?”

“No.”

“When did she say it back the first time?”

“Well.” Leon shifts. He’s not relaxed anymore. “She’s never said it in so many words.”

Kinsley nods as if she understands. As if she pities Leon. “And yet you believe she loves you? You don’t think it was just manipulation, all these years?”

“Objection,” Sophie calls. “Leading the witness. Jesus!”

“Sustained,” the judge says.

“Can I answer?” Leon asks.

Ada doesn’t want him to. She wants this Kinsley woman to be done with Leon and return to her seat.

Sophie and Nari had warned her the prosection would make it all seem pointless—like they’d already lost. Ada had prepared for that, and it had been easy enough because she’s thought it all seemed pointless from the start.

But again, it’s one thing to think something, another to experience it.

“No,” the judge says—short and simple.

“Thank you, Your Honour,” Kinsley says. “I have no further questions.”

⟡

03.02. The Trial; Night.

Leon rolls the coin between his fingers. A glass of whiskey sits untouched by his hand. Is it worth it? He’s almost at two years sober, but he itches for the burn of something strong enough to make him forget the day gone by.

The look on Ada’s face is etched into his brain. He sees it even when he closes his eyes. Her anger and disappointment. But why? Leon doesn’t understand it. He’s doing this for her. So that the jury will understand that she’s worthy of love and kindness. That she’s not a bad person. So what if she’s not a good one? Who is?

Even though Leon should probably call his sponsor, he calls Chris Redfield instead. Noah’s a great guy, but he could never fathom what Leon is feeling. Chris can. Chris might not know the pain of loving a woman on trial, but he knows the pain of carrying so much weight you start wishing it’ll all crush you.

He doesn’t take long to show. The block of ice is still mostly solid in its whiskey bath by the time Chris slides into Leon’s booth across from him.

“Hey,” he says. Gently. Like he’s talking to a spooked animal.

Leon looks up at him and lets the coin rest on the table next to the glass. “Hey.”

“Tough day?”

He snorts. “Could say that, yeah. How did it go for you?”

Chris sighs. “If it’s any consolation, I don’t think the prosecution was entirely happy with me either.”

“Why? What happened?”

“I told them the truth,” he says, as if it’s so very simple. “Not what they wanted, I guess.”

Leon nods. “Not their truth.” His fingers wrap around the glass, the condensation cool against his skin. “It was harder than I thought it would be.”

“I’m not sure we’re allowed to talk about it,” Chris says.

“What are they gonna do?” Leon snorts. “They’ve already beaten us down, Chris.”

“You’re in a shitty mood.” Chris tries to pry the glass from Leon’s hand.

“No shit.” His grip tightens.

“Don’t ruin your progress over it.”

“Why not?” he sighs.

“For her.”

Their eyes meet over the table, and Leon eases his hold, letting Chris take the whiskey away. For a few minutes, they sit in silence.

Leon rubs the pad of his thumb over the coin as if he’ll find some secret inscription, a magic spell to make it all better.

When he doesn’t, he breaks the silence. “What are her odds?”

“No idea,” Chris says. “Cases I’m a part of are usually more clear cut than this. Ada is… complex. Of course her case is, too.”

Leon nods slowly. Then he remembers something that had occupied this mind all day before his own testimony. “Mary Wesninski testified. Why?”

“Because of Nathan Wesninski.”

“I never understood why Ada killed him,” Leon mumbles, mostly to himself. “Or how. When she was twenty-one? I mean, what was she doing?”

Chris shifts. “What?”

“How did Ada kill one of the most wanted men in America when she was twenty-one?”

“You don’t know? About…”

Are there worse words? Leon doesn’t think so.

They fill him with ice, make him desperate for that whiskey again.

He knows, of course, that there is so little he actually knows about Ada. That’s not the part that hurts like a knife in the gut. It’s that Chris knows. That probably the whole BSAA knows.

“No,” he forces out.

Chris stares at him. “Nathan Wesninski took Ada from her home in China when she was ten. He brought her to the States, raised her, trained her. He’s the one who made her into what she is.”

“And she killed him for it,” Leon fills in.

“Yes.”

For a moment, Leon wonders if he’s misheard all of it.

Around him, everything fades. The music, the conversation, the clink of glasses and bubbling laughter.

“When she was ten?” he asks, just to be sure.

Chris only nods.

It’s not right. It can’t be right. There’s something that isn’t right.

Leon sees her suddenly—not Ada in red dresses and high heels and sunglasses. Not Ada dangling from grappling hooks or walking away from explosions.

A little girl.

A little girl standing in a village somewhere he can't picture.

A little girl with scraped knees and missing teeth and parents who loved her.

A little girl being dragged onto a plane.

His stomach lurches.

“Jesus fucking Christ.”

He might throw up. There’s bile in his throat, and it’s threatening to spill all over the table.

“Leon?” Chris asks, reaching for him.

But Leon stands up. He pushes his way through people until he’s on his knees in front of an already dirty toilet, vomiting. Somewhere behind him, he can hear Chris. At least they’re the only two people in the bathroom.

There’s a large hand on his back. It’s not moving, just there. A reminder that Leon isn’t alone.

Did Ada ever have that?

Perhaps he should have asked her. Pushed to learn about her past. He’s had his theories, of course, but nothing even close to the truth.

Leon collapses against the unsteady wall of the stall and looks up at Chris. “They trafficked her.”

“Yes.”

“She was a child.” He wipes at his mouth. “They… They…”

“Yeah,” Chris agrees. He kneels next to Leon. “They stole her. They never gave her a new identity beyond a name. They taught her to kill.”

“And yet she’s the one on trial.” His voice is paper thin. “Do they know? The prosecution?”

“I don’t know how these things work. Maybe. Maybe not.”

“How… How do you know?”

“Sophie told me.”

Leon nods.

There’s still something. Something that isn’t right.

“When she was ten? What year?”

“1987.”

“No,” Leon mumbles. “No, she… 1989.”

“She was born in ‘77, and unless I’ve forgotten all my math, 77 plus 10 is 87.”

Leon shakes his head. “She’s two years older than me.”

“Maybe that’s what she told you, but it’s not the truth.”

He doesn’t register he’s crying until he tastes salt.

God. He must look a mess. Bile clinging to his lips, snot and tears all over his face. Flushed with anger and aching with grief. But Chris doesn’t mention it. He just grabs Leon’s shoulder, tight and proud, if it’s even possible for a hold to feel proud.

“She’s strong,” he says. “She’s made it this far, Leon. She’s a victim, but she’s not weak. And she won’t go down without a fight. She has you to make sure of that.”

The Washington Post

“I Love Her”: Leon Kennedy’s Explosive Testimony Shakes First Day of United States v. Wong Kennedy in 2015

Decorated anti-bioterror agent admits his love for alleged bioweapons criminal Ada Wong in a courtroom moment that stunned the nation.

The first day of the highly anticipated federal trial of Ada Wong, one of the world’s most notorious figures in international bioterrorism, ended not with a scientific revelation or a devastating piece of evidence, but with a confession: “I love her.”

The words came from Agent Leon S. Kennedy, a founding member of the Division of Security Operations and one of the United States’ most decorated anti-bioterror agents, during what was expected to be the prosecution’s strongest witness testimony.

Within seconds, the courtroom descended into chaos.

The jury and public gallery alike erupted into conversation. Even the prosecution appeared momentarily stunned as Kennedy openly acknowledged a decades-long romantic relationship with the woman accused of trafficking biological weapons, collaborating with terrorists, and contributing to some of the worst bioterror incidents of the modern era.

The revelation transformed the tone of the trial. Earlier in the day, prosecutors had focused heavily on painting Wong as a dangerous and calculating operative whose actions repeatedly set back international efforts to eliminate biological threats. Dr. Alicent Tower, an expert in counter- bioterrorism, testified that Wong represented “one of the greatest failures of international counter- bioterror strategy,” arguing that her theft and distribution of biological samples may have prolonged the global bioterror crisis by years, perhaps even decades.

The prosecution also sought to establish a pattern of emotional manipulation in Wong’s personal relationships. Former U.S. President’s daughter Ashley Graham testified that Kennedy had previously told her Wong had deceived him during the 2004 Valdelobos incident. However, Graham also delivered one of the day’s biggest surprises when she defended Wong, stating that both she and Kennedy would likely have died had Wong not intervened.

“Ada Wong saved Leon’s life, and she saved America, too.”

- Ashley Graham.

The defense received another unexpected ally in Captain Chris Redfield of the BSAA, who openly acknowledged Wong’s criminal history but rejected the notion that she was a monster. However, it was Kennedy’s testimony that may have altered the public perception of the case more than any other.

When questioned about his first meeting with Wong during the 1998 Raccoon City outbreak, Kennedy admitted that she had lied to him, manipulated him, and repeatedly used his feelings throughout their long and complicated history. For a brief moment, the prosecution appeared to have secured a critical admission. That changed moments later.

When asked how he felt discovering that Wong had secretly entered his hospital room while he was recovering from injuries in 2016, Kennedy did not express anger. “I was relieved,” he said. “I hadn’t heard from her in six months. I was beginning to worry.” He then delivered the statement that immediately dominated national headlines. “I love her, you know? I was worried she’d died. I don’t think I would have been able to take that.”

The prosecution quickly shifted strategy, arguing that Kennedy’s feelings for Wong were themselves evidence of her manipulation. Assistant prosecutor Beatrice Kinsley questioned whether a man who had been deceived by Wong for nearly twenty years could be considered an objective witness.

Legal analysts are divided over whether the testimony ultimately helped or harmed the defense. On one hand, Kennedy’s public loyalty may humanize Wong, a woman many Americans know only through classified reports, leaked intelligence files, and allegations of terrorism. On the other, the prosecution may use his emotional attachment to argue that one of the nation’s most respected agents was himself a victim of her manipulation.

The first day of United States v. Wong was expected to be a trial about bioweapons, global security, and accountability for decades of violence. Instead, it ended with the world asking a much more personal question: Did Ada Wong manipulate Leon Kennedy for twenty years—or did one of America’s greatest heroes just risk everything to tell the truth about the woman he loves?

─────────────────

03.03. The Trial; Defence.

Excerpt from Trial Proceedings Witness: Dr. Margaret Linton Examining Attorney: Nari Lee, Federal Public Defender Examination: Direct Examination by the Defence Q: Dr. Linton, could you please state your qualifications for the record. A: I am a licensed psychologist with a doctorate in trauma psychology. For the past twenty-three years, I have specialised in complex trauma, child trafficking, and post-traumatic stress disorders in military and intelligence individuals. Q: Is this your first time providing expert testimony in court? A: No, ma’am. This makes my 37th time. Q: You’re a psychologist. Have you personally examined the defendant, Ms. Wong? A: No. But my opinion of Ms. Wong is based on records submitted by the defence, assessments provided by several intelligence agencies, as well as what little is publicly available regarding Ms. Wong’s history and career. Q: Has your research into Ms. Wong led you to a conclusion? A: Yes. In interviews with BSAA representatives, Ms. Wong revealed the story of how she came to America in the first place. Assuming everything she says is true and accurate, which I believe it to be, Ms. Wong’s developmental history is consistent with severe childhood trauma, trafficking, prolonged coercive control, and grooming—not in the sexual sense. Ms. Wong has never said anything that has led me to believe she was sexually assaulted by Mr. Wesninski or his wife. But I believe they groomed her, or conditioned her, to serve Mr. Wesninski as a figure of authority and power. Unfortunately, this is not uncommon. In situations like these, the child learns that safety, affection, food, shelter, and approval are dependent upon usefulness and obedience. Q: What does something like that do to a child? A: It fundamentally alters them on several levels. Perhaps one that is particularly relevant to this case is how the child views relationships. Q: How so? Why is that particularly relevant in the case of the defendant? A: Most children learn that they are inherently worthy of care. Trafficked children often learn the opposite. They learn that their value exists only in what they can provide. Q: And what does that look like in adulthood? A: Well. It manifests in multiple ways. Extreme self-reliance, difficulty trusting others, emotional withdrawal, hypervigilance, an inability to accept help, and a tendency to view every interaction as something transactional. Of course, this can vary from person to person. Q: Doctor, does that mean such individuals are incapable of forming attachments? A: No. Human beings are remarkably resilient. What often occurs instead is that attachments become… frightening. The person desires connection but associates vulnerability with danger, something that was likely drilled into them in childhood. Weakness resulted in punishment, like missed meals or lessened comforts, if not actual physical punishments. Q: Everything you’ve just described for us here, are they traits visible in the documented behavior of Ada Wong?

A: Very much so. There is repeated refusal to rely on others, a consistent avoidance of long-term relationships, and maintaining emotional distance—even from trusted individuals. Q: And based on your experience, is that behavior unusual? A: Not at all. In fact, I’d say it’s to be expected. Intimacy requires trust, and trust is difficult for survivors of trafficking. It can feel more dangerous than violence. Q: Could you explain that? A: Violence is predictable. Vulnerability is not. Q: Doctor, are you suggesting that Ms. Wong's actions should be excused because of her childhood? A: Absolutely not. Q: Why not? A: Trauma explains behavior, but it does not absolve responsibility. Q: Then what is the relevance of your testimony? A: The relevance is that human beings do not emerge in a vacuum, and I think that despite what the prosecution is trying to say, Ada Wong is very much human. Q: Meaning? A: The prosecution has presented Ms. Wong as an unusually cold and manipulative woman. My opinion is that many of the behaviors they describe are not evidence of inherent cruelty. Rather, they are survival strategies developed over decades. Q: Survival strategies? A: Yes. Ms. Wong has, seemingly, never depended on anyone. Never revealed weakness. Even in handing herself over, from what I understand of the records, she did it because she wanted to be useful. She gave the BSAA everything they asked her for. Q: Anything else? A: Having very briefly, not even studied, but considered, Ada Wong in the context of her relationship with Leon Kennedy… She seems to believe that if he truly knows her, he will leave. This is another common survival strategy that falls under the same umbrella. Distance is key. It saves your life. Q: One final question, Doctor. A: Of course. Q: In your professional opinion, what is the greatest misconception people have about survivors of childhood trafficking? A: People tend to imagine victims as broken and helpless. The reality is that some become exceptionally competent. Q: Competent? How? A: Intelligent, resourceful, independent to a fault. They become difficult to control. The traits that allow a child to survive exploitation are often the same traits that make them difficult to understand later. Q: No further questions.

········

It’s no easier being back in court on day two. Especially not after how things left off.

If only there was a way for her to speak to Leon. God, she wants to tear him a new one. To beat him bloody and weep about it. She loves him, and he’s making her watch as he throws his life away. Thinking about Leon is only a distraction, though, which is certainly saying something. But Mary Wesninski is back on the stand.

She sits in the witness box wearing a navy blazer and pearls, looking exactly as Ada remembers her. Older, certainly, more wrinkles around her eyes. But otherwise unchanged. She looks kind. Respectable. No doubt the jury both pities and trusts her after her sham of a testimony the day prior.

But Sophie Hunter is making good on her last name. So she said at least, that morning. What that means, exactly, not even Nari seemed to know.

“Mrs. Wesninski,” Sophie says pleasantly. “Good morning.”

Mary smiles. “Good morning.”

“You testified yesterday that you and your husband ensured Ada Wong was housed, educated, and trained.”

“I did.”

Sophie nods. She throws a quick glance over her shoulder at Ada before leveling Mary with an intense stare. “What exactly did you mean by trained?”

Ada tenses.

Her past has been in BSAA records since July of last year. She’s spoken extensively about it with countless people. But Sophie is about to put it out there. The whole world will know. Leon will know. Eventually, at least. Luckily, he’s not in the courtroom.

She tries not to think too much about that.

Mary hesitates. “Life skills.”

“Life skills.”

“Yes.”

Sophie glances at her notes. “Can you elaborate?”

“Household responsibilities. Discipline. We gave her an education.”

Sophie smiles—a shark showing teeth. A hunter aiming its rifle. “And combat?”

Mary blinks, laughs almost. “What?”

“Combat training,” Sophie repeats, all innocent and sweet.

Oscar Jenkins is already on his feet. “Objection. Assumes facts not in evidence.”

“Sustained if Ms. Hunter is asking a question,” the judge says. “Overruled if she's establishing one. Continue.”

Sophie doesn't even look at Jenkins, her gaze fixed on Mary. “Did Ada Wong receive combat training while under your care?”

The answer comes quickly. “No.” Mary even manages to look absolutely astonished by the question.

Ada almost envies her acting abilities.

Sophie nods as though she expected it. “Did she receive firearms training?”

“No.”

“Hand-to-hand combat?”

“No.”

“Knife training?”

“No!” Mary exclaims. “No, none of it.”

Sophie walks slowly toward the witness stand. “Mrs. Wesninski, are you familiar with Exhibit 44?”

Mary's lips press into a thin line. “Perhaps."

Sophie pulls out a sheet of paper from the binder in her arms. She looks it over as if she’s seeing it for the first time. It very nearly makes Ada smile.

“Exhibit 44 is a collection of photographs seized from a property owned by Nathan Wesninski in 1996,” she says. Mary doesn’t respond. Sophie looks at her. “Would you like me to describe them, Mrs. Wesninski?”

“That won't be necessary."

“Wonderful.” Sophie puts the sheet of paper away again. “Then you know that one of the photographs depict fourteen-year-old Ada Wong with a semi-automatic rifle in her hands.”

Mary shifts. All of the respectable kindness is gone now. Instead, there’s a storm brewing, one Ada knows too well. It’s not the kind of storm you can seek shelter from. All you can do is wait it out, even as the rain pounds on your skin and the lightning cracks above.

“Do they not?” Sophie pushes

Clearing her throat, Mary says, “Those photographs exist."

“Excellent. We're making progress.”

A few jurors exchange glances, and when Sophie then asks, “Would you describe target shooting as a household responsibility?” a ripple of laughter moves through the jury.

Jenkins stands. “Objection. Your Hon—"

“Overruled."

Mary's jaw tightens. “No."

“No? Then why was a fourteen-year-old girl learning to shoot? The photograph in question is not exactly a father-daughter hunting trip. It’s taken at a gun range.”

“I don't know,” Mary says thinly.

“You don't know. But You lived on the property?”

“Yes."

“You supervised her education?”

“Yes.”

“You testified she was raised by you and your husband in every meaningful sense.”

“Yes. Well—”

Sophie cuts her off. “But somehow this all passed under your nose? Ada Wong was learning how to handle firearms under your roof, and you never questioned it? Never knew of it?”

A long, uncomfortable silence stretches through the room. Ada risks glancing around—at the discomforted jury members, the frustrated prosecution, the judge with a burning kind of interest in her gaze… She even looks behind her, at the reporters scribbling furiously and the public gallery whispering amongst themselves.

Is it all they expected? All they could dream of? Is the trial of Ada Wong proving to be the spectacle they predicted?

At length, Mary says, “My husband arranged it.”

Sophie nods—understanding. “And your husband Nathan Wesninski. A known organised crime figure.”

“Objection.”

“Overruled.” The judge levels Jenkins with a flat look. “Mr. Jenkins, you went to law school, I’m sure. Please refrain from needlessly throwing out your objections.”

Next to Ada, Nari snorts quietly.

Mary closes her eyes briefly. “Yes. Correct.”

Sophie nods again. “So Nathan Wesninski arranged combat training for a fourteen-year-old child.”

“I suppose."

“You suppose?”

“Well. I… Yes. I suppose.”

“Was that the only training? You’ve already admitted to the firearms and combat training, you might as well keep going, ma’am.”

Mary hesitates. “No,” she says. “We… She was taught several languages, too. Russian, French, German, Arabic. English, of course. And we—they… They made sure she never lost her Mandarin.”

A murmur spreads through the room again, though not loud or disruptive enough for the judge to interfere.

Sophie’s shoulders straighten, she steps closer towards Mary on the stand. An ease spreads through Sophie’s body—she knows her victim has stepped into the trap. Ada knows that stance. That focus. Sophie is going for the kill.

“And why would a teenager need training in those languages as well as combat training?”

Mary looks down. "I don't know.”

“Mrs. Wesninski.” Her voice sharpens. “You are under oath.”

Jenkins rises again. “Objection!”

The judge barely glances up. “Overruled.”

Mary's knuckles whiten around the edge of the witness stand. Her eyes lock on Ada’s, and there was a time where that look would spread terror through her body. But that time is gone, and now when Mary looks at her, Ada returns her heavy gaze and holds it.

“I knew what Nathan wanted,” she says, voice like ice. “He wanted her useful.”

Sophie's expression doesn't change. “Useful for what?”

Mary is struggling in her trap. Tied up the way she is she’s no longer the respectable social worker or the poor grieving widow, she’s far from the woman who saved a child.

She’s a monster.

Now everyone can see what Ada saw years ago.

It’s a weird, twisted kind of relief.

“His work,” Mary says.

“And what was his work?”

“Criminal… enterprises. I don’t know what you want me to say.”

Sophie takes a step back. “Let me make sure I understand your testimony.”

Mary finally tears her gaze off Ada to look at Sophie. “What?”

But Sophie just continues. “You brought a ten-year-old girl from China, taught her multiple foreign languages. You arranged firearms training and combat instruction. You raised her in the home of a known criminal.” A smile stretches across her face. “And your position before this jury is that Ada Wong somehow became violent all by herself?”

Mary's face flushes a deep red. “She chose what she became.”

Sophie tilts her head. “Did she?” She turns to the judge. “No further questions, Your Honour.”

········

Excerpt from Trial Proceedings Witness: Dr. Alaa El-Arift Examining Attorney: Nari Lee, Federal Public Defender Examination: Direct Examination by the Defence Q: Good morning, Doctor. Would you please state your qualifications for the record? A: I’m a senior intelligence analyst specialising in bioterror incidents. I previously worked for the Department of Homeland Security and Division of Safety Operations, and currently serve as a consultant to several international counter-bioterror organisations. Q: How long have you worked in this field? A: Twenty-two years this month. Q: In preparing for this case, were you asked to evaluate the defendant's involvement in several major bioterror incidents? A: Yes. The Raccoon City outbreak in 1998, the Los Illuminados incident in Spain in 2004, and the global C-Virus outbreak in 2013.

Q: And did you reach conclusions regarding the impact of Ada Wong's actions during those events? A: I did. My conclusion was that her actions repeatedly reduced projected casualties. Q: Could you elaborate? A: In multiple incidents, available evidence indicates that Ms. Wong intervened in ways that disrupted the acquisition, deployment, or further development of biological weapons. Looking at Raccoon City, for example, Ms. Wong assisted then police officer Leon Kennedy during the outbreak and directly contributed to his survival. Which is significant, of course, because Agent Kennedy would later become one of the most effective anti-bioterror operatives in modern history. In saving Agent Kennedy, it could also be argued that Ada Wong saved the lives of Claire Redfield, who works with TerraSave today, and Sherry Birkin, a DSO agent. The survival of those three individuals—Kennedy, Redfield, and Birkin—had enormous consequences beyond Raccoon City. Q: Moving on to Spain in 2004, doctor. A: The evidence here suggests Ms. Wong prevented a dominant Las Plagas specimen from falling directly into Albert Wesker's possession. It is, of course, impossible to say exactly what would have happened had that happened. But more likely than not, Albert Wesker possessing that specimen would have represented a severe global security threat. My models indicate the potential loss of life could have reached into the millions. Q: And in 2013? A: Ms. Wong played a significant role in exposing Carla Radames' activities and providing intelligence that contributed to the collapse of Neo-Umbrella operations. As I understand it, the prosecution means to pin Neo-Umbrella on Ms. Wong— OSCAR JENKINS: Objection. I ask the Court to instruct the witness to not stray from the line of questioning.

THE CROWN: Sustained. Dr. El-Arift, please simply answer Ms. Hunter’s questions. Q: In your professional opinion, did those actions save lives? A: Yes. Q: A few lives? A: No. Conservatively? Tens of thousands. Q: Conservatively? A: Yes. Q: What does that mean? A: It means I am giving the lowest estimate I can reasonably defend. Q: Doctor, the prosecution has argued that Ada Wong prolonged the global bioterror conflict. Do you disagree? A: No, I don’t, regardless of what I just said. Two things can be true at the same time, and the prosecution's expert is not entirely wrong. Actions taken by Ms. Wong likely contributed to the continued circulation of biological research and weapons technology; I can’t argue with that. But that’s not the entire picture. In my line of work, I’ve asked two questions, while the prosecution only asked one. They wonder: what harm did this person cause? But in addition to that, I wonder: what harm would have occurred without them? Q: And what has your work shown you? A: That the world without Ada Wong is significantly deadlier than the world with her. Many individuals involved in bioterrorism sought profit, power, ideology, or destruction; Albert Wesker is one such individual. But Ms. Wong repeatedly acted against those individuals even when doing so placed her at considerable personal risk. Q: Why? A: I can’t speak to her motives; I don’t know her. But I can say that without her, the world wouldn’t be a safer place. Because many of our heroes would not be here today if it wasn’t for her.

········

Ada is barely paying attention as Dr. El-Arift steps down from the stand. The courtroom blurs together into noise and movement and endless chatter in the small moment of in-between. She can hear the reports scribbling, the jurors stretching and shifting.

Nari returns to the defence table, smiling.

“How you holding up, Ada?” she asks.

Ada looks up at her. “Fine.”

“We’ll be done soon, probably,” Sophie says. She stands while Nari sits.

“Why? Who’s next?”

When her lawyers exchange a look, Ada knows. Immediately.

Her stomach drops.

No.

When Sophie turns to the judge, binder in her arms, Ada feels a sudden, overwhelming urge to tackle her.

“Your Honor," Sophie says. “The defense calls Agent Leon S. Kennedy.”

The world stops, even though Ada knew it was coming.

Every conversation in the room dies. At least thirty necks crick when every reporter in the room looks up.

Ada closes her eyes. Maybe, if she keeps them shut long enough, she'll wake up somewhere else and this will all prove to be a horrible dream.

“I told you,” she says under her breath.

“I know,” Nari replies. “He offered.”

When Ada opens her eyes again, he’s right there. But all the easy swagger and confidence of the day before is gone. The Leon who takes to the stand now is closer to the Leon she finds in alleyways and bars. Dark circles rim his eyes, making him look tired and gaunt. His hair falls limp —not at all styled the way it had been yesterday. His suit is wrinkled. Hell, it even looks like he’s been crying.

“You told me you wouldn't,” she mouths and hopes he can read her lips.

How could he? How could he lie straight to her face? When did he agree to this? How long has he known he’d show up here today to betray her wishes?

Yesterday was bad enough.

Because Leon is selfish, and he doesn’t know when to stop, and he can’t change. He’s not up there for her; he’s doing it for himself. He needs to defend her, because he thinks that’s right. It doesn’t matter that she disagrees, that she never wanted him close to this process at all.

Once the courtroom and Leon have settled, Sophie wastes no time before she starts.

“Agent Kennedy,” she says. “I'd like to discuss Ms. Wong herself today.”

Leon nods. “Okay.”

His voice is surprisingly unsteady. He has been crying, Ada can both hear and see it now.

“Do you believe Ada Wong is a good person?”

Sarah Kinsley and Oscar Jenkins immediately stand in unison. They exchange a look before Jenkins says, “Objection.”

It’s Sophie that asks, “On what grounds?”

“Calls for speculation.”

The judge sighs. “Overruled. You may answer, Agent Kennedy.”

He doesn't immediately. Instead, he looks toward Ada. “No,” he says thinly.

A murmur sweeps through the room.

Ada closes her eyes again. It’s a small relief. But it’s nothing, because he’s there.

“You don’t?” Sophie asks. “Why not?”

“Because I think people divide the world into good people and bad people when it's convenient. But they’re arbitrary labels. Ada isn’t good, but neither am I. Can anyone say they’re truly good? I don’t think so.”

Sophie nods. “Then let me ask this instead. Do you believe Ada Wong is cruel?”

Leon shakes his head. “No.” This time he sounds firmer, more certain. “Because cruel people enjoy hurting others.” There’s a beat. Leon glances towards the jury then towards the reporters and the gallery. “I've seen cruel people. I've worked for some of them. Ada is nothing like them. I can’t deny that she’s hurt people or that she’s hurt me, even, but… She doesn’t do it because it brings her joy. She does it because she thinks there’s no other choice.”

“What do you mean by that?”

“I was just recently made aware of her…” He trails off, gaze falling. Even from where she’s sitting, Ada can see his lips trembling. “Her past. I guess I never wondered why she started, just why she wouldn’t stop. Or at least why she wouldn’t… be with me. Marry me. But I get it now.”

“Agent Kennedy,” Sophie says carefully. “How long have you known Ms. Wong?”

He straightens, and some of his pain seems to be gone now. Not that he looks a lot better for it. “We met for the first time in 1998. But if you’re asking about our… relationship… that started in 2005.”

“Since 1998, have you ever known her to place herself in danger for someone else?”

Leon laughs wetly. “Constantly.”

“Can you provide examples?”

“Raccoon City.”

Sophie hums. “Specific examples.”

“She saved my life.”

“Anything else?”

Leon nods. “Spain.”

“Specific examples, Agent Kennedy.”

“Well, she saved my life.”

A few jurors smile. Even Sophie looks amused. Nari, too.

Traitors.

“Anything else?”

“Lanshiang.”

Smiling, already aware of what’s coming, Sophie says, “Specific examples.”

Leon, unsurprisingly, says, “She saved my life.”

The courtroom laughs. Even the judge smiles before catching herself.

Sophie lets the laughter die before she continues. “Agent Kennedy. Could you please provide some details?”

“I don’t know if I can. I don’t know what’s classified. But I… I’d be dead without her. In Spain, she put herself in danger for me and Ashley Graham multiple times.”

“Is it correct you left her out of your reports?”

Leon glances towards the judge. “Yes.”

Ada wants to bury her face in her hands and scream.

“Why?”

“I wanted to protect her. She deserves someone who wants to protect her.”

Ada stares at the table. The wood grain has never been more interesting.

“Has she ever asked for compensation?” Sophie asks. “For your protection, I mean.”

“No.”

“Recognition?”

“No.”

“Credit?”

“No.”

"Why not?"

Leon lets out a small laugh. “Ada hates attention, I think. At least this kind of attention.”

A thought seems to strike him then, and his face immediately falls again.

“Ms. Wong has spent the last two days being described as manipulative, self-serving, and power- hungry. Do those descriptions fit the woman you know?”

Leon thinks for several seconds. Long enough that Ada hopes he'll stop talking. He doesn’t, and Ada realises he wasn’t thinking—just crying not to cry.

His voice is thick when he says, “No.”

Sophie takes a few slow steps toward the witness stand. “Agent Kennedy. In your opinion, what does Ada Wong want?”

“I have no idea,” he says. “I just… She’s always helped me. She got me out of my alcoholism. I was using pills and drinking, and she saved me. She’s not cruel, and she’s not self-serving beyond what we all are. Trying to survive no matter what isn’t self-serving. If it is, then I’m guilty, too.”

Why doesn’t he just kill them both?

His career is dead in the water. It has to be. Soon Ada will be, too.

“Agent Kennedy. I can see this is making you emotional,” Sophie says, her voice softer now.

“Yeah. Sorry.”

“Don’t apologise. It’s clear you care for Ms. Wong.”

“I love her.”

“Do you think she loves you, too?”

“Yes. I do. And I don’t care that she’s never said it. I know she does. That’s all that matters.”

Sophie lets the words settle with the courtroom, even turns away from Leon to cast her eyes over both the jury and the gallery.

In the interlude, Leon’s eyes meet Ada’s again.

“How could you do this to me?” she mouths. He frowns again. “How could you ruin your life for me?”

He doesn’t have a chance to try to mouth something back, because Sophie turns her attention back to him.

“I have one final question for you, Agent Kennedy. Yesterday you testified as both an expert and a government representative. We established you’ve worked tirelessly in various anti-bioterrorism pursuits. But why are you here today?”

The answer comes without hesitation, and he’s still looking at Ada when he speaks. “Because everyone else keeps talking about what Ada Wong has done. They want to paint her a villain, an evil. But they don’t know her. I have no interest in what she’s done. I want to talk about who she is.”

“Thank you, Agent. No further que—”

“Sorry,” Leon cuts in. “Sorry, can I say one more thing?”

Ada shakes her head, a futile attempt to shut him up.

“Go ahead,” Sophie says.

“Ada Wong is only on trial because the government wants a scapegoat for a laundry list of unresolved issues. She’s been given the label ‘bad guy’, and so she’s evil. But who decides who the good guys are? If Ada worked for the U.S., she’d be a fucking hero. But she doesn’t, so she’s evil.” Leon takes a deep, shaky breath. “But how many countries, people, all over the world view us as the bad guys? How many families and cities have we torn apart in the pursuit of something we value? I’ve killed. So ship me off to those countries and put me on trial. It would only be fair. Or free her. Free her and pin a fucking medal on her. She’s served this coutnry and its people more than most could ever dream off.”

“Objection!” Sarah Kinsley cries the second he’s done—as if she was waiting for him to finish the way the rest of the courtroom was.

They all sit with bated breath, staring at him.

But Leon is only looking at her. “If I’m a hero. Then so is she.”

“Objection! Your Honour,” Kinsley cries again. “The witness’ statement is irrelevant. It’s completely inappropriate!”

Her outburst seems to pop the bubble. Just like yesterday, Leon has managed to transform a civilised courtroom into chaos.

Ada cannot be bothered to care.

⟡

03.04. The Trial; The Second Night.

They’re talking to her. As if she’d want to listen. As if she could care about what they have to say.

Ada hates them both. They lied to her. Went behind her back and put Leon on the stand when she explicitly told them not to.

And now he knows all of it. He knows how she became who she is. He knows the parts she wanted to keep from him. Now she’ll never be her again in his eyes. She’ll always be the victim, the little girl without a choice. With Leon, she could always be more herself than without anyone else. With him, the little that remained of Xiao Yu could emerge—a ghost haunting its own corpse. But that’s over now.

Mainly because she never wants to speak to him again.

His betrayal hurts more than Sophie and Nari’s. It’s their job to build the best case possible, and Ada can objectively see why Leon’s words could maybe help.

But Leon…

How can she believe he loves her if he’d do that to her?

“Ada.” Sophie lays a hand on her arm.

Before either of them can really register it, Ada has Sophie pressed against the wall, arm twisted behind her back. It’s harsh enough that she cries out.

“Ada!” Nari cries. “Oh my God! Let her go! What’s wrong with you?”

She stumbles back, staring at her hands like they’d be drenched in blood. Horror and bile claw their way up her esophagus, threatening to spill over.

“Are you okay?” Nari asks Sophie, pulling her into a hug.

Ada walks backwards into the corner then slides down the wall. “I’m sorry,” she says, voice gravelly. “I’m sorry, Sophie.”

“I’m fine,” Sophie says to Nari. She glances towards Ada. “It’s okay. I shouldn’t have touched you.”

“I—” Ada’s gaze flickers between them. There’s something large and uncomfortable sitting on her chest, weighing her down. Something like grief and hatred and anger all in one. “He’s wrong about me. Leon. He’s always been wrong.”

Nari and Sophie exchange a look, then Nari shakes her head. “What do you mean?”

“I’m not a hero. I’m not a good person. That’s what he wants me to be, but it’s not true.” Her voice breaks as tears well up in her eyes. “I’ve done horrible things. Things he doesn’t know! And he’s thrown his whole life away! For what?”

“He loves you,” Nari says, voice soft. “You heard him.”

Sophie crosses the room—they’re back at BSAA’s D.C. headquarters, in a conference room with a view of the skyline, having dinner and preparing for day three—and kneels in front of Ada. “Is that what this is about,” she asks. “Or is it something else?”

“Sophie,” Nari says, a hint of a warning in her tone.

But Sophie continues. “We’ve talked, you know,” she says. “Nari and I. The whole team. We know you don’t believe you can win this, Ada. But we’ve never been sure why. Today’s testimonies gave me some much needed context, I think. Even though I knew most of it.” This time when she reaches for Ada, she’s slower. Her hand settles gently on Ada’s ankle then meets her eyes as if to ask okay?

Ada gives the slightest nod. “We won’t win because the jury will see what I see.”

“No,” Sophie says. “We will win because they’ll see what you don’t. They’ll see the gravity of what the Wesninskis did to you. They’ll see all the horrible shit you’ve had to go through, and they’ll see that despite all of that, you have someone who loves you. And that you love him, too, because you can’t seem to stop saving his life.”

Ada honest to God whimpers. It’s so humiliating she feels like she’ll throw up again.

She’s forty years old. She’s a grown woman. To… To sit on the floor, whimpering, weeping, is entirely undignified. Especially in front of an audience.

But when Sophie pulls her into a hug and Nari joins them on the floor, her arms wrapping around both of them, Ada doesn’t feel like a grown woman. She feels like the kitten she found in an alleyway, or at least how she hope it felt when she brought it to her chest.

Warm. Seen. Cared for. If only for the shortest moment. Even if death is imminent.

It’s nice.

On the other side of it is everything. A whole world of horrors. Days of being pulled apart and scrutanised.

Ada’s gotten better at living in the moment, though. Thanks to Leon.

She pushes him and the rest of it away and focuses on the warmth of the young women who have spent almost a year straight fighting for her.

The Washington Post

Defense Reveals Details of Wong's Upbringing

Testimony shifts focus from bioterrorism to childhood trafficking allegations as defense seeks to challenge prosecution's portrayal of Ada Wong.

Day two of United States v. Wong shifted attention away from bioterrorism and toward Ada Wong's childhood.

Former caregiver Mary Wesninski acknowledged that Wong received firearms, combat, and language training while living under the supervision of organised crime figure, the late Nathan Wesninski. Under questioning from the defense, Mrs. Wesninski admitted Wong was trained to be "useful" to criminal enterprises.

Psychologist Margaret Linton later testified that Wong's documented behavior is consistent with survivors of trafficking and prolonged coercive control, arguing that many of the traits prosecutors describe as manipulative may instead be survival mechanisms.

The defense concluded the day by calling Agent Leon S. Kennedy, who once again publicly defended Wong and accused the U.S. government of turning her into a scapegoat for decades of bioterror-related failures.

Kennedy repeatedly credited Wong with saving his life throughout their nearly two-decade history and rejected the prosecution's characterization of her as a villain, telling the court: "I've seen cruel people. Ada is nothing like them."

The Washington Post

Defense Argues Wong Was “Never the One in Charge”

Expert testimony challenges prosecution's portrayal of Ada Wong as a central figure in global bioterrorism.

The third day of United States v. Wong focused on a question at the heart of the government's case: how much influence Ada Wong actually wielded over the events for which she is being blamed.

Defense witnesses argued that Wong has frequently been portrayed as a major player in international networks despite rarely occupying positions of leadership or authority. Instead, experts described her as an intelligence operative who worked on the margins of larger organizations, often acting independently and frequently against the interests of the very groups that employed her.

Several witnesses highlighted the prominent figures who dominated the bioterror era—including Albert Wesker, Derek Simmons, Carla Radames, and numerous corporate and political actors— arguing that Wong's role was often overstated because she survived encounters with them when many others did not.

“Ada Wong may bear responsibility for many things, but she was rarely the person giving orders.”

The defense also presented evidence suggesting Wong repeatedly withheld biological samples, sabotaged operations, or interfered with the ambitions of clients and employers. While prosecutors maintain that her actions contributed to the spread of dangerous research, the defense argued that many of the world's most devastating outbreaks would likely have been worse without her intervention.

The day's proceedings concluded with a recurring theme that has increasingly defined the defense's strategy: Ada Wong may bear responsibility for many things, but she was rarely the person giving orders.

─────────────────

The Washington Post

Prosecution Refocuses Trial on Victims of Bioterrorism

Emotional testimony reminds jury of the human cost behind decades of global outbreaks.

After two days dominated by discussions of childhood trauma, coercion, and the broader context of international bioterrorism, prosecutors used the fourth day of United States v. Wong to return attention to the victims.

Witnesses included survivors of multiple bioterror incidents, former government officials, and experts who argued that regardless of Ada Wong's intentions, her actions repeatedly enabled dangerous individuals and organizations to acquire biological weapons and research.

While much of the defense's case has centered on the argument that Wong often prevented greater catastrophes, prosecutors stressed that preventing worse outcomes does not erase the harm that still occurred. Several witnesses described the lasting physical and psychological consequences of outbreaks linked, directly or indirectly, to organizations with which Wong was associated.

The proceedings marked one of the most emotionally charged days of the trial. At several points jurors appeared visibly affected by testimony from survivors who lost family members during major bioterrorism incidents.

Legal observers noted that the prosecution’s strategy represented a significant shift away from portraying Wong as a mastermind and toward a simpler argument: whatever the reasons behind her actions, countless people paid the price.

For the first time since the defense began presenting its case, analysts suggested the government may have regained momentum heading into the trial's final stages.

“Preventing worse outcomes does not erase the harm that still occurred.”

- Prosecution witness testimony.

─────────────────

The Washington Post

Trial Concludes With Clashing Visions of Ada Wong

Closing arguments leave jury with competing portraits of one of the world's most controversial figures.

The fifth and final day of testimony in United States v. Wong concluded with closing arguments from both sides, bringing nearly a week of often dramatic proceedings to an end.

Prosecutors argued that despite extensive testimony regarding Wong's childhood, personal history, and occasional interventions against larger threats, the central facts of the case remain unchanged. They maintained that Wong repeatedly chose to participate in the trafficking and acquisition of B.O.W.s and should be held accountable for the consequences of those decisions.

“The question before this court is not whether Ada Wong suffered. The question is whether suffering places someone above responsibility.”

- Oscar Jenkins, Assistant United States Attorney.

Defense attorneys presented a starkly different interpretation of the evidence. They argued that the government had spent years benefiting from Wong's actions while simultaneously refusing to acknowledge the lives she saved and the threats she helped stop.

According to the defense, the trial has revealed not a criminal mastermind but a woman shaped by exploitation, violence, and decades spent operating in the shadows of far more powerful individuals.

Legal analysts noted that the case increasingly evolved beyond questions of bioterrorism. Instead, much of the final day focused on broader themes of accountability, survival, morality, and whether a person can be judged solely by their worst decisions.

The jury will begin deliberations tomorrow morning.

After five days of testimony, expert analysis, emotional witness statements, and repeated public interventions from the DSO's Agent Leon S. Kennedy, observers remain sharply divided on what verdict should ultimately be delivered.

What appears far less disputed is that the trial has fundamentally changed public perception of Ada Wong. Whether viewed as a criminal, a survivor, a hero, or some combination of all three, she now appears considerably more complicated than either side first suggested.
