03.05. The Trial; Sentencing.

It’s not exactly shocking when Sophie Hunter calls and tells Leon Ada doesn’t want him in court for the sentencing. Not when it’s the fifth call of it’s kind. Since his testimony, Sophie has called him every night to ask him to please stay home.

He does, because it’s what Ada wants.

He’s aware of the irony.

Ada is angry, and that’s why she doesn’t want him there. She’s angry because he went against her wishes. But what was he supposed to do? He had to say his piece, even if it means Ada never wants to speak to him again.

As long as he did what he could do help her be acquitted, he’s happy.

The president wasn’t even that upset, in the end. That, or he was so upset he was beyond showing emotion. Nevertheless, the phone call from Richards wasn’t the termination Leon thought it’d be. But just because the phone call was a metaphorical slap on the wrist doesn’t mean all is well.

None of that matters at the moment, though.

Today Ada receives her sentencing.

Today marks the end or beginning of his life.

He’s not sure what he’ll do if they deliver a guilty verdict. It feels sort of like he’ll die, just based on how he feels thinking about it.

Then again, he’s not sure what he’ll do if they free her, either. Especially if she wants nothing to do with him. Ada Wong has been the center of his universe for twelve years, if not more. It’s not like he’ll just be able to let go. He’ll try, maybe, if she wants him to.

When he inevitably fails, he’s not sure what’ll happen. But he’d never force his presence onto her. If she doesn’t want to see him, she won’t have to. He’ll stay away, even if it kills him.

It will have been worth it.

All of it.

Every lie he’s told, every report he’s left her name out of, every sordid, secret moment, every bullet wound and hallucinated visit.

If the point of all of it was to help her get acqqitted ,then so be it. Leon can live with that. And if he can’t, that’s okay, too.

Time moves at a snail’s pace.

Leon sits in his couch and stares at his phone, waiting for Chris to call with the verdict. But there’s nothing to suggest if it’ll be in ten minutes or three hours. It might not even happen today.

Waiting gives him ample time to imagine every possible scenario that might come after. The nightmares, where Ada is sentenced. Where she’s given the death penalty or locked up for life. Nightmares where he’ll never be able to talk to her or see her again. Then there are the dreams. The fantasies. Where Ada is acquitted and shows up on his doorstep, ready to marry him. Where she’s acquitted and he runs through the city to meet her.

They all drive him crazy in equal measures.

But then his phone vibrates, and Leon almost doesn’t think it’s real at first.

He stares at the caller ID.

Chris Redfield.

The call goes to his voicemail, but immediately after the phone starts vibrating again.

This time Leon answers.

“Chris?” he says, and he can hear the terror in his voice, even though he can’t quite register it. He can feel the way his heart is beating so fast it feels like he’s having a heart attack, he can feel the lump in his stomach and the weight on his chest making it hard to breathe.

“Acquitted, Leon.” His voice is barely audible over the loud commotion in the background. “The jury cleared her of all charges. She’s free to go.”

Everything is faint. Fading.

“Hello?” Chris asks. “Are you there? Leon?”

Leon draws in a deep, trembling breath, unaware he’d stopped breathing in anticipation of the call. “What?”

“She’s free,” he says again. “She’s been granted an American citizenship, too. She’s a person, and she’s free.”

The noise falls away, and it takes several moments for Leon to realise it’s because he hung up.

He sits on the couch, phone in hand, and stares at the door, as if Ada will walk through it any moment.

She will, won’t she?

She’ll come for him like she always does?

Yes.

Yes.

Even despite her anger. She will.

Leon knows she will.

04. WONG, ADA., 2017: Free.

Two months later it’s not Leon Kennedy that Ada Wong, newly minted citizen, tracks down.

But Chris Redfield.

She finds him outside his apartment, just returning from a gym session.

Unsurprisingly, he isn’t that glad to see her.

“What are you doing here?” he asks in lieu of a greeting. “Leon is going fucking crazy.”

“I can’t see him,” she says. “You have to help me.”

He snorts. “What?”

“I can’t go to him,” Ada says again. “Not after what he did, and… I can’t be what he needs. He expects me to run to him and be his wife. I can’t do that. I don’t know how to do that. It’s not me, and I can’t trust him.”

Chris shrugs his duffle bag further up his shoulder. Around them, the street is dark and desolate.

“He loves you,” he says. “He kinda… risked a lot for you.”

“And I never wanted him to. I told him not to. He ignored that. What happens now is on his shoulders. He should be grown enough to accept that.”

“I don’t know,” he says with an awkward chuckle. “Sometimes I feel like Leon is still just a twenty-one-year-old kid.”

“Help you how, exactly?

“I’ll work for you. As a BSAA agent. You can’t exactly say I’m not qualified.”

Chris sighs, eyes falling to the ground. “I’m not sure the BSAA is the best place to work.”

Ada shrugs. “Beggars can’t be choosers, and I don’t think the DSO would particularly love to have me.”

“Maybe you can help me, actually,” he says.

“I’ll do anything,” she says.

“I’m putting together a team. Something inside and outside the BSAA at the same time. I don’t… I don’t know if the BSAA can be entirely trusted. But this team, it needs good people. Strong. Capable.”

Ada’s heart squeezes.

“Don’t know about good. But I’m strong. Capable.” She almost reaches for him. “Please. I haven’t asked for a lot in my life. But I’m asking for this.”

He considers it. Considers her. But it’s not long before he nods, gaze falling again. “Fine,” he says. “But you need an alias or something. No offence. I don’t know how good it’ll look to have you on the team.”

Ada smiles. For the first time in a year, there’s a purpose, a mission, lined up for her. A way to be useful. A way to escape.

“How about Emily Berkhoff?”

He chuckles a little. “That was fast.”

“It’s what Wesninski called me when he took me. I want to make it mine.”

He’s gone months, years, without seeing her. So it’s not even that hard to pretend this time isn’t any different.

It would be easier, though, if he hadn’t been strapped to his desk for the past half year. An internal review or something. But it means he’s not allowed on the field. Which is fine. It’s cool.

“Yeah?”

“I am.” Leon rubs at his eyes. “I just don’t get why you need a cat-sitter for two days.”

Reading glasses…

And he misses Ada Wong so much it makes him fucking stupid. He’s clumsy, forgetful, occupied. It’s like nothing has happened since 2013, and yet everything is different.

That’s okay.

“This…” He laughs at the absurdity of it. “This is a booty call?”

The government agent who professed his love for the terrorist.

How does he go back to nothing?

December, 2020.

Ada, decidedly, hates Europe. At least these stupid rural villages that seem too keen to be infected by bioterrorists.

But she hates Miranda more. She understands why Chris went rogue to stop her.

She also understands why he chose her to do recon. Out of all of them on the Hound Wolf Squad, Ada is the one with most experience in infiltration. That doesn’t mean she doesn’t feel… more afraid than usual. Or that she feels an inkling of resentment towards her Alpha for sending her alone while the rest of them wait—safe and sound—far, far away.

It’s the fear that really upsets her, though, because it’s a fear that’s lingered ever since she departed Kuala Lumpur and kissed Leon goodbye. She needs to live.

She needs to live so that she can go home and marry Leon S. Kennedy.

The halls of the White House are familiar by now. A concept he never would have imagined possible as a child. Not as an adult, either. Not that he’s particularly glad for it. He trails behind an aide he’s vaguely sure he’s seen before, feeling just as uncomfortable in his suit as he always does. It’s been four years since the last time, now. After the trial, Richards has not spoken to him personally, and he’s certainly not been in the Oval Office. Who can even be bothered to hold a grudge that long?

The aide tells him to take a seat in one of the sofas, and then she departs. Richards is there, though he’s by the window, looking out and choosing to not acknowledge Leon—yet.

Leon sits. Waits.

“Coffee?”

He looks towards the president. “Sir?”

Richards turns around. “Would you like coffee, Agent Kennedy?”

That’s new. He’s never been offered coffee before, though he doubts it’s a promise of good things to come. Rather, it feels like an omen. A harbinger of something Leon won’t like at all. Is he being sent somewhere to die? Another one-man mission in a remote location with impossible odds. It would be a clever way to get rid of him—which he’s sure Richards wants very much. There’s an election coming up, and he’s had his two terms; this is his last chance to do something.

“Uh. Yeah. Yes, sir. Thank you.”

Richards rings a bell then takes a seat on the sofa opposite Leon. The two men sit in silence as a man brings in a tray with a steaming pot, two porcelain cups, a little silver jug of cream, and three different kinds of sugar. Leon stares at the arrangement as it’s placed on the table between them.

Maybe some kind of space mission? Testing a rocket? Leon wouldn’t put it past Richards to blow him up somewhere in Nevada.

“Cream?”

Leon looks up.

The president is holding the little jug, wiggling it a little. Sometime during Leon’s vivid imaginations of his limbs being blown clean off, the man has filled the cups with coffee.

“No,” he says, a little strained.

Richards hums. “Sugar?”

“No.”

The president nods and sets it down.

Leon watches him.

The president watches Leon.

“Sir,” Leon says eventually, “are you sending me somewhere?”

“No.” Richards leans back in his chair. “I invited you here for a conversation, Kennedy. Drink your coffee.”

Leon looks down at it. For a split second, he wonders if it could be poisoned, but why bother when they can have him killed by some mutated being somewhere? So he takes a cautious sip, and, of course, it’s delicious.

Richards puts his cup down after tasting it then folds his hands in his lap. “How is Ms. Wong?”

Ah. Well.

Leon takes another sip of coffee—stalling. “Ada?” he asks, precariously balancing the dainty cup in its saucer.

“How many Ms. Wongs do you know?”

“A few. It’s a common name, sir. There’s Maia Wong who works in filing, and Agent Hay-Lynn Wong, a promising transfer from the FBI. Oh, and Sandra Wong who works at the public library.”

Richards gives him a look.

Leon puts his cup on the table. “She’s fine.”

“Is she?”

“No.” It comes out colder than Leon had expected, though he’s not that shocked by it.

The president’s expression shifts. “Is she injured?”

“Not physically.”

“I see.”

Leon doesn’t elaborate.

He doesn’t think the president needs to know that Ada has been sleeping badly since she returned from Europe with the BSAA. That sometimes, Leon wakes and finds her sitting in the kitchen at three in the morning, staring at nothing. That Ethan Winters’ death has affected her more than she will admit.

As far as Leon knows, she didn’t even know him, but she seems to mourn him anyway. In some capacity at least. Maybe there is something about watching a father die for his daughter that leaves a mark on people like her.

People who were once children nobody came for.

“Why are you asking about Ada?”

Richards smiles. It’s cold and too carefully practiced. “We would like her to join the DSO.”

For a moment, Leon genuinely thinks he has misunderstood. At length, he asks, “I’m sorry?”

“We would like Ada Wong to join the Division of Security Operations.”

“I understand the words, sir. I just don’t...” he trails off.

“I don’t see what’s confusing.”

Leon laughs—it comes out louder, more manic, than intended. “Sir.”

“Yes?”

“It’s Ada.”

“I am aware.”

“Ada Wong?”

“I would hope so, yes.”

“The same Ada Wong your government put on trial four years ago.”

“You testified at that trial, as I recall.”

“And as a result, you pulled me from the field and conducted an internal review of my performance.”

Richards’ eyebrows rise. “Which found no wrongdoings.

Leon leans back. Scoffs. “Right.”

There are a lot of reasons to want Ada in the DSO. She speaks multiple languages. She understands bioweapons trafficking better than almost anyone alive. She has contacts on every continent, although Leon suspects that now, more than half of those contacts would shoot her on sight.

She is, objectively, exactly the sort of person the DSO should want. Except for that little fact that they tried to make her swing for crimes she did not commit. There’s only one reason they’d want to hire Ada now.

“You want to keep her close.”

Richards pauses with his cup halfway to his mouth. Then he sighs, and the cup ends up back on the table.

“Kennedy,” he says. It sounds tired, like he’s a father at the end of his rope with an unruly son.

“You don’t trust her,” Leon barrels on.

“This is not about trust. We value Ms. Wong’s abilities.”

“I’m sure you do.”

“We also believe her… particular experience could prove extremely valuable in the coming years.”

Leon watches him carefully. “The coming years,” he echoes.

“She has worked intimately with the BSAA, even prior to her trial.”

There’s a picture being laid out before him, one he’s not sure he’s too happy with.

Leon thinks of Chris. Not the Chris he knows, exactly, but the man Chris has been turning into over the past few years. More secretive. Angrier, according to Ada, who is the one who has actually been by his side. Less willing to defend the organisation he helped build.

“What, exactly, is happening with the BSAA, sir?”

“The BSAA is an international organisation. You know perfectly well that the United States government has no direct authority over, or knowledge of, its operations.”

“Answer the question I asked.”

The president studies him.

Leon has sat across from powerful men for most of his adult life. Presidents. Generals. Directors. Men who believe that because they know more than he does, they are also smarter than him. He has learned to let them believe that. Sometimes. But today, he is tired. He is forty-four years old and has long since lost patience for their diplomatic, carefully worded answers.

“The BSAA deployed bioweapons in Europe,” he says. “Captain Chris Redfield knew something was wrong before any of us did; Ada knew something was wrong before any of us did. Now Redfield has effectively gone underground, and Ada has quit. So I’m asking again: What is happening with the BSAA?”

Richards exhales through his nose. “The situation is developing.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“There are… discussions.”

“What kind of discussions?”

“Kennedy.”

“What kind?”

When the president speaks again, his voice is quieter. “The United States has a responsibility to ensure that an organisation with the reach and resources of the BSAA does not become a threat to global security.”

Leon stares at him. There are many ways to interpret that sentence. Unfortunately, he has worked for the government long enough to understand all of them.

“You’re taking over the BSAA.”

“The United States cannot… take over an international organisation.”

“Sure.”

Richards’ mouth tightens. “Kennedy.”

“No, sorry. Continue not telling me about the hostile takeover of the world’s largest anti- bioterrorism organisation.”

“There is no hostile takeover.”

“So it’s friendly? Yeah, I guess that’s fine, then.”

“There is no takeover.”

Leon stares at him; once again Richards just stares back.

At that moment, Leon suddenly misses Ada with an intensity that surprises him. He wants to go home, tell her about this conversation, and watch her react to it. He wants to study her eyebrows and her lips as she learns the truth she and Chris probably already suspected.

She will be insufferable.

Leon can’t wait.

“So,” he says, palms on his thighs. “Ada?”

“We would like to offer Ms. Wong a position commensurate with her considerable experience.”

“You want her where you can see her.”

Richards sighs. “Can you convince her, or not?”

“Probably,” Leon says.

The president looks almost surprised, despite calling him there for that specific reason. “You can?”

“Sure.”

“You seem confident.”

“I know Ada.”

He wholeheartedly doubts the sincerity behind the offer. It has little to do with what Ada can do and everything to do with who she is. What she’s been through. Yet the thought of working in the same building as her stirs something warm in his chest.

What he also knows is that this is a negotiation. Now, Richards might not know that—yet—but he’s made a terrible mistake: He has let Leon know he wants something. And there’s something Leon wants, too.

“Okay,” he says. “I want something in return.”

Richards scoffs. “No.”

“We want to get married.”

“This is a free country, Agent.”

Leon rolls his eyes. “Somehow, I was still under that impression, yes. Yet our marriage licenses keep being mysteriously rejected.”

Clearing his throat, Richards leans back and crosses his legs. “Those are only a formality. No one is rejected.”

“Yet somehow…”

“Nevertheless, these are not issues for the president of the United States to deal with.”

“But I’m sure if he were to… speak up… they’d stop rejecting us.”

With narrowed eyes, Richards says, “Ms. Wong’s history is complicated.”

“She was acquitted.”

“Acquittal does not erase the national security implications of—”

“Careful.”

The word leaves Leon’s mouth before he can think better of it.

The president goes still, but Leon doesn’t apologise. There are things he will tolerate from the government, and there are things he won’t.

“I am not questioning the verdict,” Richards says at length.

“Good.”

“But Ms. Wong’s access to classified information, combined with your own clearance and position, presents unprecedented security considerations.”

“What would you have her doing with the DSO then? Scrubbing floors?” Leon shakes his head. “A marriage certificate isn’t going to suddenly give her access to state secrets. If she wants state secrets, she’ll get them herself.”

“That is not the reassurance you believe it is.”

Leon shrugs. “She’s nothing if not diligent.”

For a long moment, Richards just looks at him. Then he sighs in that annoyed way of his and says, “Let me understand your position. You are saying that Ada Wong will agree to join the DSO if the United States government permits the two of you to marry.”

“Yes.”

“Have you discussed this with her?”

“No.”

“Then how do you know?”

“She’ll do it.”

“And if she doesn't?”

“Then take me out back and shoot me, I don’t know.”

Richards snorts at that. “Why would I ever want to do that?”

“Why do you want to do any of it?” Leon asks, tilting his head. “It’s election year. These are your last months of power. I’ll ask her, and I want her to say yes. I think she will say yes. But I’m not making the decision for her. I’ll make the offer. She decides.”

“I will see what can be done,” Richards says. “About the marriage license.”

“That’s politician for ‘no’.”

“I am a politician.”

“Exactly.”

“What do you want me to say?”

Leon shrugs. “Yes.”

“Kennedy.”

“You asked me for Ada; I’m telling you the price.”

“She’s not yours to trade, is she?”

The words hit harder than the president could possibly know. They freeze his muscles, making him go very still. When he blinks, he’s back in Margaret’s office—there are tears in his eyes; he’s angry.

You went against her wishes.

To save her.

He looks down at his hands. “No,” he says. “She’s not.” Leon lifts his gaze. “That’s why you’re not making this deal with me. You’re making her an offer. A real one. A position in the DSO, salary, benefits, her own clearance, and the same legal protections as every other agent. No unofficial arrangement where you use her when it’s convenient and deny knowing her when it isn’t.”

Richards’ lips press into a thin line. Yet, he says, “That can be arranged.”

“And if she says no, she says no.”

“Yes,” he says begrudgingly.

“And you approve our marriage licence regardless.”

His eyebrows raise. “That wasn’t the original deal.”

“There is no deal, as I just said.”

“Kennedy.”

He hums. “You just reminded me she’s not mine to trade,” Leon says, smiling. “Good point, by the way.”

Richards’ hands clench in his lap. “You are extraordinarily irritating.”

“I’ve been told.”

“Frequently, I imagine.”

“Mostly by my fiancée.” It feels good to call her that. To walk around knowing that’s what she is.

The president stands, but Leon remains seated. He watches as the other man walks towards the windows, hands clasped behind his back.

All Leon can think about is how much he wants to marry Ada. God, he wants to marry her. He wants the certificate and the rings and the right to call her his wife.

But Ada already comes home to him. Her clothes hang next to his. The bathroom cabinet is filled to the brim with her creams and lotions. Her hand finds his beneath the covers. She talks to him while he cooks. Sings to him when he can’t sleep. Laughs at his jokes and steals his blankets and complains about his snoring.

None of that can be rejected by a clerk.

Still. Leon wants the paper. He wants the government that spent decades hunting her, using her, trying her, and watching her to put her name beside his and acknowledge that she belongs to no one but herself—and that she chose him.

The president turns around. “Fine.”

Leon blinks. “Fine?”

“If Ms. Wong accepts a position with the DSO, I will personally ensure that the… security objections to your marriage are withdrawn. Regardless of whether she accepts.”

“You promise?”

“I am the president of the United States.”

“That’s not what I asked.”

Finally, through what appears to be considerable effort, Richards says, “You have my word.”

Leon stands. “Great.”

“That does not mean the process will be immediate.”

“How long?”

“Kennedy.”

“A week?”

“No.”

“Two?”

“Get out of my office.”

“Can we have a summer wedding? Don’t worry about clearing your schedule, though. I doubt you’ll make the guest list, sir.”

“Out.”

⟡

July, 2021.

“Is this smart?” Ada asks.

Leon grins at her. “What do you mean?”

She purses her lips. “They’ll be disappointed.”

“By us getting married?” he asks. “They’re our friends, Ada.”

She goes around the table, lighting the candles while Leon sets out the cutlery.

“Sophie and Nari will be upset if we don’t throw a big party,” she says.

“Well. It’s our wedding.”

“They can be very persuasive.”

“I’m sure you can resist,” Leon chuckles.

“I’m not.”

He smiles, and when the last fork is in place, he wraps his arm around her waist to pull her into a kiss. She goes willingly, candles forgotten for a moment. He’s so warm, and his lips are softer now that she’s there to remind him to moisturise them. He’s also bigger than he’s ever been, arms and chest corded with thick muscles that make her embarrassingly weak in the knees. It’s almost hard to imagine—or remember, rather—what he’d looked like when they’d met. How small he’d been. How innocent.

“I don’t care,” he says against her lips. When he pulls back, he leans his forehead against hers. “I just want the paper. I want it to be official. I don’t care if we do it tomorrow at the courthouse, or if we wait five years and do it on a tropical beach. As long as you’re my wife at the end of it.”

“You’ll be sick of me,” she sighs softly, reaching up to scratch at his five o’clock shadow. “Seeing me at home and at work.”

“I could never be sick of you.”

“Sap,” she snorts.

Leon steals another kiss before he draws away and heads to the kitchen for the wine glasses. “So what?”

𓇬

Dinner is easy. It’s not familiar, exactly, but the people around the table are enough so that it’s bearable. Leon cooked too much, because he always seems to cook too much when he is nervous (it’s something new she’s realised about him, and it’s… She wants to say adorable, if a man over forty is allowed to be adorable), and despite his continued insistence that he is not nervous, there are enough roasted vegetables and bread rolls on the table to suggest he expected at least four more people.

Sophie notices, of course, because she notices most things. She teases Leon about it, and he blushes and smiles bashfully and shifts a little, which does wonders for how his shirt hugs his body. Nari notices too, though she is kinder about it, or at least quieter. Claire and Jill don’t comment on it except to say they missed out on lunch so they’re hungry, their not-so subtle way of reassuring Leon that it’s okay to over-cook—as long as it’s good. Which it always is.

Helena brings a bottle of something a friend made at home—alcohol free, of course, but fizzy and faintly pink. Sherry brings dessert, despite Leon telling her he’s got it under control. Chris brings nothing, because he seems to think being Chris is contribution enough. Perhaps it is, considering how seldom they see him now. The only one missing is Ingrid, called into work at the last minute.

It is strange, Ada thinks, how quickly a home can become full. Not just occupied, but full.

Their apartment is not small, but it has never felt as lived-in as it does tonight, with coats hung by the door and shoes abandoned carelessly in the hallway and voices carrying between the dining room and kitchen.

Ada watches all of it from the edge of the kitchen, briefly overcome by the sensation that she has stumbled into someone else’s life. Then Leon’s hand finds the small of her back as he passes behind her, warm and easy, and the thought loses some of its teeth.

It is her life, then.

Apparently.

The evening settles around them. They eat, and Leon accepts compliments on the food with embarrassed delight. It feels… almost normal. That is another mistake, perhaps. Letting herself think of it that way. Because normal things are rarely as harmless as they seem. Normal is what you lose when everything goes wrong, and Ada does not want to lose this.

Ada waits until dinner is mostly finished, until plates are empty and everyone has softened into loose, comfortable conversation. Leon’s knee is pressed against hers beneath the table—it has been for most of the night, though neither of them has acknowledged it. He is warm through the fabric of his trousers, solid and present, and when Ada glances at him, he gives her the smallest smile.

She could still let him do it—tell their friends they’re finally getting married. It’s not even a big thing, really. It’s certainly not bad. Yet there’s a lump in her throat and a baseline reluctance to speak up.

How is something like this almost as terrifying as facing the government in court? Or finding Leon half-dead in a hospital room? She’s killed people. She’s survived too much to be frightened by announcing her own marriage.

If she asked him to do it, he would. He’d clear his throat, and he’d simply say it. He would tell them they were getting married, and Sherry would cry, and Claire would beam, and Chris would pretend not to be emotional, and Ada could sit beside him, composed and untouchable. Blah, blah.

But that feels cowardly.

So Ada reaches for her glass, takes one sip she does not need, and says, “Leon and I are getting married next month.”

The conversation stops so completely that, for a moment, they can hear the hum of the refrigerator and the laundry machine going.

Right. The nice blue sheets. Can’t forget to put those in the dryer.

Ada looks down at her glass. She does not look at Leon, because she can feel him looking at her and knows, with irritating certainty, that he is smiling.

For a moment, no one says anything.

Then Claire says, very softly, “You are?”

And Ada looks up to take in the frankly ridiculous way they’re all staring at her. As if she has placed a little baby animal in the middle of the table and now they are afraid to move too quickly and startle it.

“Yes,” Leon says, because Ada does not. His voice is warm enough that something in her chest pulls tight. “We are.”

He laughs under his breath as he takes in their friends’ stunned silence and reaches for Ada’s hand beneath the table. She lets him, and his fingers slide between hers. It’s grounding enough for her to endure the way Sherry wipes at her cheeks—already crying, apparently.

“When?” Sophie asks. She sounds almost out of breath.

“The seventeenth,” Ada says.

“Of next month?” She arches an eyebrow—the wonder in her voice gone in an instant. “Where?”

“The courthouse,” Leon says.

The room shifts again. It is subtle, but Ada is good at subtle.

“The courthouse,” Nari repeats, eyebrows drawn together.

“Yes,” Ada says, and she straightens. “That is where one usually goes for legal matters.”

“Marriage isn’t only a legal matter,” Jill says.

Ada narrows her eyes without really thinking about it. “It is… primarily a legal matter.” Helena puts her forearms on the table and leans forward, gaze shifting from Ada to Leon and back again. “And afterwards?”

Ada hesitates for half a second too long. It’s not exactly hard to pick up on the fact that no one at the table seems to agree with their wedding plans.

Leon, unfortunately, answers. “Home, probably.”

This silence is worse. Ada feels it land around the table, heavier than the first. Before, they had been surprised. Now, they are… offended?

“Home,” Claire says flatly.

“Yes,” Leon replies.

Ada wonders if he’s picking up on it, too, or if he’s somehow ignorant to their frowns and questioning eyes.

“Really?” Sherry asks, cheeks dry now. “After your wedding?”

“That is where people go when an event ends,” Ada says. Her hand clenches around her dessert spoon.

“Ada.” It’s Chris—inserting himself into this conversation that Ada does not want him to be a part of.

“What?”

He arches an eyebrow. “A wedding isn’t a regular event,” he says. His gaze moves around the table, and Ada follows it, studying their friends again.

Claire looks genuinely wounded.

That is not what Ada expected. Sophie and Nari, yes. They are lawyers. Interference is in their blood. But Claire’s hurt surprises Ada. Maybe because Claire seems so… untouchable. Not cold, not at all. But she’d been nineteen in Raccoon City and still made it out, saving Sherry’s life and killing William Birkin. For some reason, it clashes in Ada’s mind. How can a person that strong be upset about something this trivial?

“We don’t want a big thing,” Leon says. His voice is careful now, softening the edges of her refusal. Ada wants to be irritated with him for it, but she knows he is not undermining her. “We just want to get married,” he continues. “That’s all.”

“That’s not all,” Nari says, quietly.

Ada looks at her, too. But her expression isn’t the plotting, scheming one Ada expected to find. Instead, Nari, too, looks upset.

“It’s not only yours,” Helena supplies.

Sophie inhales as if preparing to argue, but Jill speaks first. “I think what everyone means,” she says, voice kind and even, “is that we love you—both of you. We want to be there.”

“It is our marriage,” Ada says.

“Yes,” Jill says. “It is.”

“And our wedding.”

“Yes.”

Ada’s brows draw together. “And yet?”

“And yet,” Jill agrees.

Ada hates reasonable people. She would rather be shot at or attacked by some freak mutation than stare at Jill Valentine as she picks apart the logic of Ada’s refusal.

Across the table, Claire reaches for her glass then, apparently, changes her mind and folds both hands around the stem without drinking. “I was there at the beginning,” she says.

Raccoon City presses itself into the room, uninvited, but impossible to ignore. It is strange how easily it can appear, even now. One minute, she feels Leon’s hand in hers, and the next, she’s there —in the thick of it. She feels the rain on her skin, hears the dead through the walls of the overrun police station. Sees, feels, hears Leon at twenty-one with blood on his uniform—from the bullet he took for her—and too much trust in his eyes.

“He thought you were dead,” Claire continues.

Leon looks down, and his hand tightens on Ada’s. He doesn’t like talking about it. How he dropped her. How she tricked him into trusting her.

Ada keeps her face still. “I know.”

“For years,” Claire says, and there’s a sharpness to her voice this time.

“I know.”

“He talked about you.”

Leon clears his throat. “Claire.”

She ignores him, eyes still on Ada. “I’m not saying that to make you feel guilty.”

Guilt is familiar; guilt has shape and rules. Ada can work with guilt. But this is something else. This is being told, gently and without accusation, that her absence has weight. That the story she has always thought of as something private between herself and Leon has spilled into other people’s lives. That there were witnesses, even when she believed herself unwatched.

For most of her life, Ada has treated being seen as danger.

It is difficult to teach the body otherwise.

Sophie leans forward. “You don’t have to have a big wedding,” she says.

Ada almost argues.

To her, marriage has always been a legal contract. It is recognised by the state, recorded in documents, entangled with rights and obligations. It is paperwork. It is practical. It is, in their case, a particularly hard-won piece of paper the government has finally agreed to stop withholding.

But the argument feels hollow before she can give it voice.

Because Ada knows it is not only paperwork. If it were only paperwork, she would not have kept the first ring Leon gave her hidden for years together with the necklace he gave her so long ago now—the one she doesn’t even take off to shower these days. If it were only paperwork, she would not have proposed. She would not have watched his face change when she asked, would not have felt something inside her loosen and break and remake itself when he said yes.

And perhaps once, many years ago, marriage was love to her. Her parents, grandparents, her neighbours and teachers. Maybe Ada looked at them and dreamt of her own wedding one day, her own big love.

Wesninski took so much from her. Will she let him take this, too?

Still, a party feels different. It means people. People looking. People knowing. People placing their hands on something Ada has spent more than two decades protecting with the futile tools at her disposal.

Precious things are safer when they are hidden.

The thought arrives with such certainty that Ada almost accepts it as truth.

But is it true, or is it simply what she learned? Her childhood had been full of lessons she knows she should try to unlearn now.

Never tell anyone what you want. Never reveal attachment. Never stay somewhere long enough to be found. Never give anyone a list of things they can take from you.

Nathan taught those lessons well, and for so long, the world confirmed them. It took from her, forced her to run. It beat her down and never helped her back up.

But no one at the table wants to take Leon from her—that should be obvious. And it is obvious. But knowing something is obvious has never prevented fear from making an argument.

Chris sits at the far end of the table, arms folded, watching her with an expression she cannot quite read. He looks older than he did when she first met him, though perhaps everyone does now. There is grey in his beard and exhaustion in the set of his shoulders. He has buried enough people to understand the value of celebration perhaps better than most.

He has a child at home. A daughter that will be raised in a good home, knowing the love of a father who died for her, and of the man who took her in.

Where’s her mother? Maybe one day Ada will dare to ask.

“We don’t get a lot of good days,” he says, eyes flickering to his sister. “We get memorials. Funerals. We worry, and we keep losing. So if you two are finally getting married, I think that’s worth celebrating. For once, we win something.”

“Amen,” Helena exhales and lifts her glass slightly. When she speaks again, there’s a somewhat forced light playfulness to it. “Also, for what it’s worth, I have listened to Leon talk about you for years; I deserve some kind of compensation.”

Leon makes an offended sound. “I did not talk about her that much.”

Helena looks at him. “Sure.”

“I talked about other things too.”

“Occasionally.”

Ada’s mouth twitches despite herself.

“I just want to see it,” Sherry says. “That’s all. I know you don’t need us there. I know it’s real either way. But I want to be there when something good happens to you.”

Something good.

An embarrassingly simple phrase, all things considered. But how often has Ada allowed that? How often has something good happened without her immediately searching for the cost? Without assuming it was bait, leverage, weakness?

Leon’s thumb moves gently over her knuckles.

Ada thinks of the ten-year-old girl she has recently begun imagining with an… uncomfortable frequency. The girl whose face she cannot quite remember now. Small, frightened, learning too quickly that love could be used against her.

What would that child think of a room like this?

She looks at Leon. Again. Because he’s always safe to look at, no matter the expression he wears. His eyes always soften when they meet hers, his lips quirk into a smile nearly every time.

Now his expression is open, but carefully so. There is want in it. He tries to hide it, but Ada knows him too well now. He wants the paper, yes. He wants the quiet morning after. He wants her beside him in the courthouse, signing her name next to his.

But he also wants this.

She can see that now. It makes her wonder if it’s always been what he wanted. He’s had years to picture it, after all, while Ada has been fruitlessly hiding away from her own feelings. No doubt Leon has dreams, though. An idea of what their wedding should look like.

The spectacle of it doesn’t necessarily feel like him. But he wants their friends there. He wants witnesses—people to see him become hers and she become his, not because he doubts it will be true otherwise, but because Leon has never learned to separate love from being seen. He has spent his entire life reaching for people, and too often they’ve slipped through his fingers. Of course he wants to stand in a room and say, with all the people he loves around him, that Ada chose him back.

With her eyes locked on his, a little lighter now with age, she says, “One party.”

The reaction is immediate but not as loud as she expected. Perhaps they hear the warning in her voice. Perhaps they understand, in some collective instinct, that if they move too quickly, she may take it back.

“One party,” Sophie repeats, grinning. “Small.”

“Small,” Nari agrees too quickly.

Ada points at them. “Do not use that tone.”

“What tone?” Sophie says innocently.

“I know you,” Ada says, arching an eyebrow. “I know when you’re plotting.”

Sophie scoffs half-heartedly. “Whatever, Mom.”

Next to her, Nari hides a smile behind her hand.
