“Oh.”

“Which… I get it. Who goes shopping for a wedding dress alone?”

Though it would only make things worse, Leon almost tells her that she didn’t have to go alone. Sophie and Nari would have jumped at the opportunity, and it’s not like Claire, Jill, Sherry, Ingrid, or Helena would have said no if they were asked—even if they were Leon’s friends first.

“Yeah.”

“I said she was dead.”

Leon rubs his hand slowly down her back. She assumes her parents are both dead, though he suspects they’ll never know. The little information recovered for the trial had led nowhere.

“And then she apologised,” Ada continues. “She said she was sorry for my loss. I didn’t know what to say.”

She sounds so young in the rare moments she opens up like this, and she’s so small when she’s curled up against him. It tugs on every single one of Leon’s heartstrings, and he wishes there was something he could do to fix all of it. If he was granted a single wish in life, it would be to undo Ada’s pain. If that meant that Wesninski never took her and she never came to the U.S. and never met Leon, then so be it.

“You didn’t have to say anything,” he tells her and hears Margret’s voice lace with his own. “You don’t owe anyone anything.”

“She asked if there was someone else I wanted there,” she says. “I said no. But… I think I did.”

He keeps his hand moving over her back as if it does anything to relieve the pain she’s feeling.

She shifts again, drawing back enough to look at him. “I don’t want you to feel sorry for me.”

“I don’t,” he says, unsure himself if he’s lying or not.

Ada stares at him, and Leon traces his thumb lightly over her cheek and under her eye. But she pulls away, rolling out of his arms and onto her back again.

“I don’t understand why it matters,” she says. “I’ve done everything alone.”

He sighs. “Ada…”

But she continues, her voice growing thicker as she speaks. “I went into that shop alone because I wanted to. Then I stood there in that ridiculous dress, and suddenly…” She trails off, and Leon waits. “Suddenly I wanted my mother.”

“Ada,” he says again. His fingers graze her bicep.

“I don’t even remember her properly,” she says. “I don’t know if she would have come. I don’t know if she would have liked me. I don’t know anything about her. I don’t even know if she wanted me, Leon.”

His eyes burn with tears, and he pulls her back into his arms before he can really think twice. She lets him, though, tucking herself back into his chest and letting out a string of breaths that sound a little too close to tears.

Her fingers tighten in his shirt. “You want me.”

“I do,” he says. “I’ve wanted you since I met you, Ada.”

She nods. Then, “I was doing perfectly well before you.”

He’s so surprised by her words that he snorts. “Okay.”

“I was,” she argues.

“Ada.”

She draws back enough to glare at him. “I was chosen to get the G-sample and take down Umbrella.”

“You were a child soldier.”

Her eyes roll, and she pinches his side. “Whatever.”

“No,” he says, catching her hand. He brings her knuckles to his mouth and kisses them. “Not whatever.”

For a little while, they are quiet again as Ada settles back against him.

Then Ada says, “The dress has embroidery. Little flowers along the neckline.”

Leon’s hands tighten almost instinctively on hers. “Oh?”

“Don’t sound so excited.”

“Am I not allowed to be excited about my own wedding?”

She shifts against him, and he can hear the smile in her voice when she says, “You can’t see it until then, you know.”

“I know,” he says—a new lightness to his voice now that Ada is smiling again.

“And you can’t ask Nari.”

He scoffs—offended. “I wasn’t going to.”

“You absolutely were.”

“I was gonna ask Sophie,” he says. “She has the better poker face.”

Ada lifts her head, and Leon grins at her. “You’re not funny,” she says.

“No, I think I am.”

“You’re wrong.”

He kisses her before she can continue arguing, and she relaxes against him again. She still tastes like the mint from her toothpaste.

When the kiss ends, she stays close enough that their noses brush.

“I really like it,” she says.

“The dress?”

“Yes.”

“Then I will too.”

“You haven’t seen it.”

“Don’t care.”

“What if it’s ugly?”

“Then you’ll be hot in an ugly dress.”

She snorts, and Leon smiles.

There are still things he wants to ask.

He wants to know what she thought when she looked at herself in the mirror. He wants to know if she cried. He wants to know whether the woman in the shop was kind to her, whether someone helped her with the buttons or the zipper or whatever the hell wedding dresses have, whether Ada stood alone on a little platform while strangers told her she looked beautiful.

He wants to go backwards in time and wait outside. He wouldn’t follow her inside, because he knows she wouldn’t have wanted him there. But he’d want to be nearby. Close enough that if she stepped out of the shop and realised she needed someone, she would find him.

“You know,” he says, “if you have to go back for alterations or anything, you could ask someone to come.”

“I know.”

“And if you want to go alone, that's okay too.”

Ada looks at him for a long moment. “Thank you.”

She kisses him this time. Soft and sweet.

Then she rolls away.

Leon blinks. “What are you doing?”

“Going to sleep.”

He stares at her back. “Ada.”

“What?”

“You woke me up by grabbing my dick.”

“And?” she asks through a yawn.

“And then you made me have an emotional breakthrough.”

“I think I had the breakthrough.”

“I was there, though.”

“Do you want a participation medal?”

Leon stares at the ceiling until Ada reaches back and finds his arm, pulling it around her waist again.

“You love me,” she murmurs.

Leon presses his face into the back of her neck. It’s probably his favourite place to be, unless inside her counts, too.

“I do.”

“That’s for the wedding, silly.”

⟡

May, 2022.

Rosemary Winters does not want to give Ada the ball.

Ada has been trying to negotiate with her for nearly five minutes, which is about four and a half minutes longer than she would ordinarily spend negotiating with anyone. Rose receives special dispensation, because she can’t really talk yet.

The little red ball is clutched tightly against Rose’s chest, protected by both arms, while she stares at Ada with a wide, mischievous smile.

“Rose,” Ada says. “Give me the ball.”

Rose shakes her head, her toothy smile only widening. Ada narrows her eyes in turn, crouching down.

Behind her, from the direction of the patio, Chris says, “She’s not going to give it to you.”

Ada looks over her shoulder.

Chris and Leon are standing beside the grill, both of them holding bottles—Chris is drinking beer, and Leon a Coke—despite the fact that Chris is the only one actually doing anything. Leon tried to help when they first arrived, only to be banished from the grill after disagreeing with Chris about something Ada cannot imagine matters in the slightest. Apparently, barbecuing is some kind of art that Chris Redfield thinks he has a monopoly on.

“Did I ask?” she calls.

“No.”

“Then mind the meat.”

Chris rolls his eyes.

When Ada turns back around, Rose is escaping. The little girl runs across the lawn with all the speed available to an eighteen-month-old, which is not… particularly impressive, but Ada gives chase anyway.

Slowly. God. Why is it harder to run so slowly than to run fast? She feels a little ridiculous. She knows she probably looks ridiculous. She’s not entirely sure why she’s indulging Rose at all. She could catch her before the girl managed another three steps.

But… something about the shrieking laughter coming from her makes Ada keep her distance. She reaches out once, letting her fingers brush the back of Rose’s dress, then deliberately misses when Rose squeals and changes direction.

“Rosemary,” Ada calls after her. Rose runs faster—barely. “You’re only making this worse for yourself!”

The child has no concern for consequences, which Ada supposes is probably a good thing.

She catches up when Rose gets too close to the hedge, scooping her up around the waist before she can tumble directly into it. Rose shrieks and kicks her legs, still clutching the ball against her chest.

Ada holds her out in front of herself. “Do you surrender?” Rose beams at her. “I’ll take that as a no.”

She brings the girl against her hip instead, adjusting her hold with newfound instinct when Rose squirms.

She has never spent much time around children, and until recently, she had assumed that was for the best. Children are fragile, loud, and alarmingly trusting. They put their fingers in places they shouldn’t and seem determined to injure themselves on absolutely everything. Ada honestly finds it hard to believe that she ever was a child. It feels like it would make more sense to learn she’s some man-made Umbrella product.

Rose herself isn’t very frightening to Ada—obviously—but the trust she’s given is admittedly terrifying; Rose does not hesitate before reaching for Ada. She does not watch her face for signs of anger or test her mood before deciding whether it is safe to approach.

Rose does not know what Ada has done. To her, Ada is just another warm embrace and friend to play with.

The ball slips from Rose’s hands and lands in the grass. Ada stops it beneath her foot before it can roll away.

“Show-off,” Leon calls.

Ada looks towards him. “What?”

“Nothing.”

“Stop staring.”

He smiles. “I wasn’t staring.”

Chris snorts beside him, but Ada ignores them both.

Rose, however, has noticed Leon.

“Lee!” Rose stretches both arms towards the patio.

Ada looks down at her. “Really?” she asks. “After everything I’ve done for you?”

“Lee!”

“I see.” Then, more quietly, she says, “I guess I like him, too.”

Rose continues reaching, so, left with no choice, Ada sighs and carries her across the lawn.

She has learned that Leon is Rose’s favourite, despite the few times they’ve met. Obviously, Chris objects to this whenever Ada points it out, but the evidence is overwhelming. Rose likes Chris, certainly. She runs to him when he enters a room and cries when he leaves, but there is something about Leon that made Rose trust him almost immediately.

Ada understands it a little too well.

When they reach the patio, Rose practically throws herself from Ada’s arms into Leon’s. He puts down his bottle and catches her easily, settling her against his hip.

“Hey, Rosie,” he says, bouncing her lightly.

Rose touches his face, grassy fingers smearing over his cheeks and lips, but it only makes Leon smile.

Ada almost can’t bear to watch it.

There are certain things she has become accustomed to, and others that still catch her off guard. Leon with Rose is one of them. She has seen him frightened, delirious, drunk, furious, and half- dead. She has seen him kill without hesitation and cry both with and without shame. She knows what he looks like when he wakes from a nightmare and what his face does in the few seconds before he falls asleep.

Yet something about seeing him hold Rose still reaches inside her and presses against a place she has only recently begun acknowledging exists.

She does not want children. That remains true.

Her realisation about Sophie’s pregnancy has not changed that. Ada has examined the feeling carefully, turning it over until she understands its shape, and there is still no hidden longing for motherhood beneath it.

But Rose is different. She already exists. She is not an imagined child Ada is expected to create or raise or somehow know how to love correctly. She is simply Rose, who likes strawberries, who screams whenever Chris tries to wash her hair and has recently learned the word no.

Rose, who reaches for Ada without knowing anything about her.

“Eya.”

Ada blinks. Rose is looking at her from Leon’s arms.

“Yes?” she asks as the little girl leans towards her. Ada frowns. “What?”

Rose reaches further, prompting Ada to take one step closer, and Rose presses a wet, clumsy kiss against her cheek.

For a moment, Ada goes completely still.

Rose draws back, visibly pleased with herself when she tucks herself into the crook of Leon’s neck. Her fingers tug at his beard, a little longer now than he’s ever kept it before.

“I agree, Rosie,” Leon says. Her fingers slip into his mouth, and he pries them out. “You wanna keep those, don’t you? Don’t want me to eat them.”

Chris’ laugh blends in with the sizzling of meat as he flips the steaks over the fire.

Still reeling a little from the unexpected affection, Ada reaches up to straighten Rose’s slightly crooked sun hat and tuck a strand of fine blonde hair behind her ear—just to occupy her hands.

“Very manipulative,” she murmurs. “You’ll go far.”

“Hey,” Chris says, though he’s still smiling. “I’m teaching her to be an upstanding citizen.”

“Uhuh,” Leon says. “Like you?”

“I’m very upstanding,” Chris scoffs. “And look at Claire! Didn’t I manage it once?”

“Claire’s smart enough to have raised herself,” Leon teases.

“So is this one,” Ada says, without really meaning to.

But Rose smiles, her eyes on Ada, and she feels that strange warmth again.

It is difficult, sometimes, to reconcile the things she knows about herself with the things that continue to happen.

She knows she is not particularly maternal and that she does not want children.

She knows that once, years ago, she would have considered a child nothing but a liability on a mission.

Yet she also knows that if Rose disappeared, Ada would tear the world apart to find her—just like Ethan.

Perhaps there does not need to be a contradiction. If she loves Rose, perhaps that does not mean she secretly wants to be a mother. Perhaps loving one child simply means loving one child.

Leon seems to specialise in teaching her that the most complicated things occasionally are. Though that’s just because he’s had a therapist—so he’s cheating, really.

Rose squirms until Leon puts her down, and the moment her feet touch the grass, she turns back towards the lawn. The ball is still where Ada left it, and Rose runs towards it.

“Go on,” Leon says, his hand on Ada’s lower back.

She looks at him. “What?”

“You were playing,” he smiles. “There’s still time before we eat. Right, Chris?”

Chris barely looks away from the grill. “Yup.”

Ada gives them both a flat look. “I am not playing.”

Leon nods. “Okay.”

“I’m just keeping her occupied.” Ada narrows her eyes.

Leon takes a sip of his Coke, all faux nonchalance. But she knows that expression. That quiet, infuriating amusement that says he believes he knows something about her she has not yet realised herself.

Nevertheless, she turns around and follows Rose across the lawn.

The child has retrieved the ball by the time Ada reaches her, and Ada crouches several feet away and holds out her hands.

“Come on, then.”

Rose clutches the ball. Ada waits—for a second, she expects the girl to run again. But instead, Rose toddles towards her, and when she reaches Ada, she holds out the ball with both hands.

Ada takes it. “Thank you.”

Rose claps and squeals with delight, which, for some reason, makes Ada laugh. Loud and unhindered enough that she looks towards the patio. Unsurprisingly, Leon is watching her.

Beside him, Chris is saying something Ada cannot hear, and Leon responds without taking his eyes off her. There is a softness in his expression, and she can’t help but think she likes being looked at that way.

Then Rose grabs at her knee, demanding her attention again.

Ada looks down. “What?”

Rose points at the ball in Ada’s hands.

“You just gave this to me.”

Rose points more insistently. “Ball.”

Ada raises an eyebrow. “Yes. Ball.”

“Ball!” Rose says, squatting down and curling into as much of a ball shape she can manage.

Ada rolls the ball across the grass, and immediately Rose squeals and runs after it.

And for the next several minutes, Ada does nothing important. Her thoughts are free of work ane the wedding—which is only two months away now.

She simply rolls a ball. And when Rose brings it back, Ada rolls it again.

Behind her, Chris and Leon argue over the grill, and the smell of smoke and cooking meat drifts across the lawn. The sun is warm against Ada’s bare arms.

For a moment—for the rest of the day, at least—there is nothing to survive.

It’s surprisingly easy to stand.

⟡

June, 2022.

“I was gone for three weeks,” he says when Ada asks him why, exactly, they are spending a perfectly good Saturday afternoon wandering through a crowded shopping centre. “I want to do something normal.”

Ada looks around. “This is your idea of normal?”

“I don’t know. People go to malls.”

“Do they?”

“I think so.”

She snorts. “Maybe in 1998.”

Leon rolls his eyes. “You’re enjoying yourself.”

“I’m really not,” she says, fingers brushing over a thick coat hanging on a clearance rack outside a store.

Leon has been home from a mission for three days, and Ada has barely let him out of her sight since he returned. She will not admit this, of course, because she’s Ada. Still, she has followed him from room to room with unusual frequency, appearing in the kitchen while he cooks and sitting on the bathroom counter while he shaves.

The first night home, he woke at four in the morning to find her just… staring at him. When he asked what she was doing, she closed her eyes and told him to go back to sleep—as if he was the one doing something weird.

So, they’re at the mall. Mostly because Leon wanted to leave the apartment to do something, well, normal. He wanted coffee. Maybe lunch. Besides, he also needs new running shoes, and Ada could use some more casual-at-home-clothes.

Ada, of course, protested the mall with a long list of arguments before they’d even left the bed. All valid, to be fair. But Leon wanted to feel normal. So he convinced her. With kisses that trailed from her lips to her neck to her collarbones. Over her nipples and down her stomach. Then with his tongue between her legs.

Now she walks beside him with a paper cup of coffee in one hand and her sunglasses pushed into her hair. She is wearing jeans and one of Leon’s old jackets—displaying that, clearly, she does need to buy clothes.

“Shoes first,” he says, scanning the imposing space for some kind of athletics store.

Ada makes a sound. “Food first.”

“We just had breakfast.”

She sighs. “So? You drag me here, and you can’t even buy me food?”

Leon smiles. “Fine. Food first.”

Ada looks pleased with herself, and it’s all okay. Leon would walk barefoot for the rest of his life just to see that expression again.

He reaches for her hand, a sudden need to touch her washing over him.

It is not something they do often in public. Primarily because they rarely go out like this together. Ada tends to want to do most things solo, which Leon respects. He supposes that’s just how she’s used to things working. Unsurprisingly, it takes more than a year to get rid of old habits.

She looks down at their joined hands then up at him.

“What?” he asks.

She shakes her head. “Nothing.”

There’s, perhaps, the faintest little hint of a smile on her lips.

Leon is about to suggest they get something disgustingly sweet when he hears his name.

“Kennedy?”

He stops, and Ada does too. They turn toward the voice coming from behind them and see a man approaching. He looks to be somewhere in his sixties, perhaps older—Leon is getting increasingly bad at estimating the ages of people older than himself. He is tall and broad through the shoulders, dressed in an expensive coat, and there is something familiar about him. But when Leon searches his memory, he finds nothing.

“Leon Kennedy,” the man says again.

Leon gives him the polite smile he perfected sometime in the early 2000’s. “Yeah?”

The man stops several feet away. His face is open, clearly somewhat excited to have spotted Leon. But then his gaze moves to Ada.

The change in his expression is immediate. It’s as if his whole body changes. If the man could growl and bare his teeth, Leon is sure he would.

“Oh, you’ve got to be fucking kidding me,” he says sharply. The man laughs once, without humour. “You actually did it.”

Leon’s smile disappears. “Sorry?” he says. His hand tightens on Ada’s.

“You’re with her,” the man says, nodding towards her.

Leon moves half a step in front of Ada without thinking, but she immediately moves back beside him.

“Do I know you?” Leon asks.

“Gerald Hayes. I used to work with STRATCOM.”

Ah. Yes.

Leon remembers seeing him in meetings years ago. Before the DSO, even. A face at the far end of a conference table, someone who spoke about casualty projections so casually it made Leon feel sick to his stomach.

“You retired,” Leon says.

“I was encouraged to, as they say.”

Wasn’t this guy a buddy of Krauser’s? Leon has some faint recollection of hearing the two of them laughing, discussing a night out. He’s shocked he still remembers that, but it rings true in his mind.

“Sorry to hear that,” he says, trying to keep his voice level.

Hayes snorts. “No, you’re not.”

When Leon does not argue, he looks at Ada again. She’s gone still next to Leon, a stillness he recognises. It’s the stillness of someone who’s lost in their thoughts, unable to really decipher if this moment is actually happening right now.

“Ada,” he says quietly. Her gaze flicks towards him. “You want to go home?”

He’s already turned away, almost dragging Ada along when Hayes laughs.

“Of course,” he says. “Run away.”

Leon turns back to him. “Don’t.”

“Don’t what?” Hayes asks, puffing up his chest.

Leon wants to laugh. Does this guy think he’s gonna get into a physical fight with a federal agent in the middle of a mall? Does he think he’ll win it? He can’t laugh, though. He’s too angry. Seething almost. It ripples beneath his skin, ugly and too strong for what the situation warrants.

“Whatever you think you're doing,” he says, voice low.

“I thought I was getting the chance at chatting with an old colleague,” Hayes says. “But I suppose we weren’t really colleagues. You were always above the rest of us, weren’t you? President’s golden boy.”

The anger flares, bursting from his chest in waves. He’s sweating in his T-shirt, practically vibrating. If it weren’t for Ada’s hand still in his, Leon isn’t sure what he’d do. He’s not an angry man, not particularly violent either—despite what he does for a living. But Hayes has caught him at a bad time and pushed on all the wrong buttons.

Still, he manages to grit out a “Have a good day.”

But when he turns to leave, Ada does not move. He tugs on her hand, but her feet remain frozen in place. Her eyes are fixed on Hayes. To anyone else, the expression she wears is unreadable, but Leon can see that she’s angry, too.

Then Hayes says, attention on Ada, “What did it cost? To buy him?”

Leon steps forward, his hand slipping out of Ada’s grasp. “That’s enough,” he says, pointing at him.

“His career?” Hayes continues, looking past Leon at her. “His oath? How many classified reports did you get out of him over the years?”

“I said that’s enough.”

Hayes looks at him. “You lied for her.”

“Yes.”

The answer comes easily, and despite lifting the accusation, Hayes seems surprised by it.

Good.

Leon steps closer. “I lied in my reports. I lied in meetings and in courts and under oath. If you’ve got a problem with that, your problem is with me.”

Hayes’ gaze is ice cold. “You humiliated the country.”

Leon almost laughs.

There are many things he has done in his life for which he feels petrifying shame. Humiliating the United States government certainly is not one of them.

“I think the government will live,” he says.

Ada moves closer, coming to stand at his side once again.

Hayes shakes his head slowly. “You know how many people spent years trying to find her?”

“I do.”

“How many operations she compromised?”

“I do.”

“How many people died because people like her sold weapons to the highest bidder?”

Leon’s jaw tightens. “You don’t know anything about her.”

“I know exactly what she is.”

Leon feels Ada’s hand touch his arm. It’s light, almost nothing. But he stops himself. And then he realises he was moving forward, fists clenched.

Hayes, unfortunately, seems to be perceptive. His mouth twists. “There. That’s what I’m talking about. One touch, and you heel. Her bitch.”

Ada’s hand disappears, and this time she’s the one who closes the distance. “Walk away,” she says. Calm. Collected.

For a moment, the man hesitates. Then his gaze returns to Ada. “You should be ashamed of yourself.”

She meets his eyes. “Leave.”

Leon looks at her and finds her smiling. Not really smiling. It’s more like the expression she wore in court. Detached. Cold.

It sends a shiver down his spine, and it flushes away the anger—just like that. What remains is trepidation. A little fear, even.

“Ada…”

Her eyes remain on Hayes for a long moment, then she abruptly turns away, taking Leon’s hand in hers as she goes.

“Come on,” she says calmly. “I’m hungry.”

Hayes laughs behind them, and Leon wants desperately to turn around. To do what, he’s not entirely sure. The anger is mostly gone, and the ache to punch and break is completely so. But he still wants to do something. Even though it would be futile to argue. Hayes has formed an opinion on Ada, one that won’t change no matter how long Leon spends painting the picture of Ada’s real impact.

So he allows Ada to lead him away.

They leave the mall without buying shoes or clothes or food. They don’t even talk on the way to the parking garage, and Leon is feeling about one thousand things at once.

The traces of anger makes a rancid cocktail when mixed with his fear, his hurt. He feels stupid, too; did he really think he could just go out, the way actual normal people do? Did he think he’d earned that? Did he think he could do something nice with Ada without someone sticking their nose where it doesn’t belong?

As soon as they enter the parking garage, Ada drops his hand. She continues to stay quiet as they make their way to the car. Leon watches as she digs through her purse for the keys, face too blank. Too… crafted.

“Ada,” he says. He’s not sure what he’s supposed to say more. How he can make this right. Especially when she continues ignoring him. Still, “Ada.”

She unlocks the doors. The beep echoes slightly within the concrete structure. “I heard you.”

“Wait.” For what? Leon struggles to think.

“I want to go home,” she says, opening the passenger door.

Leon catches it before she can get inside—before it can swing back and hit her. But then Ada looks at his hand with that same empty expression, and Leon immediately lets go.

“Sorry,” he says.

Wordlessly, she gets into the car. Leon walks around to the driver’s side and climbs in.

The doors shut almost simultaneously, and then there’s just silence. Leon puts the key in the ignition but does not turn it. He’s not sure if he’s ready, just yet, to get onto the road. And he doesn’t want to spend the drive in silence, nor does he want to have this conversation while he can’t give it his full attention.

“He was an asshole,” he says—carefully. But Ada just stares through the windshield. “Ada?”

She lets out a breath. “I know.”

“He doesn’t know anything.”

“I know that.”

“Then talk to me.”

Her head turns sharply. “I said I know.”

He leans back against the seat, feeling oddly beaten. “Okay.”

What would Margaret do if Leon was acting this way? He’s sure he used to close himself off like this all the time. Those first sessions had been a struggle like no other. But that was years ago, and he can’t really remember. Can’t really focus, rather.

Ada looks away again, and he waits. For what, he doesn’t know. Something. Anything.

A minute passes. Maybe two. Maybe two hours. Leon watches people come and go through the windshield.

Finally, Ada says, “You shouldn't have said yes.”

Leon turns. “To what?”

She shakes her head. “I don’t know.”

He wants to reach for her, though he always does. But she’s hurting, and there’s nothing he can do. If he tried to touch her now, she’d reject him, and they’d both end up hurt for it. So he clenches his hands in his lap and breathes in through his nose.

“I don’t regret ever saying yes to you,” he tells her.

“Uhuh.”

“And I’m sorry for what I did at your trial, but I could never regret it. Not if it saved your life.”

“You could have lost everything.”

“I know.”

“You always fucking say that,” she says, frustration creeping into her words.

“Because I do know.”

“No, you don’t,” she says, and it sounds almost pleading now. “I don’t think you understand what you’ve done.”

He frowns. “I made a choice.”

“For me.”

“Yes.”

“That’s the problem. Don’t you see that? Y—you shouldn’t have. In Spain, you made the right choice to leave me. In China, too. What changed? Why did you change? Why did you choose to ruin everything?”

Leon knows better than to answer quickly. This is just like with Hayes, while being entirely different. Ada has made up her mind, and Leon won’t be able to change it—right in the moment at least. She needs time, he knows, to wear the thoughts down until she, too, understands how nonsensical they are.

Ruin? What has he ruined? Are they not together, about to get married? Are they both not employed by the government, are they both not alive? Leon fails to understand her perspective, because this is the best his life has ever been. How could she ever claim he ruined anything?

“I know I hurt you,” he says.

Before he can say more, Ada cuts in. “This isn’t about that.”

“Then what is it about?”

Ada looks at him.

For a second, Leon sees something raw in her face. But then it is gone just as quickly.

“You had a life before me.”

Leon scoffs. “What life?”

“You wanted to be a police officer. You went to the academy. You wanted to help people. You wanted to be good.”

“I still do.”

“But look at what happened,” she says, staring straight ahead. “Look at what happened to you.”

Leon blinks at her. “Ada.”

“You lied to your government. You lied in court. You’ve protected me for years. You nearly lost your whole career.”

“But I didn’t.”

“You could have.”

“But I didn’t.”

“Because you’re useful,” she snaps. “Because they decided what you can do for them matters more than what you did for me.”

He turns his body to face her as much as he can in the car. “What exactly are you trying to say?”

Ada doesn’t answer immediately, but Leon has nowhere to be. He has heaps of time. So he waits. Watches the minute movements of her face.

Then she says, “Maybe he was right.”

“No,” Leon says immediately. “No, Ada. He wasn’t.” He turns away then and starts the car. His hands are itching again, and though it’s extremely poor judgment, Leon needs some form of distraction. Unfortunately, driving is what’s most accessible. As he pulls out of the parking spot and drives towards the exit, he says, “So what if I had a life before you? If I hadn’t met you in ‘98, I would be dead now. We both know that. If you hadn’t been in Spain, I’d be dead right now. I love you, and nothing you or anyone says will ever change that, okay? A stranger at a fucking mall certainly isn’t going to make me regret the last two fucking decades of my life.”

Ada says nothing.

“I spent years believing that if someone gave me an order and put a flag behind it, it had to be the right thing. I wanted that to be true. God, Ada, I needed it to be true,” he says, voice tight. “For Claire, for Sherry… After Raccoon City, they stripped all my choices. Sent me to the army, sent me on missions more than likely to kill me. I never complained, not to their faces. I did my fucking duty.” He pauses to take a deep breath. A poor attempt to quell the emotion stirring inside him. “But before that, too… I was in the foster system, in and out of homes, never staying anywhere long. I never settled, never had roots. The academy and RPD was something I did because I felt like I had to. I had to pay back a debt.”

“Leon,” Ada says.

“You were the first thing I ever chose for myself.”

She looks away.

“I’ve done things I regret. I’ve followed orders I wish I hadn’t. I’ve trusted people I shouldn’t have. None of that is because of you.”

“You don’t know who you’d be if you never met me.”

“Ada,” he says, laughing a little hysterically. “I’d be dead. I would be twenty years dead if I never met you.”

She doesn’t say a word for the rest of the drive, so neither does he.

He has been so happy lately that sometimes, he feels stupid with it. He wakes up beside her. He goes to work with her. He comes home and finds her things mixed with his things, her life threaded so thoroughly through his own.

He has been thinking about August as the goal. The thing he’s been working towards since he proposed for the first time back in 2015. For once he has achieved something good. He saved someone who needed saving. He’s marrying the woman he loves!

Ada, apparently, has been looking at things differently. How did he miss that?

Maybe he knew. Maybe he just didn’t want to put two and two together. He’s known about her nightmares and her struggles with settling in alongside him, but he never spared a thought for how a wedding might stir up old fears and pains. Maybe he should have.

The car is barely in park in the garage beneath their building when Ada gets out. Sighing, Leon kills the engine before following her towards the elevators.

“Ada,” he says softly when he catches up to her.

She stares at the screen showing the floor numbers as the elevator descends. “Don’t.”

“I need you to listen to me.”

She scoffs.“No, you want me to listen while you tell me I’m wrong.”

“You are wrong.”

She laughs again. Leon reaches for her hand, but Ada pulls it away.

He wants to push. He wants to make her look at him and understand that the worst things in his life happened because of monsters and governments and bad luck. He wants to tell her that loving her has never been the tragedy.

He knows it will not matter right now.

They ride the elevator in silence. Enter the apartment in silence. Shed shoes and jackets in silence.

Then Ada says “I’m taking a shower” and walks off before Leon can even reply.

So he stands alone in the kitchen and puts the kettle on because he does not know what else to do.

He can hear water running through the pipes, tries to pull some kind of metaphor from that. At least she’s there. At least she’s alive. At least there’s a home to shower in and space to hide.

On the refrigerator, beneath a magnet from some stupid tourist shop in Finland, is the wedding invitation Sophie designed for them.

Leon looks at their names. He reads them twice. Then a third time.

Ada Wong & Leon S. Kennedy.

They haven’t discussed if either of them is changing their name yet. Will they? Should they?

It’s tempting, sometimes, to take her name. Simply because he knows people would be angry about it. Leon S. Wong.

Ada Kennedy.

He’s not sure if it matters.

As long as he gets the paper that she’s his wife, it literally doesn’t matter one bit.

The water cuts off. And a few seconds later, the kettle clicks. Leon busies himself with pouring the water into two mugs. Earl Grey for both of them, even though he doesn’t really like it. He does it because it gives his hands something to do, and because the alternative is standing in the middle of the kitchen listening for signs of Ada moving around the apartment.

He adds honey to her cup.

Hesitates.

Then adds a little more.

By the time Ada appears in the doorway, Leon is sitting at the kitchen island with his cup untouched between his hands. Her hair is wet and combed back from her face, darkening the shoulders of the robe she wears. His robe, technically, though he has long since given up on making such distinctions. Half the clothes in the apartment seem to have changed ownership at some point in the last year.

She stops just inside the kitchen, and her gaze falls to the mug waiting for her on the other side of the island.

“Tea?” she asks.

“Yeah. Earl Grey.”

She approaches slowly, wrapping both hands around the mug when she reaches it. Leon watches her take a sip.

“You put too much honey in it,” she says.

“Thought you could use something sweet.”

There is the slightest movement at the corner of her mouth, but Leon doesn’t smile back. He’s too tired now. Any adrenaline has long since drained from his body, leaving behind an aching heaviness that has settled into his shoulders and the space behind his eyes.

Ada watches him. “You’re angry with me,” she says.

Leon shakes his head. “No.”

“Leon.”

“I’m not angry.”

“Then you’re hurt.”

He looks down into his cup. “A little. Maybe.”

He expects her to retreat at that. Not physically, necessarily. But she has a habit of expertly evading emotions when they become too complicated to deal with directly. She might change the subject, or make a joke, or simply take her tea upstairs and return in an hour as if none of this happened.

Instead, she rounds the kitchen island. Leon follows her with his eyes as she comes to stand between his knees.

“What?” he asks.

Ada folds herself around him, arms coming around his neck. When Leon’s hands settle carefully on her waist, she makes no effort to get him to move. Her hair is still damp. It smells like his shampoo, even though he knows she just stocked up on her own.

“I’m sorry,” she says.

He closes his eyes. Breathes in the scent of something of his on her. “Ada.”

“No. Let me say it.”

Leon nods against her shoulder, but for a while, she doesn’t continue. He can almost feel her arranging the words in her mind, examining them for weakness before she allows them out. “I know he was wrong,” she says eventually. “I knew it while he was speaking, I think. But there are some things you can know without believing them.”

Leon’s fingers tighten slightly against the fabric of her robe. “Yeah,” he says. “I know, baby.”

“I don’t think you ruined your life for me.”

“Well. That’s good.”

“I think you’ve made some exceptionally stupid decisions for me.”

He huffs out a laugh despite himself. “Hey.”

Ada draws back just enough to look at him. “Exceptionally stupid.”

“I heard you the first time.”

“You testified for me after I explicitly told you not to.”

He smiles. “Yeah. Are we really doing this again?”

“We’ll do it once for every falsified report you wrote to protect me.”

“Technically, I mostly omitted—”

“Leon,” she sighs, but there is affection in it now. Leon can see it. More importantly, he can feel it in the way her fingers move into his hair, lightly scratching the back of his head. “I think you’ve done stupid things,” she continues. “But I’ve done stupid things for you, too.”

“Yes.”

Ada stares at him for a moment then laughs softly. The sound is enough to loosen the tension in Leon’s chest. He slides his arms more securely around her waist and pulls her closer until they’re pressed together. Ada allows it, her hands settling on his shoulders.

“I meant what I said in the car,” he tells her.

Her expression grows more serious. “Yeah.”

“No, I need you to know that.”

“I do, Leon.”

“You were the first thing I ever chose just because I wanted it.”

The admission feels a little embarrassing suddenly. It had come out in the car while he was angry and overwhelmed, and he hadn’t really considered the words before saying them. Now, with Ada standing between his legs in the quiet of their kitchen, he understands how painfully earnest they sound.

He looks away. “It sounds kind of pathetic when I say it like that.”

Her fingers touch his jaw, turning his face back towards her.

“I understand what you mean.” Ada studies him for several seconds before she continues. “When Wesninski took me, I stopped thinking about what I wanted. Not immediately, probably. I was still a child. I guess I… wanted to go home. Wanted my parents. I’m sure I wanted all kinds of things. But eventually I stopped. Wanting things was inconvenient. Usually painful. So I became good at doing what was necessary instead.” Her fingers trace the faint lines on his forehead, then they move over the moles on his neck. “I think you were the first thing I wanted too.”

Leon stares at her, emotions swelling uncontrollably. Before he knows it, he’s grinning, wide and uncontrollable, and he wishes there was more to do. More ways he could show through body language how giddy those words make him.

But clearly Ada picks up on it. She rolls her eyes, though her hand remains against his cheek. “Maybe not the first thing,” she corrects. “I wanted to kill Wesninski quite badly.”

“Okay. So I’m second.”

She tilts her head. “There were probably other things.”

“Third?”

“I’m sure I wanted a decent meal at some point,” she hums, taking a step back.

“Fourth, then.”

“Sleep. They kept me awake for days sometimes.” Another step back.

He laughs. “Fifth.”

“Money.”

“Come on, baby. What happened to lying all the time, huh? Lie to me! Tell me I was your biggest wish since the day you were born.”

Ada’s smiling when she says, “I would have liked a functional pair of shoes.”

Leon catches her around the waist and pulls her forward again. It pulls a laugh from her, and she grabs his shoulders to steady herself as he presses his face into her chest.

“Fine,” she says. “Somewhere in the top ten.”

“That’s all I wanted,” he sighs against her skin.

“I know.”

He looks up at her. The smile fades slowly from Ada’s face, but the softness remains.

“I wanted you in Raccoon City,” she continues. “I wanted you in Spain. I wanted you in China. I wanted you every time I walked away from you.”

Leon swallows. “Then why did you?”

She looks down at him as if the answer is obvious. “I didn’t know that wanting something was a reason to have it.” She brushes her thumb over the corner of his mouth. “I do now.”

Leon turns his head and kisses her palm. “So Hayes can go fuck himself?”

Ada considers it. “Yes.”

“Good.”

⟡

July, 2022.

Ada wonders if she should really take the car, too. Isn’t it bad enough that she’s leaving him? It would be unnecessary to deprive Leon of a car on top of that.

She doesn’t, however, question if she should leave. That, she’s already made up her mind on. Mostly.

Because she can’t stay. She simply can’t. She can’t allow Leon to sign his life away. This is the last chance she has to make things right, and to make things right she has to leave.

Sure, the last year and a half with him has been… magical. But it’s time for the fairytale to end now. No more Leon, no more friends over for dinner, no more playing with Rose. The time has come for Ada to go back to what she’s good at. What she was made for.

The bag is packed. It’s slung over her shoulder. Stuffed to the brim with clothes she’ll end up burning, passports she’ll end up discarding, and money she’ll be forced to spend.

But it’s time for Ada Wong to cease existing.

She was stupid to think it could actually work.

It’s not just Hayes. She’s not dumb enough to make a decision like this based on the unwanted opinion of one scorned man. But in a way, Hayes placed the last puzzle piece.

The image lies completed before her, and now she has to go.

Still, she can’t decide if she should take the car.

If she does, Leon won’t have a way to get to work tomorrow… They don’t live on the subway line, and the buses take too long. Perhaps he could call a taxi.

That is, if he even makes it out of bed.

Well. He would.

He’s woken up to an empty bed before. It wouldn’t be weird. Perhaps if Ada goes back and leaves a note, she’ll be able to keep up the ruse a little longer. She could write she was called away on a mission, didn’t want to wake him.

Yes.

Maybe.

No.

Because if she goes back inside and writes the note and tiptoes into the bedroom to deliver it, then she’ll undoubtedly stay. She’ll see him, shirtless, starfished and sweating in the July heat, and she’ll throw all her clothes off and curl up next to him.

The wedding is in barely two weeks.

It’s now or never.

Ada has to go.

It’s all she knows how to do.

She can pretend she’s capable of changing. That she can one day be the kind of person Leon wants. But pretending won’t just make it true.

She decides to take the car.

When Leon wakes up and realises she’s gone, the car won’t matter anyway.

The duffle is tossed into the back, and then she slides into the passenger seat. With a strange empty ache in her heart, she adjusts the seat to fit her, erasing her last trace of Leon (not quite true; the necklace still adorns her neck, she’s still wearing the engagement ring), then puts the key in the ignition.

He will be devastated. His life will be ruined.

Ada’s fingers wrap around the leather of the steering wheel.

A sense of out of place déjà vu washes over her.

Ada stares at him.

Then at the blood soaking through his side.

Then back at him again.

And suddenly she’s furious. She’s beyond angry. Her blood boils, burning the inside of her skin. She wants to claw at it and rip it all off. She’s so angry she hurts.

But not at Leon.

At herself.

Because she did this.

Every appearance. Every disappearance. Every moment she let him believe maybe someday while still keeping one foot out the door.

Ada spent years telling herself distance protected him, but now she’s looking at what almost sixteen years of stolen moments and lofty promises created.

A broken man.

It’s not just her, she knows that. Leon has seen and done more than people could ever imagine. He’s lost too much, too many. She teases him for working for the government, but he didn’t choose that. He probably hasn’t made a choice since Raccoon City. Since he was twenty-one. But he’s—God, he’s thirty-seven now.

They’re thirty-seven. They’re aged, in the way that a screwdriver gets a little rusty and the rubber handle starts flaking. A good tool, still useful, even if bits have been chipped off.

Leon sways, taking half a step backwards. Ada moves forward instantly and catches his arm before he can steady himself properly. For one brief second, he goes rigid beneath her touch, but then he melts. It’s not on purpose—he’s too closed off for that.

It’s just pure exhausted instinct. She knows it well enough to see it.

Some part of him has been waiting months for someone to hold him upright.

But she has to go.

Right?

She can’t stay.

She can’t let Leon tie himself to her.

Why not? a small voice whispers. It sounds a lot like Chris. Like Sophie and Nari, too. Like Leon. Why can’t you stay, Ada?

It’s not right.

Leon deserves to be someone good like him. Someone honourable. Someone strong, who never gives up no matter how impossible the odds are. Someone who does the right thing, even when it means going against the mission.

Someone like Claire, maybe. Or Jill for that matter. They’re both taken, obviously. By each other. And they make sense together, in a way Ada and Leon never could.

Ada knows, of course, what Wesker did to Jill. That he stole years of her life. That he used her body and stole her mind from her. That he made her hurt people she would have died for.

Ada knows, from Claire and Leon and Jill herself, that when Chris finally brought her back, there had still been guilt enough to drown in. Unwarranted guilt. Because Jill did not choose Wesker, and so she deserved the care she received when she returned. Claire waiting for her to learn to live inside her own skin again, sitting beside hospital beds, weathering nightmares—Jill earned that.

Because Jill was a victim.

Ada, however, chose to keep working for men like him long after she was old enough to know better.

She chose Wesker.

She chose Simmons.

She chose every mission, every exchange, every virus carried from one country to another.

No psychologist or lawyer or well-meaning friend has ever managed to convince her otherwise, because there comes a point, doesn’t there? A point where a frightened little girl grows into a grown woman and responsibility becomes her own.

Jill deserved Claire’s patience. Ada is no Jill.

Leon deserves someone who only needs healing. Not forgiving.

So Ada must go.

She has to.

She has to.

She has to.

So why can’t she?

Why can’t she start the fucking car and just leave?

Time is running out. The window of opportunity is shrinking, shrinking, shrinking.

She already dallied too long inside. Spent too long watching Leon’s chest rise and fall in peaceful sleep.

It’s almost four A.M.

She has to go.

A guttural sob rips through the silence in the car, and it takes Ada a moment to realise that it came from her.

She slaps a hand over her mouth and finds her face already soaked with tears.

When did she start crying? She doesn’t know. It doesn’t matter.

Why can’t she just leave?

The tears are choking her now, making it harder to breathe. Harder to see, too.

She can’t possibly drive like this.

Can’t possibly leave.

But she has to.

She must.

The thought plays on a loop.

Leave, Ada. Go. Go. Go. Go.

I think we both know this is where we go our separate ways.

So go!

Sobbing, hands trembling, Ada turns the key in the ignition. The car rumbles to life around her; lights flash, the AC starts. What she’s interested in is the radio, a distraction. She needs a clear mind so she can start the car and drive. So she can go.

The station is set to something Leon likes. Talk radio. A smooth voice that, for some reason, makes Ada pause. She lets out a few shuddering sobs and uses the heel of her hand to wipe at her eyes. She’s forty-five. She’s been through much worse. She’s left before. Why is she crying like this?

“—records indicate prolonged isolation, systematic coercive control, physical abuse, and military- style training,” the man on the radio says.

A woman hums. “And Professor Julia Murray, who specialises in this kind of, um, childhood trauma has spoken, like, extensively about how this case is consistent with trafficking.”

“Right,” the man says.

“There’s this section from her latest book that I really thought says a lot. I’m just gonna read it in full, I think.” The woman laughs a little. “So, Murray writes: When people hear the word ‘trafficking’, they often imagine movement across borders. But trafficking is really about exploitation. Once she arrived in the United States, every aspect of her existence was controlled. Her education. Her relationships. Her identity. Even her name.”

Ada’s breath hitches.

She doesn’t understand. The cruelty, what is it for? Can it ever end? What do people gain from harming children?

She doesn’t understand anything, really. That’s how she feels. She doesn’t understand why she’s there—in the car, with Leon, in America. None of it is right. How it should be.

The woman continues, “She never attended school, never formed lasting friendships, never developed outside the control of the man responsible for her abduction.”

The man makes a noise. “It’s… I mean, I can’t swear—” he laughs “—but it’s effing messed up.”

“It is,” the woman agrees.

“It is,” Ada echoes.

Her own voice startles her a little.

She sounds… tired. Old. Beaten.

She feels beaten.

How many children out there fall victim to men like Wesninski? How many children suffer under the thumbs of men who value power above everything. Children like Sherry. Like Rose, too. It feels futile to even try to quell the tears now. They just keep coming.

She lets herself hurt instead. Perhaps she’s earned that much.

She hurts for the child they’re talking about. She hurts for Sherry and Rose and Leon.

“And I mean, the Ada Wong we’ve seen in public… I think a lot of people find it difficult to put these two people together into one,” the man says.

“I don’t!” the woman says.

“Neither do I! Neither does Murray, obviously. She’s very—”

The words fade into the background.

Ada stares at the radio.

Is that her?

The girl?

Is that Ada?

The hosts continue talking, but the words no longer land in complete sentences. They arrive as fragments, disconnected from one another. Nonsensical.

“The remarkable thing,” the woman on the radio says, “is that when you read the actual case files, she just... sounds like a little girl.”

“Which I don’t think people expected. Right? We were painted such a clear picture of her in the media. The terrorist. The spy. The femme fatale.”

The woman hums in agreement. “Uhuh. Yeah. But she was just a girl. Like, literally, a fu—sorry, hah. A baby. A literal baby! When Nathan Wesninski took her to the States.”

Ada grabs onto the steering wheel. She puts the car in reverse. Her foot inches towards the gas pedal.

Go!

Drive!

Go!

The radio program has served its purpose. The tears are gone. She’s too stunned to cry more, honestly.

So she should drive.

Go.

Leave.

“A frightened child,” the woman continues, softer now. “Someone’s baby.”

The man chuckles. “Dany, are you crying?”

The woman—Dany—laughs wetly. “Yes!” she says. “It’s sad, Carl! It’s sad. I’m sad.”

Ada lets her head fall forward, pressing her forehead against the steering wheel.

The car hums, the sound of the rumbling engine taunts her.

But she’s frozen. She can’t push down the gas pedal, can’t reverse. Can’t go.

Thoughts swarm like a roadblock, keeping her fixed in place and time. She sees now the child they’re talking about. Someone small enough that her feet don’t touch the floor when she sits in a chair. A child whose clothes are a little too big and who still counts on her fingers.

Ada’s chest aches. Her breaths come in stuttered gasps, frantic things that barely give her enough oxygen.

She wants to reach into the radio and pull the child out—the child Dany and Carl are talking about.

She wants to tell her it wasn’t her fault. She wants to tell her she shouldn’t have had to become useful just to survive. She wants—

The thought dies.

Because there is no child inside the radio.

There is only her. Ada.

Forty-five years old. Sitting in a parking space beneath the apartment building she shares with the man she’s about to leave. The man she—

Dany laughs again and seems to wipe her tears away. “Wow. Oh my God. Sorry. Uh. There’s actually something Professor Murray says later in the chapter that I highlighted, too.” Paper rustles. “Okay, so, she writes: Perhaps the greatest tragedy of prolonged childhood abuse is that survivors often become extraordinarily compassionate towards other victims while remaining incapable of extending that same compassion to themselves. They can recognise a stolen childhood from miles away, provided it belongs to someone else.”

Someone else.

The violent compassion that cropped up had vanished the moment she’d realised the child was her.

Why?

Go. Leave. Run. Run. Run.

If Sherry had told her this story, Ada would never have blamed her.

If it had been Rosemary—God, if somebody had taken Rose—Ada would have crossed oceans to bring her home, and she would never have expected that little girl to apologise for what had been done to her.

Sophie.

Nari.

Chris.

Leon.

Every single one of them had spent years trying to convince Ada that there had been a child who needed saving. A child who needed to be loved and to be healed. A little girl who needed to relearn trust. But it’s easy to dismiss words from people with bleeding hearts and bias.

These people on the radio do not know Ada, yet the woman is crying for her.

Someone’s baby.

Ada was someone’s baby once. When she was Xiao Yu.

But there’d be no Xiao Yu without Ada; there’d be no Ada without Xiao Yu. They’re one and the same.

Maybe one can’t simply split a person into two and ignore one of the halves.

Maybe Ada Wong is the little girl who was stolen from her home just as much as she is the person who did all those things.

Maybe Ada Wong is, in fact, the last person to come to this conclusion.

But so what? Does that mean she’s suddenly earned… staying? That she’s earned Leon?

She lifts her head, just slightly. Just enough that she can stare at the engagement ring. The stones, set in the shape of a butterfly, catches the fluorescent lights overhead, throwing tiny shards of light across the dashboard.

“I want you to marry me.”

“What?”

“I want you to marry me.”

“I can’t.”

“Says who?”

“I do.”

“That’s typically what one says at the altar.”

“You’re funny.”

“Great quality in a husband.”

“You know I can’t, Leon.”

“But do you want to?”

“I can’t. That’s what matters. Ada Wong doesn’t exist. I do not exist. I’m not real, Leon.”

She twists the ring around her finger.

Ada Wong does exist now. And so she can admit to herself how badly she wanted to say yes to him right then and there in the hotel lobby. How… happy it made her to see him smiling, excited. It hadn’t mattered that much that she’d said no. He’d expected it, ‘cause over the years, she’d made him always expect the worst. That she’d say no, that she’d leave. Not because she wanted to, because she had to.

Because she didn’t know that wanting something was a reason to have it. Just a month ago, she told Leon she knew differently now.

So why is she leaving?

It’s easy, her mind supplies. It’s what I know.

It would be easy—in the sense that the motion required to shed the last pieces of Leon—the ring, the necklace—aren’t bonebreaking. She should leave them now, really. Toss them out the window of the car and leave them for Leon to find come morning.

It’s kinder.

The thought lingers for several seconds before another follows it. Kinder for whom? For Leon? No. It would kill him. Leaving has never protected Leon in any way. It has only ever hurt him.

The realisation settles over her with unbearable slowness. It claws its way through her brain with familiar talons, thick with blood and generous with pain. She can see them so clearly—Wesninski’s hands. The red under his nails, in his cuticles. The dirt in the lines of his palms. She feels, still, every knuckle and callous digging into her skin. He used to slap her around a lot. When she was young. A slap was enough to send her staggering. To shut her up. But it wasn’t a punch. It didn’t make her bleed or disorient her to the point she couldn’t complete her drills.

Ada’s hand wraps around the pendant hanging from her neck. Another butterfly. Leon knows she likes them. He buys her things, all the time, with butterflies on them. Cups, trays, shirts, pictures, books. Anything and everything.

Her thumb runs over the warm metal of the butterfly’s wings.

They’re pretty insects. So fragile, so short-lived. Two different lives, though one half can’t be ignored. Without the larva, there’s no butterfly. Without the butterfly, there’s no larva. They live two lives, yet neither can exist without the other.

People speak of transformation as though the caterpillar disappears. As though the butterfly simply emerges one morning, entirely new. But that isn’t true. The butterfly carries the caterpillar inside it. Every wing beat owes itself to the creature that crawled before it.

One half cannot exist without the other.

Without Xiao Yu, there is no Ada Wong.

Without Ada Wong, there is no Xiao Yu.

She has spent decades trying to separate them. One deserved grief. The other deserved blame. One had been stolen. The other had chosen.

But perhaps that has never been possible. The little girl did not vanish the day Nathan Wesninski renamed her. She grew. She adapted. She survived in the only way she knew how, until surviving became so familiar that Ada mistook it for living.

And now, sitting in the car with one hand around the butterfly at her throat, Ada wonders whether she has been wrong in trying to kill one half of herself. Perhaps healing isn’t choosing between Xiao Yu and Ada Wong. Perhaps healing is finally allowing them to become the same person. Because if the child deserved to be rescued, then the woman she became deserves the chance to live.

Leon has never loved one without the other. He’s never cared one bit about what she’s done. Perhaps because he’s been able to see beyond the larva and the pupa to see the butterfly lying dormant, waiting to emerge. Maybe he has loved the larva, the pupa and the butterfly all the same.

She kills the engine. Leaves the duffle in the car.

It should just be discarded anyway.

Her boots echo on the concrete as she crosses the desolate parking garage, heart beating like thunder in her chest.

If Ada Wong really is that child, then doesn’t she owe it to her—herself—to give her some form of happiness? Will she let Wesninski steal more from Xiao Yu? Isn’t her parents, her home, her future enough?

She finds Leon asleep soundly, taking up all too much space, barely covered up and sweating.

Just as she’d pictured him.

Just as she loves him.

She has to stay.

She can’t go.

For Leon, who almost died once because she thought distance was the way to solve everything.

For Xiao Yu, who had all her loved ones taken from her.

For Ada, who has damn well fucking earned this.

She has to stay.

She slips out of her clothes, sweated through in her panic, and crawls into bed.

Leon stirs a little, clumsy hands pawing through the air for her.

“Baby?” he slurs.

Ada curls into his side, and his arms come around her. “I’m here.”

He hums. Draws her in tighter. On a sigh, he mumbles, “Yeah.”

“I’m not leaving.”

“Hope not,” he says. “The wedding.”

His words are tired, full of sleep and thick vowels. Ada doubts he’ll remember this conversation in the morning.
